--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal dedication of the work. It follows the verso of the title page, which contained library marks and ghosting from the printing process. The translator, Giovanni Bernardo Feliciano, is addressing his patron, Alvise Michele.</meta>

<page-type>dedication</page-type>
<sig>a ii</sig>
<meta>catchword: to us (original: "nobis")</meta>

# TO ALVISE MICHELE
## GIOVANNI BERNARDO
### FELICIANO SENDS GREETINGS <note>original: "S." is an abbreviation for "Salutem," a standard Latin greeting wishing the recipient health and prosperity.</note>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'Q' depicting a scholar wearing a cap, seated at a desk and reading a book. The background shows bookshelves and various academic objects.</image-desc>

How much effort the ancients always devoted to regulating and shaping human conduct <note>original: "moribus," referring to ethics, character, and social habits</note> can be clear to any person who considers the ancient method of philosophizing. Indeed, it is evident that all the schools of the philosophers were held more for the purpose of **establishing a way of life** than for the sake of mere **contemplation and theoretical knowledge**. This is shown both by the ancient discipline of **Pythagoras** and the later instruction of **Socrates**: from whose discussions every principle of living civilly and morally emerged. Many others, but especially those two splendid lights of wisdom, **Plato** and **Xenophon**, handed these principles down to posterity most seriously and elegantly in their literary monuments. For since man has an innate natural desire not so much for merely *finding* happiness <note>original: "beatitudinem," often translated as "blessedness" or the highest form of human flourishing</note> as for actually *attaining* it, the philosophers did not think it sufficient to have labored in defining what happiness is, unless they also devised a method by which we could actually reach it. Just as the investigation of the nature of things [provides] a path to finding the first cause of all, and the first author of all, God...

<summary>This page contains the opening of Giovanni Bernardo Feliciano's dedication to Alvise Michele, arguing that ancient philosophy was primarily a practical guide for moral living rather than just abstract study. He cites Pythagoras, Socrates, Plato, and Xenophon as the primary architects of this lifestyle-based wisdom.</summary>

<keywords>dedication, Alvise Michele, ethics, moral philosophy, Pythagoras, Socrates, Plato, Xenophon, happiness, beatitude, purpose of philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Alvise Michele. It completes the sentence started on the previous page regarding the relationship between theoretical truth and practical virtue.</meta>

<page-type>dedication</page-type>
<sig>ii s</sig>
<meta>catchword: also (original: "etiam")</meta>
<meta>bleed-through from reverse page</meta>

...fortifies us: so the **integrity** and **purity** of life, which we shape in ourselves by imitating the goodness of God, makes us fit to possess Him. For the supreme power of goodness and uprightness <note>original: "probitas," referring to moral excellence and honesty</note> can easily be seen from this: that no action and no speech has any authority, even among the wicked, unless some uprightness, or at least some appearance of uprightness, lies beneath it. For in truth, whatever is not established and supported by the solidity of uprightness—no matter how much talent and artifice it is composed with—it is necessary that it eventually appear to everyone as it truly is: vain and ridiculous, and thus be despised.

For this reason, most illustrious **Alvise**, although you excel in the nobility of your family, your wealth, and your talent, you have nevertheless decided that your life must be conducted so moderately and holily that you have embraced, in a wonderful way, that part of philosophy which regards **ethics** <term>mores: the branch of philosophy concerning character, customs, and social conduct</term>, which you have been accustomed to call the **soul of philosophy**. This you do as becomes one who is truly good, and "a sapling from Zeus, fashioned entirely for truth," <note>original: "πᾶν ἐπ’ ἀλαθείᾳ πεπλασμένον ἐκ Διὸς ἔρνος," a phrase attributed to the Greek poet Pindar</note> as the poet says. This is what you display through the very tranquil way of life you lead, and through those most noble thoughts with which you daily keep your mind elevated above the disturbances of the world and the filth <note>original: "colluvie," literally a collection of washings or dregs; used here to describe the corrupting influence of the unrefined masses</note> of the common crowd.

Therefore, since those writers have also greatly pleased you who did not only investigate the truth with subtle and sharp disputations, but...

<summary>Feliciano praises Alvise Michele's commitment to ethical living, arguing that moral integrity is the "soul of philosophy" and essential for true human authority and divine imitation.</summary>
<keywords>Alvise Michele, ethics, moral integrity, Pindar, soul of philosophy, virtue, goodness</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Alvise Michele. The author is responding to Michele's request to translate ancient philosophical texts that promote moral purification and the "likeness to God."</meta>

<page-type>preface</page-type>
<sig>a iii</sig>
<meta>catchword: of living beings (original: "lium", the end of "animalium")</meta>

...also at the same time might so instruct and purify the life of man that they might raise our minds toward that **likeness to God as far as possible** <note>original Greek: "τὴν ὁμοίωσιν τοῦ θεοῦ κατὰ τὸ δυνατόν"</note>, as **Plato** desired. You repeatedly encouraged and even, by your own right, imposed upon and commanded me that if I could find anything of this sort among the ancient authors, I should rescue it from the darkness and take care that it might go forth into the light even in Latin, so that it might be shared with more people.

For my part, although I was distracted by other occupations and deterred by the awareness of my own modest **learning** <term>eruditio: comprehensive knowledge and cultivation through study</term>, I nevertheless decided I must not fail to obey your commands, at least in some part—especially since I hold the one who gave the command in the greatest respect and veneration, and I saw that the things commanded were of such a nature that to be engaged in their investigation could not fail to be most useful.

Therefore, having undertaken the task, I sought out several such Greek authors; and I wished to begin this edition with this work of **Porphyry**, thinking that its reading would not be unpleasant to many, both because of the author’s name and because of the subject at hand. For **Porphyry** is considered so famous in both **Platonic** and **Aristotelian** philosophy that he can easily attract students of philosophy by his name alone. The work itself, moreover, is filled with various and manifold learning; for it treats of **abstinence from eating [the flesh] of living beings**...

<summary>The translator explains that he has followed Alvise Michele's request to translate ancient Greek texts into Latin to reach a wider audience, selecting Porphyry's work on vegetarianism due to the author's high reputation in Platonic and Aristotelian circles.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Plato, Aristotle, Likeness to God, translation, dedication, abstinence from meat, Greek philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedication to Alvise Michele. It follows the discussion of Porphyry’s work on abstinence, specifically focusing on the spiritual and philosophical motivations behind the text.</meta>

<page-type>preface</page-type>
<sig>a iii</sig>

...[living] beings, he treats it not so much in a **Pythagorean** <note>Referring to the followers of Pythagoras, who were famous for their vegetarianism and belief in the transmigration of souls</note> manner, as in a **Platonic** and, finally, a **theological** way. For although he pursues many points concerning the injustice done to animals and the power of unclean **demons** <term>daemones: in Neoplatonic thought, these are intermediary spirits that could be influenced by physical substances; here, meat-eating is said to attract lower, "impure" spirits</term> (which is secretly attracted by such bloody feasts), based on the decrees of the **Pythagorean** and **Platonic** schools and several others of this sort, all these things nevertheless point toward one purpose. As he himself also seems to interpret it, by diminishing our nourishment, the bond of the **soul**—which is the body—is thinned and weakened. In this way, the mind, released from the disturbances of the emotions, is able to ascend more freely to contemplate and enjoy divine things. Furthermore, by setting aside more luxurious feasts, a more easily obtained diet does not distract our attention from more serious matters, and every occasion for gluttony is cut away. This serves no other purpose than to demonstrate that the principle of **fasting** and **abstinence** is validated not only by divine law but also by natural law. To make everything more clearly understood, we thought it would be worthwhile to also translate the "Life of the Author" written by **Eunapius** <note>Eunapius (c. 347 – after 414 AD) was a Greek historian whose "Lives of the Philosophers" is a primary source for the biographies of Neoplatonists like Porphyry</note> and place it before the work itself.

->Farewell.<-

<summary>The author argues that Porphyry's vegetarianism is a tool for spiritual purification, intended to weaken the body's hold on the soul so the mind can focus on the divine. The preface concludes by introducing a biographical supplement by Eunapius to provide context on Porphyry's life.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, Pythagoras, Plato, vegetarianism, abstinence, demons, fasting, soul, Eunapius, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the editor's preface (the conclusion of the dedication to Alvise Michele) into the biographical section. It begins the Life of Porphyry as recorded by the 4th-century historian Eunapius.</meta>

# THE LIFE OF PORPHYRY
## FROM EUNAPIUS.

<image-desc>A decorative historiated initial 'P' featuring a seated scholar or saint holding an open book.</image-desc>

Porphyry was a Tyrian <note>Tyre was a major city-state in ancient Phoenicia, located in present-day Lebanon</note> by birth; Tyre itself was the leading city of the ancient Phoenicians. He was born to parents of no small reputation. Once he had obtained a liberal education, he made such progress that, having become a student of **Longinus** <note>Cassius Longinus (c. 213–273 AD), a renowned rhetorician and Neoplatonist philosopher known as a "living library"</note>, he soon brought honor to his teacher. Indeed, Longinus, living at that time, was considered a kind of "living library" and a "walking museum." He had devoted his mind to the critical study of ancient writings, just as many others had before him, and especially **Dionysius of Caria** <note>Likely referring to a celebrated Greek rhetorician or critic from the region of Caria</note>, who was the most famous of them all. 

Now, while Porphyry was originally called **Malchus** <note>original: "Malchus"; a Latinized version of a Semitic name (like the Hebrew *Melek* or Arabic *Malik*) meaning "king"</note> in the native language of the Syrians—which signifies a "king" in the idiom of that people—his name was later changed to **Porphyry** <note>original: "Porphyrius"; derived from the Greek *porphura*, referring to the imperial purple dye</note> by Longinus, a title referencing the distinctive color of royal garments. Under Longinus’s discipline, he applied himself most excellently to the study of literature. He reached the highest peak of grammar—no less than his teacher—and of rhetoric, although he was not quite so naturally inclined toward the latter. He was also highly cultivated in every branch of **philosophy** <term>philosophia: here referring to the broad Neoplatonic curriculum including logic, ethics, and metaphysics</term>. For Longinus was of all...

<summary>This page introduces the biography of the philosopher Porphyry, detailing his noble Phoenician origins and his early education under the famous scholar Longinus, who renamed him from "Malchus" to "Porphyry."</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Eunapius, Tyre, Phoenicia, Longinus, Malchus, etymology, education, rhetoric, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Porphyry, detailing his transition from the tutelage of the rhetorician Longinus to the mystical philosophy of Plotinus in Rome, and his subsequent psychological crisis in Sicily.</meta>

<sig>i u p iiii</sig>
<meta>catchword: uana</meta>

inclined to it <note>Continuing from the previous page, this refers to Porphyry's natural aptitude for rhetoric compared to his teacher</note>, yet among those who flourished at that time, Longinus was easily the foremost. 
A great wealth of his books survives, and they are held in such 
admiration that if anyone found fault with something in the ancient 
writers, he did not think he should settle on his own opinion until 
the judgment of Longinus had also been obtained. Thus, when 
Porphyry had been educated in this manner in his primary 
studies, and had become conspicuous to all, he was seized by a great 
desire to see Rome, so that he might take possession of the city of 
wisdom. As soon as he arrived there, he entered into the company of 
the divine **Plotinus** <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was the founding figure of Neoplatonism, focusing on the ascent of the soul to "the One"</note>; forgetting all others, he devoted himself entirely 
to him. Applying himself with insatiable greed to that discipline, 
he spent some time drawing from those divine disputations as if 
from a sacred spring. Later, however, overcome by the 
magnitude of his contemplations, he began to pursue his own 
body with hatred and to be distressed that he was a human 
being. For this reason, when he had sailed to Sicily and 
crossed the Strait of Charybdis <note>The Strait of Messina, which separates Italy and Sicily; in mythology, it was the home of the whirlpool monster Charybdis</note>, where **Ulysses** <note>The Roman name for Odysseus, the hero of Homer's Odyssey</note> 
is also said to have traveled, he could not bear to see a city 
nor hear the voice of men; so completely had he cast off every 
sense of pain and pleasure. And so, having set out on a 
steady journey toward **Lilybaeum** <note>Modern-day Marsala, a strategic port on the western tip of Sicily</note>, which is one of the three promontories of Sicily 
and faces toward Libya, he cast himself down there; groaning 
and enduring, he lay there refusing all food, and, as the 
poet says, "avoiding the tracks of men" <note>original: "hominum vestigia vitans"; an allusion to Homer’s Iliad (6.202) describing the melancholy of the hero Bellerophon</note>. But it did not escape...

<summary>Porphyry moves to Rome to study under the great philosopher Plotinus, but his intense metaphysical studies lead him into a deep depression and a rejection of his physical existence, resulting in a self-imposed exile and hunger strike in Sicily.</summary>
<keywords>Longinus, Rome, Plotinus, Neoplatonism, Sicily, asceticism, Charybdis, Lilybaeum, mental health in antiquity</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Porphyry, specifically his time in Sicily where he suffered a suicidal depression. It describes how his teacher, Plotinus, intervened to save him and the subsequent relationship between their two styles of philosophy.</meta>

...vain exploration; in these matters, the great **Plotinus** <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD), the founder of Neoplatonism, who taught that the soul must ascend back to the "One"</note> was attentive. For he followed immediately, whether because he had a premonition of what was happening, or because he was searching for the young man who had fled; he found him lying there <note>Porphyry had withdrawn to Sicily to starve himself to death</note>. He devised words for him that would both recall the soul which was just then about to fly away from the body, and strengthen the body to hold it. And so, having now recovered his spirit, he arose. 

Plotinus later recorded the words he had spoken in a book, among those that are now extant. But since philosophers used to hide their secrets behind an obscurity of speech—not unlike the way poets use fables—**Porphyry**, who approved of the remedy of clarity and had tasted its benefits by experience, brought these ideas into the light by illustrating them with his own commentaries. 

Having then returned to Rome, Porphyry devoted himself to the study of oratory, even appearing in public to display his skills. Nevertheless, every assembly and every gathering attributed Porphyry’s glory to Plotinus. For Plotinus, because of the heavenly power of his soul and his abstruse, enigmatic way of speaking, seemed profound and difficult to grasp. 

Porphyry, however, was like a kind of **Homeric chain** <note>original: "Homerica catena." This refers to the Golden Chain in the Iliad that binds heaven to earth; in this context, Porphyry acts as the link that brings "heavenly" philosophy down to human understanding</note> let down to men; with his varied erudition and knowledge, he proclaimed all things with ease and transparency. And he himself says—though perhaps he had written these things when he was still quite young—that he had encountered no public oracle. In the same book, he discusses many other matters, such as what care should be... <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "benda" suggests the Latin "habenda," meaning "to be had" or "to be maintained"</note>

<summary>This page describes how Plotinus rescued Porphyry from a suicide attempt in Sicily and contrasts their intellectual styles: Plotinus as the obscure, divine mystic and Porphyry as the brilliant "chain" who made those difficult ideas accessible to others.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Porphyry, Neoplatonism, Homeric Chain, Sicily, Philosophy, Oratory, Commentaries</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Porphyry, focusing on his reputation among his peers and his immense range of scholarship after returning to Rome.</meta>

...should be regarded; and that he had put to flight and cast out a certain demonic nature from a bathhouse, which the locals called **Causathan** <note>This refers to an anecdote where Porphyry or his circle purportedly performed an exorcism of a local spirit</note>. 

His fellow students, as he himself writes, were quite distinguished: **Origen** <note>Origen the Pagan (3rd century AD), a Neoplatonist student of Ammonius Saccas, not to be confused with the Christian Church Father</note>, **Amelius**, and **Aquilinus**. Their writings are still extant, yet no beauty is perceived in them, even if their doctrines and opinions are sound and they adhere strictly to their words. **Porphyry**, however, praises the eloquence and gravity of these men, though he himself surpasses them all in grace. He alone truly reveals and proclaims his teacher’s work, having neglected no branch of learning. 

Indeed, anyone might wonder and find it difficult to decide what he most preferred to study: whether it was the art of **rhetoric** <term>oratory: the skill of public speaking and persuasion</term>, or those things which pertain to the exact inquiry of **grammar** <note>In the ancient world, "grammar" involved the deep philological study and interpretation of classical texts</note>. It remains ambiguous whether he devoted his primary study to arithmetic, geometry, music, or the parts of philosophy—both those concerning reason <note>original: "rationis" — referring to logic and theoretical philosophy</note> and those concerning administration <note>original: "administrationis" — referring to ethics and political philosophy</note>. 

As for his works on natural and divine contemplation, they are so sublime that they seem more appropriate for sacred rites and religious mysteries than for common reading. Thus, that man emerged as a person perfectly fashioned for every virtue. At one moment, one might admire his words more than his doctrines; at another, his decrees and insights more than his sheer power of speech. His wife...

<summary>This page highlights Porphyry's intellectual superiority over his fellow students and his mastery of both the liberal arts and the "sublime" mysteries of Neoplatonic philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Plotinus, Origen the Pagan, Amelius, Causathan, rhetoric, grammar, quadrivium, Neoplatonism, polymath</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Porphyry, picking up from the discussion of his vast learning and transitioning into his personal life and historical context.</meta>

...however, he seems to have married. For a book is circulated addressed to his wife, **Marcella**. Although she was already a mother of five children, he states that he married her not to have more children with her, but so that the children his friend had already fathered with her might be raised and educated in the best possible way. He is said to have lived to a very advanced age and passed away <note>original: "commutasse uitam" — literally 'exchanged life', a common euphemism for death</note> in **Rome**. During this period, there were also those who taught **rhetoric** <term>the art of public speaking and persuasion</term> in **Athens**, such as **Paulus** and **Andromachus** from **Syria**. This era extended through the reigns of **Galen** and **Claudius** <note>This refers to Emperor Claudius Gothicus (reigned 268–270 AD)</note>, as well as **Tacitus** and **Aurelian**. During their time, **Dexippus** also flourished; he was a renowned historian who wrote a chronicle of his times and was a man exceptionally well-versed in all branches of learning and the skills of **logic**.

# HIS LIFE AND A LIST OF HIS WORKS
## FROM THE SUIDAS <note>The *Suda* (historically referred to as the Suidas) is a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia that preserves a wealth of ancient biographical and historical information.</note>

### 

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'P' depicting a seated figure, likely a philosopher, in front of a classical building with columns, set within a square border decorated with foliage.</image-desc>
**Porphyry**, the philosopher from the Phoenician city of **Tyre**, was called "King" <note>original: "rex" — Porphyry's birth name was Malchus, which means 'king' in Semitic languages</note> in his own native language. He was a disciple of **Amelius** (who was himself a student of **Plotinus**), the teacher of **Iamblichus**, and a student of **Longinus** the critic. He wrote a vast number of books...

<summary>This page concludes a biographical sketch of Porphyry’s later life in Rome, including his marriage to Marcella, before beginning a new biographical entry sourced from the Byzantine Suda encyclopedia.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Marcella, Rome, Athens, Rhetoric, Dexippus, Suda, Neoplatonism, Biography</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the works of Porphyry, picking up from the description of his education and intellectual output begun on the previous page.</meta>

...philosophical, as well as rhetorical and grammatical works he wrote: one book *On Divine Names*; two *On Principles*; six *On Matter*; five *On the Soul* addressed to **Boethus**; four *On Abstinence from Eating Living Creatures* <note>original: "De abstinentia ab esu animalium" — Porphyry's famous treatise advocating for vegetarianism based on Neoplatonic principles</note>; four *On that which was said by the wise man, "Know Thyself"*; a work *On Incorporeal Realities*; seven books arguing *That the school of Plato and Aristotle is one and the same*; four *On the history of Julian the Chaldean philosopher*; seven books *On Aristotle's Categories* addressed to **Gedalius**; one on those same *Categories* via question and answer; a work *On the Philosophy of Homer*; a treatise showing *That according to the opinion of Aristotle, the soul is an entelechy* <note>original: ἐντελέχεια (entelecheia) — a philosophical term meaning 'actualization' or 'full realization'</note>, that is, it is a perfection; five books of *Philosophical History*; a work *On Genus, Species, Difference, Property, and Accident* <term>This work is known as the "Isagoge," which became the standard introductory textbook for logic throughout the Middle Ages</term>; *On the Sources of the Nile according to the opinion of Pindar*; ten books *On the utility that kings can derive from Homer*; seven books of *Miscellaneous Questions*; seven books *On the introduction of Thucydides* addressed to **Aristides**; a work *On the rhetorical art of Minucianus*; and many others, especially astronomical works, among which is an *Introduction to Astronomy* completed in three books, and *Grammatical Questions*. He flourished under the reign of **Aurelian** <note>Roman Emperor from 270–275 AD</note> and lived until the times of **Diocletian** <note>Roman Emperor from 284–305 AD</note>.

<summary>This page provides an extensive bibliography of Porphyry’s writings, covering logic, Homeric scholarship, and psychology, while dating his career to the late 3rd century AD.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Bibliography, Aristotle's Categories, Isagoge, Entelechy, Homeric Philosophy, Aurelian, Diocletian, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the bibliographic catalog of Porphyry's works into the primary text of his most famous treatise. # PORPHYRY ON
## ABSTINENCE FROM THE EATING
### OF ANIMALS
# BOOK ONE.
->Translated by Giovanni Bernardo Feliciano.<- <note>original: "Ioanne Bernardo Feliciano interprete" — Giovanni Bernardo Feliciano (c. 1490–1554) was a Venetian scholar who specialized in translating Greek philosophical and patristic texts into Latin.</note>

---

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut initial 'C' depicting a bearded scholar in academic robes sitting at a desk, writing in a large volume with a quill. He is surrounded by shelves of books and other instruments of study.</image-desc>WHEN I had heard from those who were coming from your region to us, that you, having renounced your abstinence from flesh, had returned again to the eating of meat: at first I did not believe it, taking into account both your temperance <note>original: "temperantiæ" — a core Platonic virtue involving self-restraint and moderation</note> and the piety with which we followed those ancient and God-fearing men who had handed these traditions down to us. But after others, arriving subsequently, confirmed the same thing to me: if I had wished to rebuke you—noting that you had neither, as the proverb says, "fled the evil and found the better," nor, like **Empedocles** <note>A Greek Pre-Socratic philosopher who famously advocated for vegetarianism based on the belief in the transmigration of souls</note>, having lamented your former life, returned to a better one—it seemed to me to be both unrefined and far from the persuasion of reason. But if I had uncovered your errors through the refutation of an argument, and made manifest from what state into what state you had fallen...

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Porphyry's treatise on vegetarianism, written as a letter to a friend who had abandoned his ascetic diet. Porphyry expresses his initial disbelief and sets the stage for a rational, philosophical argument to win his colleague back to a life of abstinence.</summary>

# ON ABSTINENCE
<meta>catchword: against,</meta>

1. Truly, I also thought it was both worthy of our mutual friendship and suitable for those who have dedicated their own life to the truth. For when I consider the cause of this change, I would by no means think it was for the sake of health and strength—as the common and vulgar crowd asserts—because, on the contrary, a diet free from meat <note>original: "uictum à carnibus alienum"</note> contributes both to health and to a moderate endurance of philosophical labors. You yourself used to confess this while living with us, and it is easy to know by experience itself that you were speaking the truth when you affirmed these things. 

Either, then, you have been led by some deception into thinking that whether you eat in this way or that, it makes no difference to wisdom <note>original: "prudentiam" — translating the Greek *phronesis*, which refers to practical wisdom or the intelligence required for a virtuous life</note>; or perhaps it is for some other reason, unknown to me, which could strike a greater terror into you than the impiety of violating your established rule, that you seem to me to have run back to your former sins. For I will never claim that it was on account of a lack of self-control or for the sake of gluttonous greed that you have thus held in contempt the ancestral laws of philosophy which you used to admire. Nor will I say that you are by nature inferior to unlearned and common men of certain nations <note>A common rhetorical device in Neo-Platonism, comparing the philosopher's discipline to the strict religious taboos of "barbarian" cultures like the Egyptians or Jews</note> who, when they have accepted laws contrary to those they previously used, both endure the cutting off of their limbs <note>original: "membrorum præsectiones" — likely a reference to ritual circumcision or the self-mutilation practiced by certain priesthoods, such as the Galli of Cybele</note> and would, if necessity pressed upon them, almost more likely abstain from certain animals with which they were previously filled than from human flesh. Since, however, some have also called to mind arguments which are against...

<header>BOOK I.</header>
<sig>A ii</sig>

...overwhelmed <note>original: "perculsus", the first part of which appeared on the previous page</note> by fear regarding those who abstain, as you were once accustomed to say, it seemed to me not only something to be borne with difficulty, but even a cause for indignation and anger. For you have allowed yourselves to be persuaded by such inept and foul little sophistries <note>original: "captiunculis", referring to deceptive or tricky logical arguments</note>, and have thus permitted yourselves to be deceived and to overturn an ancient practice pleasing to the gods.

Wherefore I have decided that I would be doing something worthwhile if I not only confirm our own positions and show them to be as they are, but also if I collect and refute the arguments of our adversaries. These arguments are far stronger than those you have encountered in their abundance, force, and every other preparation. In doing so, I will demonstrate that the truth is not overcome even by those arguments that seem more serious, much less by foul and trivial trifles.

For against abstinence from living beings <term>animatis: literally "ensouled" beings, referring to animals as creatures possessing a "soul" or life-force</term>—a fact of which you are perhaps unaware—not a few have spoken. These include philosophers from the <term>Peripatetics: followers of Aristotle's school</term>, the <term>Stoics: a school emphasizing reason and indifference to external circumstances</term>, and the <term>Epicureans: followers of Epicurus who sought peace through the absence of pain</term>. For the most part, these thinkers directed their opposition against the philosophy of Pythagoras and Empedocles <note>Pythagoras and Empedocles were the most famous early Greek proponents of the idea that animals have souls and should not be eaten</note>, whom you have strived to emulate until now.

Among these opponents were also orators, including a certain Clodius of Naples, who even wrote a book against those who abstain from meat. From all of these sources, I will bring forward those questions which generally concern the matter and this practice; I will omit the others which are directed specifically against the particular proofs of Empedocles. Those, therefore, who contradict this practice claim immediately that justice is thrown into confusion and that "the immovable is moved" <note>A Greek proverbial expression for upsetting fundamental laws or principles</note> if law...

ii A

...carry [justice] <note>original: "eo usque prouehamus"</note> so far that it is judged to pertain not only to rational beings, but even to irrational ones; so that we might think not only humans and gods have a claim upon us, but even the other brute animals, with whom we have no interaction <note>original: "commercium", referring here to a legal or social contract</note>. Nor should we use some for labor and others for food, judging them to be entirely foreign and devoid of all society, no different than if they were outside the human republic. For whoever wishes to behave toward them just as toward humans—sparing them and doing them no harm—while he assigns to <term>justice: the principle of giving each their due</term> that which it cannot bear, he destroys that which it can; and by the introduction of what is foreign [to justice], he takes away and ruins what is proper to it. 

The result is that either, if we do not spare them, we must necessarily act unjustly; or, if we do spare them and refuse to use them, we will be unable to carry on with our lives. In the end, we would lead the lives of cattle <note>original: "pecudum"</note> ourselves, if we were to repudiate the use of cattle. 

For even if I omit the infinite multitude of <term>Nomads: wandering tribes who did not practice agriculture</term> and <term>Troglodytes: literally "cave-dwellers," a term used by ancient Greeks to describe various remote peoples</term> who recognize no other nourishment but meat—for us, who seem to live gently and humanely, what work of the earth, what work of the sea, what notable art, what ornament of our livelihood would be left to us if we were so affected toward other animals as if they were our relatives, and behaved toward them harmlessly and piously? Truly, there would be no remedy for such poverty, which [destroys] all life and just-

# BOOK II.

A iii

...takes away the very charm <note>original: "gratiam"; completing the sentence from the previous page</note> of life, nor will we be able to provide for ourselves unless we observe the ancient law and definition. For as **Hesiod** <note>An early Greek poet (c. 700 BCE); the quote is from his work *Works and Days*</note> says, when Jupiter had distinguished the natures of things and set each kind of animal apart:

> To the fish, and the race of birds, and the beasts of the field,
> He granted that they might devour one another, since there is no
> Justice among them; but to humans he gave the gift of justice,
> Making them sharers in equity.

Regarding those with whom we have no legal standing <note>original: "commercium," meaning a mutual social or legal contract</note> for acting justly, we have no reason to fear acting "unjustly" toward them. Indeed, those who reject this reasoning leave no other path for justice—not even a narrow one—that a person might walk. For, as we have already stated, to bar our nature (which is not self-sufficient but stands in need of many things) from the assistance of other animals is nothing less than to utterly destroy life, confining it to a state that is difficult, lacking in all tools, and destitute of necessary things.

Furthermore, they say that those first humans did not live in a very fortunate manner; for superstition does not contain itself within the boundaries of "living animals," but eventually breaks even into the realm of plants. For why is he who slaughters an ox or a sheep acting more unjustly than he who cuts down a fir tree or an oak? Especially if, according to the <term>transformation of the soul: the doctrine of metempsychosis or reincarnation</term>, a soul is also implanted within the stocks of plants themselves. And these are the principal arguments which the **Stoics** and **Peripatetics** <note>Followers of Aristotle, known for studying "peripatos" (walkways) of the Lyceum</note> are accustomed to bring—

### ON ABSTINENCE

A iii

...are accustomed <note>original: "sueuerunt"; this completes the previous page's sentence regarding those who have become accustomed to sparing even plants</note>. But the **Epicureans** <note>Followers of Epicurus (341–270 BCE), who taught that justice is not an inherent natural law but a social contract designed to prevent mutual harm</note>, recounting a certain lengthy "genealogy" <note>an account of the historical development or evolution of laws</note>, as it were, say that the ancient lawmakers, when they had looked toward the society of life and the mutual actions of humans among themselves, ordained that the slaughter of a human being was impious. They imposed significant penalties on those who committed such an act. 

For although there is a certain natural kinship among humans toward one another—arising from a similarity of both form and soul—which ensures that such an animal is not killed as readily as the other animals that are permitted to us, they believe the primary reason why this deed was held to be so impious and detestable was that they judged it did not contribute to the overall constitution of life <note>original: "constitutionem," referring to the stable structure of human society</note>. 

For from this origin, those who perceived the **utility** <term>utility (utilitas): the practical benefit or common advantage that serves as the basis for Epicurean justice</term> of this institution had no need for any other cause to restrain them from such a crime. However, those who could not sufficiently grasp the sense of this matter abstained from mutual slaughter because they were deterred by the magnitude of the punishment. 

Both of these, they say, are still seen to happen today. For those who consider the utility of such a decree readily persevere in their duty; whereas those who are unable to perceive it maintain the same behavior only because they are terrified by the threats of the laws. Indeed, some promulgated these threats for the sake of those who were unable to reach a consideration of utility, after the majority had already accepted them. For from the beginning, no law—whether written or unwritten—was established by force,

### TITLES OF BOOK II

...is, from those things which remain until now and are of such a nature that they are capable of being widely distributed among people; but the agreement of those who were to actually use them was also added. For those who introduced such things to the majority of people were influential through the **wisdom** <term>wisdom (prudentia): here referring to the practical foresight or intellectual skill of the early lawmakers</term> of the mind, not by physical strength or by dominion over nations. By this wisdom, it was brought about that some were led to a perception of **utility** <term>utility (utilitas): the practical advantage or common benefit that serves as the basis for Epicurean justice</term>—namely, those who previously held a sense of it without reflection and frequently forgot it—while others were terrified by the magnitude of the penalties.

For no other remedy could be applied to the ignorance of utility than the terror of punishment established by laws; this indeed is the only thing that even now restrains any common person and forbids them from committing, whether publicly or privately, that which is harmful <note>original: "inutile," literally "useless" or "unprofitable," which in this context refers to actions that damage the social contract</note>. For if everyone were able in a similar way both to perceive and to call utility to mind, there would have been no need for laws; instead, of their own accord, people would avoid what is forbidden and do what is commanded. Indeed, the consideration of what is useful and what is harmful would be sufficient both to encourage the flight from evil and the choice of the good.

But the imposition of punishment is directed at those who cannot perceive utility. For these, the looming terror forces them to master the appetites that drag them toward harmful actions, and thus it keeps them to their duty by force. For this reason, the **legislators** <note>original: "legislatores," completing the word from the catchword at the bottom of the page</note> did not wish even involuntary killing to be entirely free of all punishment...

...[legis]lators, so that they might not only give no occasion for others to voluntarily imitate the deeds of those who had acted unwillingly <note>That is, to prevent people from using "accidents" as an excuse for intentional harm.</note>, but also to guard against the occurrence of many such things that were truly involuntary. For this was not beneficial <note>original: "conferebat," meaning it did not contribute to the common good.</note>, for the same reasons that we said it was forbidden for people to voluntarily destroy one another. Therefore, since among involuntary acts, some are committed due to an uncertain cause—of such a kind that cannot be avoided by human nature—while others arise from our **negligence** <term>negligence (negligentia): a failure to exercise the care that a reasonably prudent person would exercise in like circumstances</term> and carelessness, because we do not pay attention to what is important: in order to prevent others from being harmed by our laziness, they did not leave even an involuntary action unpunished. Instead, they restrained the frequency of such sins through the terror of penalties.

And indeed, I believe that the requirement for certain customary **expiations** <term>expiations (expiationibus): ritual cleansings or ceremonies intended to remove the spiritual "stain" of an action</term>—even for killings permitted by law—was instituted by those first lawgivers for no other reason than their desire to keep people as far as possible from voluntary killing. For they needed some form of prohibition from every side, lest people should so recklessly slip into perpetrating things that are not useful. 

For this reason, those who first observed these things did not only establish **fines** <term>fines (mulctas): monetary penalties imposed as punishment for an offense</term>; they also introduced another kind of "irrational terror," declaring that those who had killed a human being in any manner whatsoever <note>Even if the killing was accidental or legally justified, such as in self-defense or war.</note> were contaminated <note>original: "contaminatos," referring to the ancient concept of "miasma" or religious pollution.</note>, unless they purged themselves with certain **atonements** <term>atonements (piaculis): specific sacrifices or rituals performed to appease divine anger or purify a person after a death</term>. # BOOK I
->B<-

...the part of the soul devoid of reason, being educated in various ways, reached an appropriate **gentleness** <term>gentleness (mansuetudo): a state of being tamed or civilized, moving away from wild, predatory impulses</term>; this occurred while those who first civilized nations applied various methods of taming to the irrational impulse of desire. One of these methods was the rule that people should not kill one another without a trial. 

However, regarding other animals, those who first established for us what ought or ought not to be done rightly forbade the killing of none; for in their case, our **utility** <note>original: "utilitas," referring to the practical benefit or survival of the human species</note> arose from the opposite action. For it was not possible for men living together to remain safe unless they attempted to repel the attacks of wild beasts. 

Since, therefore, some among those who were then the most refined <note>original: "elegantissimi," referring to the most cultured or wise leaders of early society</note> remembered that they had abstained from mutual slaughter specifically because it contributed to their safety, they reminded others in these common **societies** <term>societies (societatibus): early organized communities or fellowships bound by mutual needs</term> of how things had turned out. They did this so that by abstaining from harming their own kind, they might preserve the social bond, which provided the greatest help for the individual safety of every person. 

Furthermore, this gathering of men into one place and their abstinence from mutual injury was useful not only for repelling the attacks of animals of a different species, but also for warding off other men who might approach with the intent to do harm. For a time, indeed, those who came together into the same community of necessities abstained from one another for this reason, and <unclear>they</unclear> brought about some use for both of the purposes mentioned above...

<header>ON ABSTINENCE</header>
->B<-
<meta>catchword: of this kind (huiusmodi)</meta>

...they brought. But afterward, as time progressed and **kinship** <term>kinship (cognatio): the bond of blood and family that expanded from immediate households to entire tribes</term> became more widely spread through mutual procreation, the wild animals were driven out and scattered. Consequently, some individuals began to consider more deeply the benefits gained from living together; they no longer relied merely on a wordless memory of past dangers, as they had before. 

From this realization, they attempted to more firmly restrain those who—through a forgetfulness of the past—committed mutual slaughters and thereby weakened **human protection** <note>original: "humanum præsidium," the collective security and defense found in a united society</note>. To achieve this, they established the legal institutions that are still observed among cities and nations today. The general population willingly assented to these laws because they now perceived a greater sense of **utility** <note>original: "utilitatis," referring to the practical benefit, safety, and survival of the community</note> in congregating together. 

In a similar way, it led to a strict distinction: just as everything harmful was killed without any consideration, so everything that was useful for preventing such death was preserved. For this reason, the killing of humans was rightly forbidden, while the killing of other things was not prohibited at all.

Furthermore, it cannot be argued that there are certain animals that the law permits us to kill despite their not harming human nature or our way of life. For there is none among the animals that the law allows to be killed that would not bring harm to humans if allowed to increase in **excessive numbers**; conversely, if kept in moderate numbers, each provides certain uses for human life. ### BOOK I.

...things of this kind, if they remain in **moderation** <term>moderation (mediocritate): the classical ideal of the "golden mean," or a balanced state between two extremes</term>, provide certain benefits for the necessary conduct of our lives; but if they were to fall into great excess, and exceed the number that is appropriate, they would undermine our life. Oxen, for instance, might turn to causing trouble—being endowed by nature with quite strong and robust power for that purpose; or sheep might graze away the very sustenance that springs from the earth for us. 

For this reason, the slaughter of this kind of animal has not been prohibited, so that only that multitude of them might be left which serves a **use** <note>original: "usum," referring to the practical application of the animal for labor or food</note> and which can be easily managed. For it is not the same with these as it is with lions, and wolves, and all others called **wild beasts** <note>original: "feræ"</note>—among which, whether small or large, no multitude can be found that, if left alone, could support our life. The situation is different with oxen, and horses, and sheep, and others of that kind called **domestic** <note>original: "manfueta," literally "tamed by hand"</note>. 

Therefore, we strive to utterly destroy the wild beasts; but for the domestic ones, we suppress their abundance only if it exceeds measure. For the same reason, one must believe that the ancient laws regarding the eating of animals were established. For since some things are held to be unfit for eating, it is clear that the **useful and the useless** <term>utility (utilitas): the core of Epicurean and later Neoplatonic legal philosophy, suggesting that laws are not divine or absolute, but tools for human survival</term> are the underlying cause. 

Wherefore, it is no small folly to assert that everything honorable and just arises from each person’s own opinions regarding what is sanctioned by laws. For the matter does not stand so. But just as in other useful things, as—

<header>ON ABSTINENCE</header>

For instance, in those things which lead to **health**, and in countless others, we are sometimes deceived; thus, in many matters, both public and private, error is accustomed to occur. Indeed, since not everyone pays attention to those **legal institutions** <term>legal institutions (legum institutiones): the established laws and customs that govern a society</term> which are suitable for all, some overlook certain things, thinking they are of no consequence; others hold contrary opinions about the same matters, and judge that things which do not benefit everyone in general are useful everywhere. 

From this it happens that people sometimes decree things that do not suit everyone, provided they have found those things which offer them a profit and serve their common **utility** <term>utility (utilitas): the practical benefit or advantage that a law or action provides to society</term> among themselves. Of this kind are those regulations established regarding the eating and slaughter of animals among many nations according to the specific nature of their region; which certainly ought not to be observed by us, since we do not live in the same place. 

If, therefore, a pact regarding slaughter could have been made between other animals just as it is between humans—so that neither they would kill us, nor we them, indiscriminately—then **justice** <note>original: "ius," here referring to the concept of legal right or justice derived from a social contract</note> would have been extended that far; for it would have been sufficient for our security. But because it was in no way possible for those creatures who are **devoid of reason** <note>original: "expertia rationis," referring to the classical belief that animals lack the capacity for logic and moral reasoning</note> to have a share in laws and justice, no benefit existed in such a plan for obtaining protection from other animals. Instead, security is procured for us only by the freedom to kill them, which we possess even now. 

And these are the things, and others of this kind, which are said by the **Epicureans** <note>Epicureans: followers of the philosopher Epicurus, who argued that justice is a social contract based on mutual utility rather than a divine or natural law</note> regarding this opinion...

# ->ON FREEDOM<-

...are accustomed to be established. It remains for us to add those things which are commonly circulated by others among the common people concerning this matter. They say that those ancient people did not abstain from the eating of animals for the sake of piety, but because they had not yet discovered the use of fire. When they perceived its use, they considered it so venerable and sacred to themselves that they called it **Vesta** <term>Vesta: the Roman goddess of the hearth; the author implies that the discovery of the hearth fire was the foundation of human civilization and social bonding</term>, and from that they became "hearth-sharers" <note>original: *conuestales*, a term derived from 'Vesta' to describe those sharing a home</note> and "tent-mates," and began to feed upon animals. For it is according to nature for man to eat meat, just as it is contrary to nature for him to eat it raw. 

From this it followed that once fire was discovered, they arrived at what suited their nature, as they admitted meat through the application of **cooking** <note>original: *coctione*</note>. For lynxes are raw-meat eaters <note>original: *crudiuori*</note>, and that saying was uttered as a reproach:

> For you would eat Priam raw <note>original: *crudum nanq; ederes Priamum*; a reference to Homer’s *Iliad* (4.35), where the goddess Hera’s rage is described as a hunger to devour the King of Troy raw.</note>

and,

> Tearing raw flesh, he devoured it with a full mouth <note>original: *Crudas dilacerans carnes pleno ore uorabat*</note>

as if, forsooth, the eating of raw flesh is attributed to the impious. But this is truly human:

> the servant set down dishes full of various meats on this side and that.

At first, therefore, they did not feed upon animals because man was not a raw-flesh-eating animal. But once the use of fire was found, he used not only meats cooked by fire, but also many other foodstuffs. For certain tribes who feed only on fish demonstrate that man is not a raw-flesh-eating animal; for some [cook] fish upon rocks where they have gathered the greatest heat from the sun, while others in [the sand]...

<summary>This page presents an early anthropological theory that the transition to meat-eating was a result of the discovery of fire rather than a lapse in piety. It argues that while raw meat is for beasts and the impious, cooked meat is a natural and civilizing food for humans, centered around the sacred hearth of Vesta.</summary>

# ON ABSTINENCE

...flesh they were accustomed to roast. Moreover, the fact that no nation abstains from the eating of animals—a custom held not only by the Greeks but also by the barbarians—manifestly shows that humans are meat-eaters <note>original: *carnivorum*. The author argues that meat-eating is a universal human trait rather than a local quirk.</note>. For he who forbids feeding on them and deems it unjust, will also necessarily assert that it is unjust to kill them or to deprive them of life <note>original: *priuare’q anima*; literally "to deprive of the soul" or "breath of life."</note>.

But against wild beasts, a war is innate and just for us: especially since some attack humans of their own accord, such as wolves and lions; others do so not of their own accord, such as vipers, which bite only if they are stepped on; and while some attack humans, others attack our crops. For all these reasons, we are accustomed to take vengeance on these beasts, and we kill them—whether they have provoked us or not—so that no harm may come to us from them. For there is no one who, if he sees a snake, does not immediately kill it to the best of his ability, lest he himself or any other human whatsoever be infested by its bite. This action is driven not so much by a hatred of the beast as by a benevolence of man toward his fellow man.

Since, therefore, there is a just war against wild beasts, we nevertheless usually abstain from many of those animals that live alongside humans. For this reason, the Greeks eat neither dogs nor horses, nor likewise donkeys, regardless of whether they are wild or tame, because they are of the same kind; but they do eat pigs, as well as poultry <term>poultry: original *cohortales aues*, referring to birds kept in a farmyard or coop</term>. For a pig is useful for nothing else but for eating <note>This reflects a common ancient trope that the pig's soul was given to it merely as "salt" to preserve its meat until it could be eaten.</note>. However, the Phoenicians and Jews abstained from the pig for this reason: because it did not even originate <note>The author suggests pigs were not native to these regions, a common historical misconception used to explain dietary laws from an environmental rather than religious perspective.</note> in those places at all. Even now in Ethiopia...

<summary>This page explores the concept of a "just war" against wild animals for human safety while explaining why Greeks and other cultures choose to eat some domestic animals, like pigs, but not others, like horses or dogs.</summary>

# BOOK II.

...they claim that such an animal is not found there.
Just as, therefore, none of the Greeks sacrificed a camel or an elephant to the
gods, for the reason that Greece did not produce 
those animals at all; so too in Cyprus or Phoenicia this animal <note>The author likely refers to the pig or swine, which was considered taboo or unavailable in certain Eastern rites mentioned in the preceding context of Porphyry's *De Abstinentia*.</note>
was not offered to the gods because it was lacking in those places.
Nor do the Egyptians sacrifice it now for the same reason.
Moreover, the fact that some abstain entirely from this animal is 
just as if we too were to recoil from eating camel meat. 
But why should anyone abstain from eating animals? 
Is it so that they do not make their soul or their body 
worse? But it is clear that neither of these happens as a result. 
For those animals that feed on meat are more perceptive than the rest. 
Indeed, they are hunters, and they practice that skill by which they 
render their lives more abundant. They also acquire 
strength and power, like lions and wolves. Thus, it is observed 
that the eating of meat harms neither the soul nor the body. 
Add to this that proof of this matter can also be sought 
from the fact that athletes <note>original: *certatores*; referring to those competing in the public games who required high-protein diets.</note> make their bodies 
stronger by eating meat, and doctors are 
accustomed to restore the weakened bodies of the sick 
by providing meat as food. For it is no small indication 
that Pythagoras <note>Pythagoras (c. 570–495 BC) was famous in the ancient world for his "Golden Verses" and his prohibition against eating meat, based on the doctrine of the transmigration of souls.</note> was not correct in his opinion. 
For none among the wise men agreed with his decree. 
Not one of those Seven Sages <note>The Seven Sages were traditional wise men of the 6th century BC, including figures like Thales and Solon, admired for their practical wisdom.</note>, nor any of those who came after. 
Even the wisest Socrates, and those who followed him...

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous leaf, which discussed the physical benefits of meat-eating for athletes and the sick. -># ON ABSTINENCE<-

...were led away. But come, let us grant that all men have agreed to this <note>The "agreement" refers to the proposal that all humans should abstain from eating meat.</note>. To what point will the propagation of animals proceed? For it is hidden from no one how many offspring a sow bears, or a hare. Add to this the offspring of all other animals: from where will the fodder be supplied? What will the farmers do? For when their crops are devastated, they will not kill the devastators <note>The "devastators" are the overpopulated animals eating the crops.</note>. And the earth will not be able to bear the abundance of over-multiplying animals. Moreover, from the rot of those that die naturally, a corruption of the air will be introduced; and thus, once plagues have arisen, no refuge will be found. For the sea, the rivers, and the lakes will be filled with fish; the air with birds; and the earth will teem with various crawling things.

Add to this that many would be deprived of medicines if one had to abstain from eating animals. For those who are losing their sight preserve their vision by eating viper <note>original: *viperæ efu*; ancient and medieval medicine frequently used "theriac" or viper-flesh preparations to treat various ailments, especially poisonings and eye diseases.</note>. When a servant of the physician Craterus <note>Craterus was a famous Greek physician mentioned by Cicero and Horace; he was known for treating high-profile patients in Rome.</note> had fallen into a certain new disease, such that his flesh was falling away from his bones, he never felt any remedy from drugs; but when he began to eat a viper prepared in the manner of a fish, his flesh was restored to health and he recovered. Many other animals, and indeed even individual parts of animals, cure many diseases. All these cures he cuts himself off from, whoever refuses to feed on animals. 

But if, as they say, plants are also endowed with a soul <note>The author is likely addressing the Pythagorean or Manichaean view that plants, like animals, possess a form of life or "soul" (*anima*), suggesting that a consistent vegetarian would eventually have nothing left to eat at all.</note>, what finally will our life be, if we take nothing from animals nor from any plants? If indeed he does not act impiously who [plucks] plants...

-># BOOK I.<-

...[the farmer] plucks and pulls up [his crops]; nor is he who kills an animal to be considered guilty of a crime. But someone might say that if the souls of brutes <note>original: *brutorum*; in this context, "brutes" refers to all non-human animals, which were traditionally viewed as lacking the rational "spark" of humanity.</note> are of the same substance as our own, one should not behave cruelly through the slaughter of kin. But if one grants that these souls are joined to bodies of their own accord, he will assert that they do so because they are captured by the love of youth; for indeed, in youth, one is allowed to enjoy every pleasure. Why, then, do they not enter back into human nature? If, however, it must be admitted that they enter into bodies voluntarily and out of a love for youth—yet in such a way that they must travel through every species of animal—then we must think that the slaughter of animals is actually pleasing to them, because through it a faster return into human form is procured for them.

For if their bodies are eaten, it can bring no trouble to the souls, since they have already been separated from them and they long to be in the nature of man. Indeed, we must think that just as they are affected by great pain when they leave a human body, they feel an equal pleasure when they are released from other bodies. For in this way it happens that they return more quickly to man, who holds dominion over the rest of the living creatures, just as God holds it over men. Therefore, there is a sufficiently suitable reason for killing them: namely, that [unless they are killed] they harm men <note>The argument suggests that animals, if left alive, compete with humans for resources or cause direct harm; thus, killing them is a defensive necessity.</note>. For if the souls of men are immortal while those of brutes <gloss>quod</gloss> are mortal, we do not act unjustly by killing them. But if they too are immortal, we actually benefit them by killing them; for...

-># ON ABSTINENCE<-

...for by doing this, we make their return into human nature faster. But if we take vengeance <note>In this context, "vengeance" refers to the justified punishment or killing of animals that are perceived as harmful to humans.</note>, we do not commit an offense, but rather punish things that cause offense. Therefore, if their souls are immortal, we benefit them by inflicting death upon them; if they are mortal, we do nothing impious. If indeed we even take vengeance, how could we not be acting quite justly? For we kill the snake and the scorpion, even if they have not yet provoked us, aiding the common race of men so that no one else might suffer anything evil from them. Moreover, those things which attack men, or harm those who live with men, or lay waste to the crops—how should they not deservedly be removed from our midst?

Furthermore, if someone should consider this to be entirely an injustice, then he must also not use milk, or wool, or sheep <note>original: *ouis*; while this usually means sheep, in a list of products like milk and honey, it refers to the animal itself or potentially its eggs (*ova*), though the subsequent mention of shearing points toward the sheep.</note>, or honey. For just as you act unjustly if you despoil a man, so if you shear a sheep, you inflict an injury: for that is the sheep's clothing which you are taking away. Milk also was not prepared for you, but for the offspring already born. The bee also gathered that food for itself, which you, having snatched it away, turn into a luxury for yourself. 

I set aside for now the religion of the Egyptians, who think we act unjustly if we inflict violence upon plants <note>The author refers to certain ancient Egyptian or Pythagorean taboos against harming even plant life, suggesting that if one follows the logic of "non-harm" to its extreme, even eating vegetables would be a crime.</note>. But if these things are produced for our sake—the bee, serving us, works the honey, and the fleece grows upon the sheep so that it may be an ornament and a warmth for us. Indeed, we even sacrifice animals to the gods themselves as an exercise of piety; and among them, Apollo [is known as] the wolf-killer <note>original: *lupicida*; an epithet for Apollo (Lykeios), characterizing him as a protector of flocks who slays predatory wolves.</note>...

# BOOK I.

...[murderer], and Diana is called a "killer of wild beasts" <note>original: *fericida*</note>. For both the demigods and all the heroes who surpassed us in lineage and virtue so approved of the eating of living creatures that they even instituted the slaughter of sacrifices to the gods consisting of "dodecoids" <note>A sacrifice of twelve animals; original Greek: δωδεκοΐδας</note> and "hecatombs" <note>A massive public sacrifice, literally meaning "a hundred oxen"; original Greek: ἑκατόμβας</note>—that is, sacrifices of twelve sheep and a hundred oxen. Indeed, Hercules was celebrated with praise for this among other things: that he used to eat oxen regularly.

If, however, someone should say that Pythagoras <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 570–495 BC) whose followers famously practiced vegetarianism, often linked to the belief that souls migrate between humans and animals.</note> intended nothing else by this decree of his than to guard from a distance against humans eating one another, it is plainly foolish. For if in Pythagoras's time all people were eating one another, it is an absurd institution that seeks to deter the slaughter of other animals simply to keep humans from mutual cannibalism. Indeed, such a rule would rather incite the opposite behavior, as it would imply that eating one another is the same degree of sin as if they were feeding on the flesh of oxen and pigs. 

But if they were not eating one another at that time, what was the need for such an institution? Or, if he established this law for himself and his companions, it is a shameful argument indeed: for it suggests that those who associated with Pythagoras were suffering from this savage habit of eating one another. 

Add to this that the opposite of what he intended would have occurred. For if we were to abstain from living creatures, we would not only be deprived of such wealth and pleasure, but we would even lose the fields themselves, devastated by wild beasts. Since the entire earth would be occupied by serpents and birds...

-># OF ABSTINENCE<-

[...devastated] <note>The Latin suffix "-pata" likely completes a word like *vastata* (devastated) or *conculcata* (trampled), referring to fields.</note>: so that plowing could only be done with great difficulty, and whatever had been sown in any manner was immediately snatched away by birds; even those crops which had barely reached maturity were utterly destroyed by being consumed by four-footed beasts. At last, humanity would be reduced to such a scarcity of food that they would be forced to attack one another. To this is added the fact that the gods also handed down many institutions concerning animals for the sake of assistance and as a remedy. Histories are full of instances where the gods commanded certain people to sacrifice specific animals to them, and that the people should feed upon the sacrificed animals themselves.

Moreover, during the return of the Heraclidae <note>The "Heraclidae" were the legendary descendants of the hero Hercules who returned to the Peloponnesus to reclaim their ancestral kingdoms.</note>, those who had made the expedition into Laconia with Eurysthenes and Procles <note>Twin brothers and legendary founders of the dual kingship of Sparta.</note>, being placed in a state of extreme lack of necessities, ate serpents, which the earth brought forth as nourishment for the army. In Libya as well, a cloud of locusts presented itself to another army suffering from hunger. Something of this kind is also said to have happened in Gades <note>Modern-day Cádiz, Spain.</note>. Mogus was a king of the Mauritanians who was killed at Methone by Agrippa <note>Likely Marcus Vipsanius Agrippa, the Roman general and statesman.</note>. He was attacking the temple of Hercules, which was very wealthy in that place. 

It was the custom of the priests to sprinkle Hercules's altar daily with blood. That this practice was established not by human design but by a god, the occasion itself made clear. For when the siege was prolonged for a long time, and all the sacrificial victims <term>hostiae</term> <note>Animals designated for religious sacrifice.</note> had already run out, the priest—lacking both resources and a plan—experienced a dream of this kind. He seemed to himself...

## BOOK II

[...between] the Pillars of Hercules <note>original: "Herculis columnas"; the ancient name for the peaks flanking the entrance to the Strait of Gibraltar.</note> to stand in the middle, and from there to behold a bird sitting on an altar, which, when it had flown down into his hands, he had used to consecrate the altar. When he had seen this, having awakened immediately as day dawned, he went to the altar, and there—just as it had appeared to him in his dreams—when he had climbed the tower, he looked out. And behold, he saw that bird. He himself, joyful while he hoped the dream would come true, observed the matter. The bird, flying down, settled on the altar: then it worked its way into the priest's hands. And thus, having been sacrificed, it sprinkled the altar with its blood. 

Even more remarkable than this is what happened at Cyzicus <note>An ancient Greek city in Anatolia (modern Turkey).</note>. For at that time when Mithridates <note>Mithridates VI, the King of Pontus, who fought a series of wars against the Roman Republic.</note> was besieging it, the feast of Proserpina <note>The Roman name for Persephone, goddess of the spring and underworld.</note> was traditionally celebrated, during which it was the custom to sacrifice a heifer to her. Moreover, the sacred herd from which the victim was to be taken was grazing outside the city across the strait; the animals were, of course, already marked with the goddess’s brand <note>original: "inusta"; referring to the practice of branding sacred livestock to denote they belonged to a deity.</note>. Therefore, when the hour had now arrived, a heifer lowed and swam across the strait: after the guards had opened the gates for her, running inside, she stood at the altar, and so the sacrifice was offered to the goddess at its proper time. 

Not without merit, therefore, do people think it most pious to slaughter as many animals as possible, since this also seems pleasing to the gods. But if all citizens were of the opinion that they must abstain from the eating of animals, what kind of city would that turn out to be? By what means would it repel enemies invading against it, if it were most careful [to avoid harming living things]...

-># CONCERNING ABSTINENCE <-
<meta>catchword: sailors,</meta>

[...would follow] so that no other animal would be killed? Indeed, nothing would stand in the way of it <note>Referring to a city or society practicing total non-violence toward animals.</note> being most easily stormed and overturned. It would take too long to recount the other absurdities that would necessarily occur. Furthermore, that it is not impious to kill and eat animals is shown by the fact that even **Pythagoras** <note>Pythagoras is famously associated with vegetarianism; however, the author here cites a conflicting historical tradition to challenge that assumption.</note> himself—when the ancients used to provide competitors with milk to drink and cheese soaked in water to eat—whereas others after them rejected this diet and gave dried figs to athletes for food, he was the first to abandon that ancient custom and provide meat for those in training to eat. From this, he discovered a far superior capacity for building strength. There are also some who have handed down to memory that even the **Pythagoreans** themselves lay hands upon animals when they sacrifice to the gods. 

And these, indeed, are the arguments that I have found in **Clodius**, **Heraclides Ponticus** <note>A Greek philosopher and astronomer, a student of Plato.</note>, **Hermarchus the Epicurean** <note>The successor of Epicurus as the head of the Epicurean school.</note>, and among the **Stoics** and **Peripatetics** <note>term: Peripatetics; the followers of Aristotle, named after the "peripatos" or covered walkways where they walked and talked.</note>. Among these are also your own collected views, whatever have been brought to us by messengers. However, as I am about to oppose these and the opinions of nearly all men, I think it is right for me to make some preliminary remarks.

First, therefore, it must be known that this discourse of mine is not intended to provide a rule of life for every class of men. For it is meant neither for those who practice the common trades <note>original: "sordidas artes"; literally 'sordid' or 'dirty' arts, referring to manual labor or crafts considered lowly in the ancient world.</note>, nor for athletes, nor for soldiers, nor...

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that his defense of vegetarianism is not a universal rule for all people, but a specific spiritual discipline for philosophers. He is in the middle of listing those for whom these arguments are NOT intended.</meta>

### BOOK I

[...not intended for] sailors, nor orators, nor finally for any others who have committed themselves to a **busy life** <note>original: "negotiosam vitam" — a life of public affairs, business, or politics, as opposed to a life of quiet contemplation.</note>. No persuasion of this kind can be brought to them. Instead, it is meant only for those who consider who they truly are and from where they have come, and who have set for themselves a purpose regarding food and other duties that is different from the customs of others. For to those others, not even a word of this kind should be spoken.

Indeed, even in this common life, the same advice is not given to the man who sleeps—and who strives throughout the whole course of his life to prepare **sleep-inducing things** <note>original: "somnifera"</note> for himself from every source—as is given to the man who, striving earnestly to repel sleep, provides everything that leads to **wakefulness** <note>original: "vigiliam" — here used metaphorically for spiritual or intellectual alertness.</note>. 

For the former, one must advise him to indulge in **drunkenness**, **overindulgence** <note>term: crapulae — literally a "hangover" or the sickness resulting from excessive wine and food.</note>, and **gluttony** <note>original: "repletioni" — literally a "filling up" or stuffing of the belly.</note>. He should be advised to choose for himself dark rooms, a soft and wide bed—and as the poets say, a "rich" one—and to take every **sleep-inducing medicine** <note>original: "soporiferum medicamentum"</note> that brings on lethargy and forgetfulness, whether by smelling it, anointing himself with it, or by drinking or eating it. 

But for the latter, just as a sober drink free from wine is prescribed, so too food so meager must be ordered that it closely approaches **starvation** <note>original: "inediam"</note>. Furthermore, his house should be bright, filled with thin air, and open to the breeze. It must also be commanded that he be stirred by the vigorous movement of **cares and thoughts** <note>original: "cogitationum"</note>, and that he sleep in a small, hard bed. 

Whether we are naturally suited for this—I mean for **wakefulness**, so that we grant as little as possible to sleep—is clear, insofar as we are not currently in the region of those [who sleep]...

<meta>catchword: who</meta>

<summary>Porphyry distinguishes his audience, arguing that the discipline of abstinence is only for the philosopher seeking spiritual wakefulness, not for those living a life of worldly business. He uses a detailed metaphor of "sleep" versus "wakefulness" to contrast the indulgent life of the masses with the ascetic life of the seeker.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, abstinence, spiritual wakefulness, asceticism, philosophy vs. business, diet and sleep, Neo-Platonism</keywords>
