# ON ABSTINENCE

...who are perpetually **wakeful**; whether we were designed for sleeping would be another discussion, which would require long demonstrations. Now, speaking with the person who has once suspected the deceptions of **this inn of ours** <note>original: "diuersorii huius nostri" — a metaphor for the physical body or the material world as a temporary and distracting lodging for the soul.</note> in which we live, and has noticed both his own natural wakefulness and the **drowsiness** <note>original: "somnolentiam"</note> contracted from the place in which he dwells, we hand down a **way of life** <note>term: uictus rationem — a specific diet or regime of living designed to achieve a goal, in this case, spiritual clarity.</note> consistent with his suspicion and knowledge.

And we urge that he allow those who are sleeping to lie relaxed in their beds: lest, just as those who look at people with **sore eyes** <note>original: "lippitudine" — an inflammation of the eyes; ancient writers often believed such conditions could be caught simply by looking at the sufferer.</note> are affected, or those who are among people **yawning** <note>original: "oscitatione"</note> begin to yawn, we too might be filled with sleepiness and slumber. This is especially true since the place in which we stay is quite **lethargic** <note>original: "ueternosus" — literally "full of the sleep of old age," implying a heavy, chronic dullness.</note>, so that it brings inflammation to the eyes and, through its **vapors** <note>original: "exhalationibus"</note>, leads everyone into deep sleep and forgetfulness. 

If, therefore, even the **lawgivers** <note>original: "legislatores"</note> established laws with the intention of leading men toward a **contemplative life** <note>original: "contemplatiuam uitam" — a life dedicated to the study of truth and the exercise of the intellect.</note> and one led by the mind and intellect, then they must certainly be obeyed, and those things which they handed down concerning diet must be accepted entirely. But if they established their decrees while looking toward the "natural" life—which is called the **middle way** <note>original: "media dicitur" — a life of moderate social virtue, rather than the rigorous discipline of a philosopher.</note>—and toward those things which most of the common people could agree upon (those who are accustomed to regard external things and things pertaining to the body as either good or evil), then why would anyone, by bringing forward the laws of these men, seek to subvert that life...

<meta>catchword: piam [likely "piously"]</meta>

<summary>Porphyry argues that the physical world acts as a "lethargic inn" that dulls the soul's natural wakefulness. He asserts that while common laws are made for the average person, the philosopher must follow a stricter diet and discipline to remain intellectually and spiritually alert.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, De Abstinentia, contemplative life, spiritual wakefulness, lawgivers, diet, materialism vs. intellect</keywords>

Having warned against the "sleepiness" of the material world, he now distinguishes between the external laws of the state and the internal, divine law that governs the soul's return to its true nature: the Intellect.</meta>

# BOOK II

...ought to practice a **pious way of life**, which is superior to every written law promulgated to the common people, and follows most closely the **unwritten and divine law** <note>original: "non scriptam & diuinam... legem" — referring to the "natural" or "cosmic" law that governs the soul, as opposed to man-made statutes.</note>. For this is how the matter stands. That **contemplation** <term>contemplatio: the act of focused, spiritual vision or meditation on divine truths</term> which makes us happy is not established in a multitude of sciences and disciplines, as someone might suppose; nor does it receive growth from their increase. For if that were the case, nothing would prevent those from being happy who had acquired all disciplines from everywhere.

But it is far from being the case that every discipline completes contemplation; indeed, not even the **knowledge of those things that truly exist** <note>original: "eorum quæ re uera sunt, cognitio" — a Platonic reference to the Forms or the spiritual realities that exist beyond the physical world.</note> can provide this, unless a manner of life related and conformed to those truths is also added. For since there are, as they say, three goals for us—just as there are targets in any pursuit: 

1. That we attain the **contemplation** of that which truly exists; 
2. That the attainment of this, according to the faculty within us, may effect a **kinship** between the contemplators and the thing being contemplated; 
3. For one does not return to anything other than what he truly is himself; nor does kinship and union happen with anything other than what he truly is himself. 

Now, the "self" is truly the **Intellect** <term>intellectus: the highest part of the soul, representing pure reason and spiritual intuition</term>; therefore, the end goal will also be nothing other than to **live according to the Intellect**. Toward this goal, letters and external disciplines hold the place of **purification** <note>original: "purgationis" — in Neoplatonism, the "purgative virtues" are the first step in spiritual growth, where one cleanses the soul of worldly attachments.</note> in us, but they do not complete our happiness. For if happiness were established in the acquisition of disciplines, it might happen that, even if one neglected both nourishment and actions, they could still reach the end...

# ON ABSTINENCE

...to be mixed with it <note>original: "-sceremur" — likely the completion of "transmisceremur," referring to the soul becoming "mingled" or "mixed" with the physical body and its sensations.</note>. Since, indeed, we must be transformed from this present life into another, purified by both **reason** and **works** <note>original: "rationibus & operibus" — the "reasons" are philosophical contemplations, while "works" refer to the practical lifestyle of a philosopher, including diet and behavior.</note>: come then, let us consider what reasons and what works may lead us there.

Would it not seem that those things which separate us from objects exposed to the senses and from their disturbances, and which raise us toward a life that is **intellectual**, free from the imagination, and **impassive** <term>impatibilem: a state of being "un-passioned" or unaffected by the external world’s physical sensations and emotional disturbances</term>, are of this kind? Conversely, would not the opposite things be foreign and to be cast aside—all the more so because, while they call us away from the former, they entice us and drag us down toward the latter? I believe these things must certainly be granted.

For we are like people who, having traveled to foreign nations, have not only been driven out from all their domestic comforts, but have also been so imbued with the disturbances, customs, and foreign habits of that external region that they have become quite inclined toward them. Just as if someone wished to return home from there, he would not only strive to make the journey, but—in order to be welcomed back by his own people—he would also take care to **lay aside** whatever he had adopted from the foreigners. He would recall to his memory those things which he possessed before but had forgotten, without which he could in no way be received by his kin.

In the same way, if we are to return from this place to those things which are truly our own and domestic to us, we must lay aside all those things which we have contracted from our **mortal nature**, together with the affection with which we pursue them—

<summary>Porphyry employs an allegory of a traveler in a foreign land to argue that the soul must shed material habits and sensory passions to return to its true "home" in the intellectual realm.</summary>

# BOOK I.

...familiarized <note>original: "[-transmi]sceremur" or "[-conue]rteremur" — this completes the verb from the end of the previous page, likely referring to being accustomed to foreign habits</note>: from which our **descent** also occurred. We must remember our blessed and eternal substance and strive with our utmost power to return to those things which lack all color and quality. We must use two meditations: one, by which we cast aside everything material and mortal; the other, by which we make our return and ascend to those higher things in a manner opposite to how we descended to these lower ones.

For we were **intellectual substances** <note>original: "Intellectuales... substantiae" — beings of pure mind or spirit</note>, and we still are, pure from all sense and irrational nature. We were, however, joined with sense-perception because of a failure <note>original: "impotentia" — a lack of strength or an inability to maintain focus</note> of our eternal familiarity with the **intelligible** <term>intelligibili: the realm of pure reality and truth, which can only be perceived by the mind, not the physical eyes</term>, and because of the power we directed toward the things that exist here in the physical world. 

Indeed, all the faculties and powers that operate through the senses and the body sprouted forth because our **soul** <term>anima: the part of us that animates the body but originates in a higher spiritual realm</term> did not remain in the intelligible world. It is not unlike what happens from the unfitness <note>original: "prauitate" — literally 'crookedness' or 'defect'; used here as an agricultural metaphor for poor soil</note> of the earth, which often, when it has received wheat seed, produces wild oats instead. 

In the same way, this "descent" happened through a certain defect of the soul. This defect did not corrupt the soul's essential substance through the creation of an irrational nature, but through that nature, it joined the soul to **mortality** and dragged it away from its own home toward a foreign place. 

Therefore, if we wish to return to our former state, we must first strive with all our might to withdraw from sense-perception, from **imagination**, and from that irrational quality and its passions which follow from them—at least as far as the necessity of our physical birth <note>original: "generationis" — referring to the state of being a generated, physical being</note> allows. Secondly, we must set right those things that concern the **intellect**, bringing them into peace and quiet...

->D ii<-

<summary>Porphyry describes the soul's "descent" from a pure intellectual state into a material body as a result of a "defect" or "unfitness," comparing it to good seed producing wild oats in poor soil. He argues that the path back to our eternal substance requires stripping away sensory passions and focusing on the intellect.</summary>

->## ON ABSTINENCE<-

...is reconciled to [the soul] from the war which it wages with that nature devoid of reason. We must act so that we might not only hear about the **intellect** and the **intelligible** <term>intelligibili: the realm of pure reality and truth, which can only be perceived by the mind, not the physical eyes</term>, but also, as much as lies within us, enjoying its **contemplation** and being restored to an incorporeal nature, we may lead a life truly with that [higher world], and not falsely with those things that are kindred to bodies. 

We must, therefore, strip off many tunics: this visible and fleshly one, and then those with which we have been inwardly clothed <note>Porphyry is using the Neoplatonic metaphor of "garments" to describe the layers of the soul's attachment to the physical world; the outermost is the body, but there are inner layers of emotion and desire</note>, which immediately follow the skin-like and leathery ones. This is so that, thus unencumbered and naked, we may be able to enter the stadium to compete in the Olympian contests of the soul. However, the beginning of this struggle is that we strip ourselves; for unless we have done so, it is not permitted to compete. 

Because some of our garments are cast upon us from the outside, and others are thrown over us from within, we must also undertake a two-fold method of stripping: both through manifest things and through hidden things. For not eating [certain foods], or not accepting money when it is offered, is manifest and outwardly displayed; but the hidden and secret thing is to not desire them. 

Wherefore, along with the outward actions, we must also desist from the **affection** <term>affectio: the emotional attachment or internal drive toward material things</term> by which we are carried toward them. For what does it profit us if, retreating from the actions, we remain fixed and clinging to the causes from which those actions proceed? This retreat can be achieved both by applying force and by persuasion and reason, while the **affections** gradually waste away and—as someone might say—are committed to oblivion and die off. This is indeed the best kind of retreat, since it ef-

->## ON FREEDOM<-

...ward <note>original: "perficiat," completing the word from the previous page</note> it completes the task, so that nothing remains attached to that part from which the tearing away occurred. For whatever is torn away by force, even in these physical things we perceive, is usually accustomed to carry with it some small particle or a trace of that tearing. However, that voluntary separation <note>The "voluntary separation" is the philosophical practice of distancing the mind from bodily concerns while still alive</note> arises from a constant disregard for and lack of exercise of the **passions** <term>affectuum: the emotions or disturbances of the mind that pull it away from reason</term>. 

This disregard is achieved if, along with constant meditation on **intelligible** <term>intelligibilium: the realm of pure reality and truth, which can only be perceived by the mind, not the physical eyes</term> things, we abstain from those "garments" which excite disturbances within us—among which are also those things that pertain to our diet. We must therefore, no less than from other things, abstain from certain foods that are of such a nature that they incite the emotional power of our soul. 

Regarding this matter, it should be considered in this way: Two fountains flow here toward the binding of the soul, from which the soul, filled as if by a lethal potion <note>A likely reference to the River Lethe, the water of forgetfulness in Greek mythology</note>, is led into a forgetfulness of its own **contemplations**; these are, namely, **pleasure** and **pain**. These two are prepared by the senses and the perceptions that occur through the senses, as well as by the imaginations, opinions, and memories that follow those sensations. From these, the excited passions and the entire irrational power, having become "thickened" <note>Neoplatonists believed that indulging in the physical world made the soul "heavy" or "crass," literally weighing it down so it could not ascend to the divine</note>, drag the soul downward and turn it completely away from its own love toward that which truly *is*. 

From these things, therefore, one must withdraw with all one's strength. This withdrawal must be managed by excluding the passions that arise from the senses and from the irrational power. Now, sense-perceptions arise from those things which either are seen...

->## ON ABSTINENCE<-

...seen <note>original: "videntur," completing the word from the previous page</note>, or are heard, or which are perceived by taste, or smell, or touch. For the **senses** <term>sensus: the five physical faculties</term> exist in us as a sort of metropolis for a foreign colony of the passions. Behold indeed, look through each individual sense and see how great a fuel for disturbances flows into us: partly from the sight of horse races and athletic contests, or of wanton dances; partly from the contemplation of a woman. These things, by offering bait to the **irrational faculty** <term>irrationali facultati: the part of the soul governed by instinct and emotion rather than logic</term>, invade and subjugate it from every side with various snares thrown upon it. 

For because of all these things, the soul, raving through a force void of reason, causes men to jump up, to shout, and to cry out, while the external disturbance is inflamed by the internal one which the senses had ignited. But those things which happen through hearing—from certain shouts, and the sounds of foul speech and mockery—are such turbulent agitations that most people, as if having utterly departed from reason, are carried away in a frenzy; others, by contrast, are transformed into an effeminate state in every way. 

Moreover, there is no one who does not know how much the use of perfumes, or scented breaths, which peddle their charms to lovers, fattens the irrational power in the soul. For regarding the disturbances admitted through **taste**, what could anyone say? Especially since a double bond is woven from there: the first, because by the act of tasting the soul is fattened by the passions; the second, because from the filling up with **foreign bodies** <note>A reference to the consumption of animal flesh, which Porphyry argues weighs down the soul with the material essence of another living being</note>, it becomes heavy and powerful...

<summary>Porphyry describes the five senses as a "metropolis" that allows external passions to colonize the soul, specifically citing the dangers of public spectacles, sexual desire, and the consumption of meat.</summary>
<keywords>senses, passions, irrational soul, asceticism, diet, perfumes, public games, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument regarding the soul's entrapment by sensory experiences. The opening word "redditur" (is rendered) completes the sentence from the previous page which discussed how the sense of taste acts as a second "bond" to the physical world.</meta>

->### BOOK I<-

is rendered <note>original: "redditur," completing the thought from the previous page regarding how the soul is made physical through the senses</note>. For as a certain physician said, **poisons** are not only those prepared by **medicine** <term>medicina: here referring to the apothecary's art of mixing drugs or compounds</term>; rather, they are also the foods and drinks which we are accustomed to consume daily for **nourishment** <gloss>alimentum</gloss>. From these, a lethal power is diffused into the soul even more than the destruction brought to the body by physical poisons.

Moreover, the sensations of touch, by making the **soul** <term>anima: the vital breath or life-force, which Porphyry believes should remain pure and separate from matter</term> almost corporeal, often cause it to break out into inarticulate sounds, as if the soul itself were already <gloss>iam</gloss> becoming a physical body. From all of these things, memories, imaginations, and opinions are gathered, which stir up an innumerable herd of **disturbances** <term>perturbationum: the "passions" or emotional agitations that cloud human reason</term>. These fill the soul with the "diseases" of fear, desire, anger, love, allurements, grief, envy, anxiety, and other such agitations.

And for this reason, it is difficult to be purged of them; a truly great labor must be undertaken if we wish to be liberated from the contemplation of these things, since the necessary **connection of the senses** <term>sensuum coniunctio: the inescapable link between our consciousness and our physical organs</term> accompanies us everywhere, day and night. Hence, as much as it lies in our power, we must go far away from those types of places where a person might, even against their will, run into a crowd. We must especially avoid the "battle" that arises from experiencing these things—and, if you will, even avoid the "victory" over them; instead, we should embrace the state of being unpracticed, which comes from a lack of experience <note>Porphyry argues that it is better to never be tempted at all than to struggle to overcome temptation</note>. For in this way we hear of the fame of the men of old, the **Pythagoreans** <term>Pythagoreorum: followers of Pythagoras who practiced strict asceticism, silence, and vegetarianism to purify the soul</term> and other sages; some of whom chose the most deserted places for their dwelling...

<summary>Porphyry explains how food, drink, and physical touch "thicken" the soul, making it more like a body and filling it with disruptive passions. He concludes that the only way to attain purity is to physically withdraw from society and avoid sensory temptations entirely, following the example of the Pythagoreans.</summary>

->## ON ABSTINENCE<-

[...conducted] <note>original: "gebant," the ending of the verb "agebant," meaning they spent or conducted their lives</note> their lives: others indeed lived in cities, but nevertheless they spent their lives in temples and sacred precincts, to which access was barred to the common crowd. **Plato**, however, chose to inhabit the **Academy** <term>Academia: the school founded by Plato in a grove outside Athens</term>, which besides being in a deserted place and far from the city, was also considered somewhat unhealthy, as they say <note>Historical accounts suggest Plato chose this location specifically because its quietness outweighed the risk of its reputedly "swampy" or malarial air</note>. Indeed, it is well known that there were some who, out of a desire for **internal contemplation** <gloss>internæ cōtemplationis</gloss> and so as not to be distracted, did not spare their own eyes <note>A reference to the philosopher Democritus, who legend says blinded himself to prevent his vision from disturbing his mental focus</note>.

But if anyone thinks that by living among men and satisfying his senses with their passions, he will remain **unaffected** <term>impatibilem: the state of being "impassible" or free from emotional suffering and external influence</term>, he deceives both himself and those he has persuaded. He fails to notice that by the very fact that he does not distance himself from the crowd, he has surrendered most fully to the power of the passions. For it was not without cause that he <note>Plato, specifically in his dialogue the *Theaetetus*</note> spoke concerning the nature of philosophers, saying: 

"These truly from childhood do not even know the way to the marketplace, nor where the **forum**, or the **law-court** <gloss>curia</gloss>, or any other public meeting-place of the city is situated. Moreover, the **laws** and **decrees** <gloss>leges autē, & decreta</gloss> which are either spoken or written, they neither see nor hear. The strivings of political clubs for office, and gatherings, and dinners, and revelries with flute-girls—these things do not even occur to them in their dreams. For whether anything good or bad has happened in the city, or if some misfortune has befallen someone from their ancestors, whether male or female, it is more hidden from him than the pebbles of the sea washed upon the shore." And all these things...

<summary>Porphyry discusses the historical precedent of philosophers seeking isolation—such as Plato's Academy—to protect the soul from the "passions" of the city. He quotes Plato's description of the ideal philosopher who remains entirely ignorant of worldly politics and social gossip.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Porphyry’s *On Abstinence*, specifically the section where he quotes Plato’s *Theaetetus* to describe the ideal philosopher's detachment from worldly affairs and physical appetites.</meta>

### BOOK I.

...nor does he even know that he is ignorant; for he does not abstain from these things for the sake of catching glory. Instead, in truth, only his body remains and dwells in the city, while his mind, considering all these things small and worth nothing at all, holds them in contempt. It flies everywhere, as **Pindar** <note>Pindar was a famous Greek lyric poet; Porphyry is here quoting a passage Plato used to describe the soaring nature of the mind</note> says, measuring the depths beneath the earth and the heights above, surveying the celestial stars and the whole nature of things everywhere, yet never involving itself in anything that is near at hand. 

By these words, **Plato** says that the one who does not involve himself in the aforementioned things remains **unaffected** <term>impatibilem: a state of being "impassible" or free from being acted upon by external physical sensations</term> by them—but only if he does not immerse himself in any of these matters. Therefore, he says, such a man does not know the way to the marketplace, nor where the **forum** <term>forum: the public square used for judicial and business affairs</term> is, nor the **council chamber** <term>curia: the meeting place of the senate or high officials</term>, nor any other specific place of this kind. He does not, he says, know these things and yet frequent them; for if he were frequenting them and filling his senses with them, he would not even know if he was ignorant. Rather, on the contrary, while he abstains from them and remains ignorant of them, only then does he say that he does not even know whether he is ignorant. 

As for attending dinner parties, he says it does not even occur to him in his dreams. Much less, then, is he going to be indignant if he is deprived of some **broth** <note>original: "iusculo," a small or thin soup</note> or a **morsel of meat** <note>original: "caruncula," a diminutive of "caro" (flesh), suggesting a trivial piece of meat</note>. Nor will he apply any care at all to these things; rather, he will consider all these things small and nothing if he abstains, but if he partakes, he will judge them great and harmful. For since he holds within the nature of things the examples that the divine is most happy, and conversely, that which lacks divinity is most wretched, he [makes] <note>The sentence continues on the next page</note> himself like the former and unlike the latter...

He emphasizes that the philosopher views worldly concerns, including meat-eating, as insignificant or even harmful compared to the pursuit of divine happiness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument from Book I of *On Abstinence*. Having previously described the philosopher’s detachment from civic life and physical distractions, he now transitions into how this detachment must extend to the consumption of food to prevent the soul from becoming "nailed" to the body.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>### ON ABSTINENCE</header>

...he will provide: and he will establish a life suitable to that which he wishes to resemble, content with a very meager **diet** <term>uictu: referring here to both "food" and "way of life"</term>, and one which is minimally filled with mortal things. For as long as someone argues about foods, and contends that even this or that ought to be eaten, and is not of the opinion that—if it were possible—one should assert that one must abstain from all **nourishment** <term>alimento: food or sustaining matter</term> entirely, he seems to boastfully and vainly champion the **passions** <term>affectibus: the "affects" or emotions and physical urges that disturb the soul</term>; for he thinks those things about which he argues are of no consequence. 

But he who **practices philosophy** <term>philosophatur: to live according to the pursuit of wisdom</term> will not withdraw himself from these things merely by force: for he who is violently compelled is nonetheless accustomed to resist in that place from which he is unwillingly led away; however, he will not think he is doing something of no consequence if he makes his bond <note>The "bond" refers to the connection between the soul and the physical body</note> thicker. 

Therefore, providing only what is strictly necessary to nature, and that very light, and by means of the lightest foods possible, he will reject whatever exceeds this measure, as it contributes to **pleasure** <term>uoluptatem: sensory delight which Porphyry views as a distraction</term>. For he is convinced of what that man <note>Porphyry is referring to Plato, specifically a passage in the *Phaedo* (83d)</note> says: that **sensation** <term>sensum: the faculty of perceiving through the five senses</term> is a **nail** to the **soul** <term>anima: the vital and intellectual principle of a person</term>, which, through the multitude of things it perceives, penetrates the soul and, through enjoyment, glues and, in a certain way, fastens it to the **body**. 

For if the senses were not an impediment to the operation of the pure soul, what difficulty would there be so that, although someone were in the body, he would nonetheless be affected by no motion of the body? For in what way would you have judged, or said, what you had suffered, unless you were affected and present to those things which you suffered?

<meta>catchword: Mind</meta>

<summary>Porphyry argues that a philosopher must embrace a meager diet because sensory pleasure acts as a "nail" that glues the soul to the body, hindering spiritual purity. He asserts that arguing for specific foods is a sign of being controlled by physical passions rather than reason.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the detachment of the philosopher's mind. The previous page established that the philosopher seeks to thin the "bond" between the soul and the body by minimizing food and sensory distractions. Porphyry now explains how the mind’s presence is determined by its focus, citing Plato’s description of the philosopher as someone who appears "ridiculous" to the worldly because his attention is fixed elsewhere.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>### BOOK I. 18</header>

For the **mind** <term>mens: the higher intellect or spiritual consciousness</term> is indeed present to itself, even if we are not present to that same mind. However, he who has departed from the mind is in that place to which he has departed: wandering here and there according to how he directs his soul toward various **perceptions** <term>perceptionibus: sensory or mental apprehensions</term>, he is present there, wherever the object of his senses occurs. For it is one thing not to notice sensible things elsewhere due to the soul’s focus, and quite another to think one is not present while actually standing by and paying attention. 

Certainly, no one will show that **Plato** <note>Porphyry is likely referencing the *Theaetetus*, where Socrates describes the philosopher as someone who knows nothing of his neighbor's business because his mind is elsewhere.</note> concedes this point, unless they also wish to demonstrate that Plato deceives himself. Therefore, he who has voluntarily given himself over to the enjoyment of **foods** <term>edulia: things to be eaten</term>, and spectacles, and social gatherings, and laughter, is—by that very surrender and approach—present in that place where the **passion** <term>affectio: a state of being influenced or moved by external things</term> is located. 

But he who is intent on other things and stays away from these [pleasures], is that one who, because of inexperience, offers a cause for laughter not only to **Thracian handmaids** <note>original: "thracibus ancillis". This refers to a famous anecdote in Plato's *Theaetetus* where a Thracian servant girl laughs at the philosopher Thales because he fell into a well while looking up at the stars.</note>, but even to the rest of the crowd. And when he is compelled [to engage in worldly affairs], he falls into every hesitation: certainly not because he lacks sense, or because he does not sense accurately, but because he operates only by the **irrational faculty** <note>The part of the soul responsible for basic reactions and instincts, as opposed to the higher "reasoning" part.</note>. Indeed, Plato never dared to say this: rather, he said that such a man has this specific trait even in the face of insults—that he cannot affect anyone with an insult himself, as one who knows no evil of anyone, because, as Plato says, he has not meditated upon it. 

Therefore, hesitating, he appears ridiculous; and in the midst of praises and the boastings of others, when he is seen to laugh not mockingly but truly, he seems **delirious** <note>original: "delirus". In this context, it means appearing insane or out of touch with reality to those who value social status.</note>. Wherefore, through inexperience and **abstinence** <term>abstinentia: the practice of refraining from bodily pleasures</term> he does not know [of worldly matters]; but not because, coming into experience, his irrational faculty...

<meta>catchword: tate</meta>

<summary>Porphyry explains that the philosopher is "absent" from worldly affairs not through a lack of ability, but because his mind is focused on higher truths, making him appear foolish to the unlearned. <header>->ON ABSTINENCE<-</header>
<sig>M</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...of [generation]</meta>

...operating with this faculty, he is able to contemplate fully with the **intellect** <term>intellectu: the higher, intuitive mind</term> and the **mind** <term>mente: the seat of reason</term>. For not even those who say we have two souls grant us two separate powers of attention. To do so would be to create a union of two distinct animals, where one could not perceive the actions of the other while it was occupied with its own affairs. 

But what need would there be to waste away the **passions** <term>affectus: the emotional and sensory impulses</term>, and to die as far as they are concerned, and to meditate on this daily <note>This is a reference to the Platonic "meditation on death," which involves the separation of the soul from the body’s desires during life.</note>: if it were possible for us to operate from the intellect and attend to mortal things without any inspection from the intellect, as some assert? For "the intellect sees, and the intellect hears," as that famous man says <note>Porphyry is quoting the philosopher-poet Epicharmus.</note>. 

If, indeed, while eating very sumptuously and drinking the sweetest wine, you can focus your mind on immaterial things: why can you not do the same while performing those acts which are not even honorable to speak of? For everywhere, these are the passions of the **child within us** <note>A common Platonic metaphor for the irrational, desiring part of the soul that must be disciplined by reason.</note>. 

But will you assert that because these things are shameful, the mind is not drawn down to them? But what kind of distinction is this at last: that you cannot be affected by some things unless you pay attention and are present to them, yet you grant that you can perform others even while you are occupied in perceiving **intelligible things** <term>intelligibilibus: non-physical truths accessible only to the mind</term>? For it must certainly not be said that some things are considered shameful among the common people, while others are not. For all things are shameful insofar as they relate to a life that ought to be led according to the intellect. And one must abstain from all of them, no differently than from **sexual pleasures** <note>original: "a venereis"</note>, except for the small amount of food needed for the purpose of **generation**...

->### BOOK I.<-

...of the necessity of nature must be granted. For wherever there is sensation and the perception of it, there must necessarily be a withdrawal from the **intelligible** <term>intelligibili: the realm of pure, immaterial reality accessible only to the mind, not the senses</term>. Moreover, the more the movement of the **irrational part** <note>The lower part of the soul governed by instincts and desires.</note> is stirred up, the more the **act of intellection** <term>intellectio: the operation of the higher mind or 'intellect'</term> fails. For it is not possible for us to be carried here and there at the same time; while we are here, we cannot also be present there. For attention is not achieved by a single part of us, but by our whole being. 

Indeed, this opinion—that one can remain free for the things of the intellect while all the senses are being affected—has driven many even among the **barbarians** <note>Porphyry likely refers to certain Gnostic sects or radical groups who argued that physical actions were irrelevant to spiritual purity.</note> headlong into ruin and destruction. Through negligence, they poured themselves into every kind of pleasure, asserting that they could indulge themselves because they could still use their irrational faculties while their attention was focused elsewhere. 

Now indeed, I have heard some who defend their own wretchedness in this manner. They say, "Food does not pollute us at all, just as the sea is not defiled by dirty currents; for we master all things, just as the sea is superior to all fluids." They argue that if the sea were to close its mouth so as not to admit what flows into it, it would indeed be great in itself, but in relation to the world it would be considered small, as if it were unable to contain filth. If it were truly wary of being defiled, it would surely not admit such things; but since it recognizes its own greatness, it receives all things and turns away nothing that flows into it. "In the same way," they say, "if we avoid the eating of certain things, we [fear]... <note>original: "timorem" — the sentence continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that human attention is a unified force and cannot be divided between intellectual contemplation and sensory indulgence. He specifically refutes the "sea" metaphor used by some to justify eating meat and seeking pleasure without spiritual consequence.</summary>

# ON ABSTINENCE

...[being] subject to death, we are reduced to slavery: for whom it is necessary that all things be subjected. For if a small amount of water collected in some place admits any filth, it is immediately polluted, and it is overwhelmed and overcome by that filth. However, a **great abyss** <note>original: *gurges*; here used metaphorically to mean a vast body of water or a whirlpool that can absorb impurities without changing.</note> is not defiled. In the same way, food overcomes and oppresses those who are small [in spirit], but for those who possess an abyss and an immense breadth of liberty, all things are consumed as nourishment and bring no defilement. 

These people, therefore, deceiving themselves with such follies, have acted in accordance with their own errors; they have thrust themselves out of liberty into the **abyss of misery** and the depths, and have suffocated. This was also what led some of the **Cynics** <note>A school of philosophy famous for rejecting social norms. Some took this to an extreme, arguing that since "nature" is good, no physical act is shameful.</note> to desire and devour everything, because they applied their minds to the cause of errors—to that which we are accustomed to call **indifferent** <term>indifferens: a technical philosophical term (Greek: *adiaphora*) for things like food, wealth, or health, which were thought to be neither morally good nor bad in themselves.</term>. 

But a cautious man, who suspects the deceits of nature and considers the nature of his own body—noticing how it was fitted like an instrument to the powers of the soul—is well aware that its harmony is such that if it is struck from the outside and that strike is perceived, then whether we wish it or not, the passions resonate. For perception is nothing other than the sound itself. Yet the soul cannot resonate unless it is entirely turned toward the resonance, directing its **presiding eye** <note>The *eye of the soul* refers to the faculty of attention or the intellect's focus.</note> toward it. For since the **irrational faculty** <note>The lower part of the soul that governs instincts and sensory reactions rather than logic.</note> is by its nature thoughtless, and can in no way judge how far, or in what manner, or from where, or toward what it should act,

-># ON ABSTINENCE<-

->A iij<-

...and in taking food, as well as in other bodily operations or enjoyments, let the present **charioteer** <note>original: *auriga*; a classic Platonic metaphor where Reason acts as a driver controlling the "horses" of the soul's passions.</note> prescribe both the measure and the proper timing. But if he is absent—and as some say, occupied with his own affairs—if he should take our attention along with him, he allows the **irrational faculty** <term>irrational faculty: the *facultas irrationalis*, the part of the soul driven by instinct and appetite rather than logic.</term> neither to be affected nor to perform any action at all. Yet if he allows our attention to remain present without his guidance, he destroys the man, since the man is then dragged headlong by irrational madness.

Therefore, it is fitting for upright men that, among the pleasures and actions that arise through the body, they should abstain more from foods than from touch <note>original: *tactus*; in this context, it likely refers to sexual desire or general physical sensations.</note>. This is because, although it is necessary to descend from our own higher character to train the irrational faculty within us while we are dealing with physical things, this must be done even more so when taking food. For the irrational power is unable to consider what the eventual result of these foods will be; its nature is such that it can in no way perceive what is not immediately present. 

For if we could be as free from the need for nourishment as we are from things seen by the eye once they are removed—since when those visual images are put to rest, the mind is free to focus on other matters—it would be a simple enough matter: once we had indulged the necessity of our mortal nature for a short while, we would immediately emerge free. But in reality, a great delay of time is drawn out while food is being both **concocted** <term>concoction: *concoquitur*, an ancient medical concept where the stomach "cooks" food using internal heat to turn it into nutrients.</term> and digested; and while, even before that, strength is gathered from sleep, rest, and other leisure; and after certain things from the digestion result in a **temperament**... <note>The text refers to the *temperatura*, or the balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that results from the digestive process.</note>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a charioteer (Reason) to argue that humans must consciously govern their physical appetites, especially the consumption of food. The author explains that unlike sight, which can be dismissed easily, food requires a long, taxing biological process of digestion that distracts the soul from higher thoughts.</summary>

# TO OPTATIANUS, BOOK I.

...future things, and the excretion of waste is accomplished: it is necessary that a **pedagogue** <term>pedagogue: from the Greek *paidagōgos*, a guardian or tutor who supervised a child’s conduct and escorted them to school. Here, it refers to Reason guiding the soul.</term> be present, who, choosing for himself light things that present no hindrance, grants them to nature. For since he foresees what is to come, and how great a hindrance it would be if he yielded to desires, he has judged it unfair that, for the sake of a brief pleasure—which is perceived in swallowing while we take in food—such a heavy **burden** <note>original: *sarcina*; literally a soldier's pack, used here to describe the physical weight and sluggishness of a heavy diet.</note> should weigh us down.

Therefore, not without cause, **Reason** <term>Reason: the *ratio*, the logical faculty that distinguishes humans from animals.</term>—excluding what is excessive and superfluous—contracts that which is necessary into a small and narrow space. This is so that Reason does not undergo troubles in providing while it seeks more; nor require more servants to season rich foods; nor be affected by more pleasures in eating; nor be filled with much laziness by over-sating itself; nor fall into sleepiness by stuffing itself with a heavier burden; nor, by filling itself with things that fatten the body, make the **bond** <note>original: *vinculum*; a reference to the Platonic idea that the body is a prison or a bond that ties the soul to the material world.</note> stronger, and itself lazier and weaker in performing its own duties. 

Therefore, let anyone show us—anyone who strives, as much as possible, to lead a life according to the **intellect** <term>intellect: the *intellectus*, the highest part of the soul capable of grasping divine truths.</term> and desires to be disturbed and distracted as little as possible by bodily **affections** <term>affections: *affectibus*, the passions or physical states that influence the mind.</term>—whether the eating of meat is more abundantly available than the side dishes of fruit and vegetables, or prepared more cheaply; and whether it brings less pleasure, and is lighter in **concoctions** <note>original: *concoctionibus*; in ancient medicine, this refers to the "cooking" or first stage of digestion in the stomach.</note> and digestions, and irritates the desires less, and to...

# *ON ABSTINENCE*

...of the soul, and to make the powers of the body less, than a diet which is sought from inanimate things, and those which require no labor from **cooks**. <note>The author argues that processed or cooked foods—especially meat—require more effort and cause more internal distraction than raw or simple plant foods.</note> But if neither a physician, nor a philosopher, nor a **trainer**, <term>trainer: original: *alipta*; a specialist in Greek gymnasia who anointed athletes with oil and supervised their physical conditioning and diet.</term> nor anyone from the common crowd has ever dared to affirm this, why do we not of our own accord abstain from this **burden** <note>original: *sarcina*; a soldier's pack, used here to describe the physical weight and sluggishness of a heavy diet.</note> of the body? Why, finally, do we not liberate ourselves from the slavery of many things by so abstaining? For one can be liberated not from one, but from six hundred things, if they have become accustomed to being content with the least: from an abundance of money; from the service of many slaves; from a wealth of household goods; from a sleepy disposition; from the violence and multitude of diseases; from the need for doctors; from the incitement to **lust**; <note>original: *venerea*; refers to the desires of Venus, or sexual impulses, which ancient philosophers believed were inflamed by "hot" or heavy foods like meat.</note> from thicker exhalations; from an abundance of waste; from the thickness of the **bond**; <note>original: *vinculi*; the Platonic concept of the body as a chain or prison for the soul.</note> from powers exciting one toward external actions; and finally from an **Iliad of evils**, <note>original: *malorum iliade*; an ancient idiom for a massive or endless series of troubles, referencing the length and tragedy of Homer's *Iliad*.</note> from which a frugal, thin, and easily obtained nourishment leads us away and withdraws us; while it prepares peace and tranquility for **Reason**, which suggests what is healthy for us. For, as **Diogenes** <note>Diogenes of Sinope, the Cynic philosopher famous for his extreme poverty and simple lifestyle.</note> says, thieves and enemies do not arise from **bread-eaters**, <term>bread-eaters: original: *paniuoris*; those who subsist on a simple, grain-based vegetarian diet.</term> but slanderers and tyrants arise from **meat-eaters**. <term>meat-eaters: original: *carniuoris*; the text associates meat consumption with aggression and political corruption.</term> Now indeed, once the cause for our many needs is removed, and the abundance of those things taken into the body is withdrawn, and the weight of those things which are digested is lightened, it happens that the free eye emerges tranquil, out from the wave and the **bodily smoke**. <note>original: *fumum corporeum*; a metaphor for the digestive vapors that were thought to rise to the head and "cloud" the mind's ability to think clearly.</note> This is something that requires neither proof, nor...

# BOOK I.

...which <note>original: *neque*; completing a sentence from the previous page regarding things that do not require proof.</note> requires demonstration because of the evidence inherent in the matter itself. From this, not only those who strive to live according to the **intellect**, and who establish such a life as their goal, believe that this abstinence is necessary for them to achieve their end; but also every philosopher, I think—judging **frugality** <note>original: *parsimoniam*; the wise management of one's resources and needs.</note> to be suitable for conducting a life in society—would more highly approve of a life content with few things than one that required many. 

Add to this what might perhaps seem incredible to most: we shall find that those who believe **pleasure** to be the goal of philosophy also assert and prove this. For many of the **Epicureans**, <note>Followers of Epicurus, who was often wrongly accused of promoting gluttony; in reality, he advocated for *ataraxia* (tranquility) through simple living.</note> even if we choose to begin with their founder himself, seem to have been content with a simple crust of bread and the fruits of plants for their food. Their books are filled with this kind of teaching, intended to show how little nature actually requires, and to declare that a meager and **easily-obtained diet** is sufficient to supply all necessities. "The riches of nature," he says, "are limited and easily obtained; but those which arise from empty opinions are infinite, and are of such a kind that they can only be acquired with difficulty." 

For those things which are easily available, since they possess the simple nature of the **moist and the dry**, <term>the moist and the dry: original: *humidi & sicci*; referring to the basic elemental qualities of food and the body in ancient Greek and Roman medicine.</term> are accustomed to removing, well and abundantly, every bodily distress that arises from want. All other things, however, which slide into luxury, possess a desire that is not necessary...

<header>ON ABSTINENCE</header>
<page-num>7</page-num>

...they say, nor is it of such a kind that it results from some necessary pain. Instead, it arises either from the mere distress felt by the absence of a thing, or entirely from the joy experienced in its presence, or from empty and false opinions. It has nothing to do with any natural defect which, if not satisfied, could destroy our **physical constitution** <note>original: *constitutionem nostram*; our bodily health or survival.</note>. For those things which nature necessarily requires are sufficient for maintaining our health. Moreover, because such things are cheap and few in number, they can be easily acquired.

Furthermore, he who eats meat also requires **inanimate things** <note>original: *inanimatis*; in this context, plants, grains, and water.</note> for his diet. But for the person who is content with inanimate things alone, his needs are easily met at half the cost, and he requires very few expenses for the preparation of his food. 

For they say that it is not right that a man should first prepare his necessities and then later apply philosophy as a kind of optional addition. Rather, he should first acquire **endurance of mind** and virtue in the proper way, and then attend to his daily needs. For we would surely be entrusting our affairs to a **bad counselor** <note>The "bad counselor" refers to the unbridled passions or the irrational part of the soul, which cannot distinguish between true needs and excessive desires.</note> if, without philosophy, we sought to measure and prepare for ourselves what nature requires. 

Wherefore, while practicing philosophy, it is necessary to provide for these basic needs, but only insofar as the attention required by them does not interfere with higher concerns. However, to the extent that it takes away from the soul, so that it does not hinder its **perfect purification** <note>original: *perfectae purgationi*; a core Neoplatonic goal where the soul is "cleansed" of its attachment to the physical world to focus on the divine.</note>, one must not pursue the preparation of wealth or elaborate food. Therefore, when these necessities are managed alongside philosophy, they will present themselves immediately. For if much indeed...

<header>ON FREEDOM</header>
<page-num>23</page-num>

Therefore, it is better that we pursue what is least among these things, as well as cheap and light. For since these things are very few, the very least amount of trouble will arise from them. Truly, a great **elaborate preparation** <note>original: *apparatus*; refers here to the complex arrangements for a luxurious lifestyle or meal.</note> for these things brings many hindrances with it: partly from the burdening of the body, and partly from the difficulty of the things being prepared. Furthermore, it prevents the constant exercise of our **primary reflections** <note>original: *præcipuarum considerationum*; the core philosophical meditations that lead to a tranquil soul.</note>. For any other reason, such luxury is immediately useless; it is not of such a kind that it might in any way compensate for the disturbances that follow from it. Add to this that since a philosopher ought to be in such hope as to believe that nothing will be lacking to him in life: hope of this kind is preserved by things easily obtained, just as it is weakened and diminished by things that are expensive and luxurious. For it happens because of this that most people, although they possess much, never cease to labor, as if wealth were always about to fail them. Now, so that we may be content with whatever is cheapest and easiest to acquire, it helps most of all if we keep in memory that there is nothing that has any power to bring about the **calming of the soul's disturbances** <note>original: *turbationum animi sedationem*; a reference to *ataraxia*, the state of being undisturbed or tranquil.</note>—at least those of any significance—even if all the riches in the world were collected. On the other hand, to settle the distress of the body, anything even quite moderate is sufficient, and these are things that can very easily be supplied from everywhere. Even if these are slightly lacking, they cannot disturb the person who **meditates constantly on death** <note>original: *mortem assidue meditatur*; a classic philosophical practice (memento mori) used to realize the triviality of physical comforts.</note>. It follows—

<meta>This page continues the argument for a simple, meat-free diet. -># ON ABSTINENCE<-

...and the pain which exists from deficiency is far milder than that which comes from **repletion** <note>original: *repletione*; the state of being overfilled or stuffed with food.</note>, unless someone deceives himself with vain opinions. Furthermore, a variety of foods not only fails to remove the disturbance of the soul, but it does not even increase the pleasure of the body; for pleasure reaches its natural limit as soon as the removal of pain has been accomplished. Thus, neither does the eating of **flesh** <note>original: *carnium*; meat.</note> dissolve any natural distress, nor does it provide anything which, had it not been consumed, would have led to pain. Instead, it offers a **violent delight** <note>original: *violentam gratiam*; a sharp, intense, but ultimately destabilizing physical pleasure.</note> which is quickly mixed with its opposite. Indeed, it does not contribute to the preservation of life, but only to the variety of pleasures, no differently than **sexual matters** <note>original: *uenerea*; literally "things of Venus," referring to sexual indulgence.</note> or the drinking of foreign wines, without which nature is perfectly able to persist. For those things without which nature cannot endure are very few, and of such a kind that they can be obtained easily, with justice, liberality, and in great quiet and leisure. Indeed, we shall find that meat does not contribute to health either, but is rather a hindrance to it. For it is necessary that health be preserved by the same means through which it is recovered; and it is recovered by a very slender diet, free of meat. Therefore, it is by this same diet that health will be made to endure. If, however, food sought from **inanimate things** <note>original: *inanimatis*; plants and non-living sources.</note> does not grant the **strength of Milo** <note>original: *Milonis robur*; Milo of Croton was a legendary Greek wrestler, used here as the archetype of unnecessary physical bulk.</note>, nor contribute at all to the massive increase of power—well, neither strength nor the increase of physical power is required by a **philosopher**, who is devoted to contemplation, not to physical actions...

<summary>This page argues that meat is an unnecessary luxury that does not increase true pleasure or health. The author posits that a simple, plant-based diet is sufficient for the philosopher, whose goal is intellectual contemplation rather than the physical bulk of an athlete.</summary>

### BOOK 2.

...and is likely to be devoted to **excesses** <note>original: *intemperantiis*; a lack of self-control, particularly regarding food and drink.</note>. Nor should it be surprising if many of the **common people** <note>original: *e uulgo*; the masses, whom the author distinguishes from philosophers.</note> think that the eating of meat contributes to **health**; for these same people also believe that pleasures and **sexual matters** <note>original: *uenerea*; literally "things of Venus," referring to sexual indulgence.</note> preserve health. These are so far from helping anyone, that it might be more than enough if they do not even cause harm. 

But if most people are not of this sort [that seeks wisdom], it is nothing to us. For neither is any certainty or stability of **philosophy** and contemplation found in the majority of people. Nor are common men fit to take up **wisdom**, or any part of it which possesses something excellent within itself; nor do they sufficiently perceive what it contributes either privately or publicly; nor are they able to judge which customs are base or honorable. 

Furthermore, much insolence and **lack of restraint** <note>original: *intemperantia*.</note> is in them: so that it is not at all to be feared that people who eat animals will not be found. For if everyone were to think most excellently, we would have no need for **fowlers** <note>original: *aucupibus*; those who catch birds.</note>, or hunters, or fishermen, or **swineherds** <note>original: *subulcis*.</note>. 

But the animals themselves, governing themselves, if they have no one by whom they are cared for and ruled, will easily perish, and will be consumed by others which attack them, and thus their multitude is diminished. This is what usually happens in the case of innumerable animals which humans do not eat. But if this varied and universal **foolishness** <note>original: *insipientia*.</note> persists among men, there will also be innumerable people who will serve these [cravings of the] **gluttony** <note>original: *gulæ*; the catchword "læ" completes the word "gulæ," meaning the throat or appetite.</note>.

<summary>The author argues that popular belief in the health benefits of meat is as mistaken as the belief that sexual indulgence is healthy. # ON ABSTINENCE

...those devoted to [excesses] gorge themselves. Furthermore, **health** <note>original: *sanitas*</note> is not to be maintained out of a fear of death, but for this reason: so that we are not hindered from attaining those goods that arise from **contemplation** <note>original: *contemplatione*; refers to the philosophical practice of deep, intellectual meditation on truth.</note>. Moreover, its primary preserver is an undisturbed and tranquil state of the **soul** <note>original: *animae*</note>, and the fixed attention of our mind on that which truly exists. 

For there is much from this state that extends even to the physical body, as our friends have shown by experience. There were those suffering from a **joint disease** <note>original: *articulari morbo*; likely gout or severe arthritis.</note> which had seized their hands and feet so violently that for eight whole years they had to be **carried** <note>original: *gestatione*; being transported in a litter or chair because they were unable to walk.</note>; yet they drove out the disease as soon as they cast aside their wealth and devoted themselves to the contemplation of divine things. For together with their money and their cares, they also laid aside the disease of the body. 

Therefore, a great power—indeed, almost every means of bringing health—passes into the body from a certain good state of the soul. This is true even though a reduction in food also contributes largely to the same end. In general, however, as **Epicurus** <note>A Greek philosopher (341–270 BCE) who taught that true pleasure comes from a simple life and freedom from fear and pain, rather than sensory indulgence.</note> used to say correctly, we must avoid that food which we desire and pursue while hungry, but which we judge to be unpleasant once it has been consumed. 

Of this kind, surely, is all food that is luxurious and **rich** <note>original: *crassus*; literally "thick" or "heavy."</note>. This [unpleasantness] usually happens to those who crave such food desperately, as they fall into heavy expenses, diseases, **surfeits** <note>original: *repletiones*; the state of being overfull or gorged.</note>, or distractions. Hence, even in the case of simple and cheap food, overfilling is to be avoided. And everywhere it must be considered...

One must also consider that he who fears **abstinence from animals** <note>original: *abstinentiam ab animalibus*; the practice of vegetarianism for philosophical or spiritual reasons.</note>, if he permits the **eating of meat** <note>original: *esum carnium*</note> for the sake of pleasure, actually fears death. From these and similar causes, an insatiable desire for life, for wealth, for money, and for **glory** <note>original: *gloriæ*; here meaning worldly reputation or status.</note> arises; while they believe that with these things they will increase their well-being for a longer time, they always dread the evil of death as if it were an unending threat.

Such wealth would not only be idle and of no use to us, but would even become a burden, much like the physical discomfort of overeating. From this kind of necessary satiety, a true and sincere pleasure arises. One must become accustomed...

# ON ABSTINENCE

...truly **ignorant** <note>original: *ignoret*; the catchword "igno:" suggests the author is describing a state where the soul is so focused on higher things that it is "ignorant" or unaware of the body's lower impulses.</note>, yet truly his body also is such that it perceives not the filling of hunger, but the pleasure of this satiety, so that it may attain all these things. Let it set a limit not by what is **unbounded** <note>original: *indefinitum*; referring to a lack of limits or restraint in physical desire.</note>, but by what is necessary. For in this way it will happen that the body also, in that which is possible for it, may attain the good through **sufficiency** <note>original: *sufficientia*; the state of having exactly what is needed for life without excess.</note>, and through the likeness it shares with the divine. Thus, the body itself will not desire the use of a longer span of time, as if that would bring any increase to its own good. In this way it truly grows rich, while it measures wealth by a **natural limit** and not by empty opinions.

Thus, it will not always be suspended by the hope of the greatest pleasure—which, as it has no certain outcome, is most turbulent—but it will instead be established in the sufficiency of the present and the past, so that it is not anxious about the future or about how much longer it might endure.

Come now, I ask, how is it not absurd if a man who is afflicted—either oppressed by some heavy external calamity or caught in chains—does not even think of food, nor toil over how it is prepared, but if it is placed before him, takes only what is necessary; yet he who is truly bound, and afflicted by internal calamities, seeks out the preparation of **feasts** <note>original: *epularum*; refers to lavish banquets or formal, indulgent dining.</note> and procures a variety of dishes, by which he only strengthens his own chains and makes them thicker? In what way are these the actions of men who recognize what they have suffered, and not rather of those who take delight in their own suffering and the evils in which they are placed?

<summary>This page argues that a virtuous person should limit bodily desires to what is strictly necessary, mirroring divine self-sufficiency. It contrasts the simple needs of those in physical chains with the excessive, self-imposed indulgences of those enslaved by their own internal passions.</summary>

# BOOK I.

...they are **ignorant** <note>original: *ignorent*; continuing the thought that those enslaved to luxury do not realize their own spiritual state.</note>: for to these people, calamity truly turns out in the opposite way than it does for those who, although they are bound, recognize their own misery. For since their present state of life is unpleasant to them, and they are agitated by an insatiable disturbance, they desire things that are absent until they are filled. Indeed, there is no one among those who have lavished themselves in every abundance of luxury and variety who is led to desire silver tables and couches, and **ointments** <note>original: *ungnenta*; expensive perfumes or oils used for grooming</note>, and cooks, and household furniture, and garments, and sumptuous dinners because he has calmed all his inner disturbances. Instead, they are accustomed to come to this state because of the unpleasantness of their present life, the unending pursuit of material goods, and an unquenchable inner turmoil. For this reason, the former [the afflicted] do not remember [to eat] while they are occupied with repelling present evils; but the latter, while they ungratefully enjoy what they have, strive to acquire for themselves what is absent. 

But the person devoted to **contemplation** pursues a **meagerness of diet** <note>original: *tenuitatem uictus*; a simple, light way of eating</note> for both reasons. For he knows in what things the bond [of the soul to the body] consists, so that he is unable to desire luxury. And since he delights in **parsimony** <note>original: *parsimonia*; here meaning philosophical frugality or simplicity</note>, he by no means seeks the **eating of living things** <note>original: *animatorum esum*; the consumption of meat/animals</note>, as if he were not content with that food which is obtained from inanimate things. Add to this that even if the nature of the body in a **philosopher** <note>original: *philosopho*; a lover of wisdom who practices self-discipline</note> were not such that it could be so easily managed and cared for by whatever cheap things are at hand; but if it were necessary that we undergo pains and troubles for the sake of true **health** <note>original: *salus*; implies both physical health and spiritual well-being/salvation</note>: should we not still do exactly the same? ...to be freed from disease, we must endure so many things: being **cut** <note>original: *secamur*; referring to surgical procedures</note>, being **bled** <note>original: *cruentamur*; the practice of bloodletting</note>, being **cauterized** <note>original: *adurimur*; burning tissue to treat a wound or infection</note>, swallowing bitter medicines, and being purged through the bowels, through vomiting, and through the nostrils. On top of all this, we must even pay fees to those who treat us in this way. 

If this is true for the body, then for the sake of our **internal disease** <note>spiritual or moral corruption</note>—where our struggle is for immortality and for achieving communion with God (from which we are blocked by our habit of favoring the body)—should we not endure everything, even if it were necessary to suffer pain and afflictions? We certainly should not follow the laws of the body, which are violent and stand in opposition to the laws of the mind and the paths of salvation. 

But since we are now philosophizing not about enduring pain, but about rejecting **unnecessary pleasures**, what further excuse remains for those who impudently wish to defend their own **intemperance** <note>original: *intemperantiam*; a lack of self-control, particularly regarding food and drink</note>? For if we wish to speak freely and without fear, we can reach our goal in no other way except by being **fastened** <note>original: *affixi*</note> to God and **unfastened** <note>original: *refixi*; Porphyry uses a wordplay here to suggest that the soul is currently "nailed" to the body and its desires</note> from the body and its pleasures. This is because salvation is acquired through our works and deeds, not simply by hearing words. 

Moreover, no one can be reconciled to God—not even to any of the lesser deities, let alone to the One who exists before all things and absolutely above every **incorporeal nature** <note>spiritual or non-physical existence</note>—through just any diet, and certainly not by the eating of meat. Instead, it is only through various **purifications** <note>original: *castimoniis*; ritual or moral cleansings</note> of both soul and body that a person who is of noble character and lives a holy and pure life can barely be deemed worthy so that his **senses**...

### BOOK I.
<meta>warning: Significant ink bleed-through from the reverse side of the page is visible on the bottom half of the sheet, appearing as mirrored text.</meta>

...to have someone. Therefore, in as much as that **Father of all** <note>original: *omnium pater*; referring to the Supreme Being or the Neoplatonic "One"</note> is more simple, pure, and self-sufficient—existing far removed from all material representation—so much more is it necessary for the person who intends to approach Him to be pure and chaste in every respect. This kind of **holiness** <term>sanctimonia: a state of ritual and moral purity</term> should begin with the body and culminate in the inner self, attributing to each of its parts—or to all those elements present within us—the specific purity that is appropriate to its nature. 

Perhaps no one will argue against these points. However, an objection will surely be raised: If we have determined that **abstinence** <term>abstinentiam: specifically the refraining from eating animal flesh</term> is so holy, why then has it been our custom to slaughter both sheep and oxen in **sacrifices** <note>original: *sacrificiis*</note> to the **gods** <note>original: *deorum*</note>? And why do we consider such **immolations** <note>original: *immolationes*; the ritual killing of victims</note> to be not only pious and holy, but also pleasing to the gods? 

Because we require a somewhat more lengthy discourse to resolve these questions, we must take up a new starting point to thoroughly examine the nature of sacrifices.

<summary>Porphyry argues that the purity of a seeker must mirror the simplicity of the Divine, then introduces the central conflict of his work: if meat-eating is impure, why do traditional religions require animal sacrifice?</summary>

# PORPHYRY ON
# ABSTINENCE FROM THE EATING
## OF ANIMALS
## BOOK TWO.

->**Translated by Giovanni Bernardo Feliciano**<-

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal woodcut ornament featuring scrolling foliage and a central floral motif.</image-desc>

<image-desc>Historiated woodcut initial 'Q' depicting two figures, possibly in a room or a landscape.</image-desc>

Continuing our inquiry into the **simplicity and purity of diet** <term>uictus tenuitate & puritate: referring to the ascetic lifestyle of a philosopher</term>, O Castricius <note>Firmus Castricius, a friend of Porphyry who had abandoned vegetarianism, prompting this work</note>, let us now come to the discussion regarding **sacrifices** <note>original: *sacrificiis*</note>. This is a subject that is difficult to judge and requires a great deal of explanation, if it is to be explained truly and in a manner pleasing to the gods. Therefore, since this line of consideration—which has been deferred until now—has finally found its proper place, we shall now state our views on the matter and express them to the best of our ability. First, however, I must correct and rectify an error that commonly arises in this argument. We assert that it does not necessarily follow that because animals are killed, they must also be eaten. Nor does someone who concedes the former point—that animals may be slaughtered—immediately establish that they should be consumed as food. For example, the laws permit us to take vengeance on invading enemies; of them— <note>The text ends mid-word with the catchword "rum," completing "eorum," likely referring to the fact that while we kill enemies, we do not eat them.</note>

<summary>Porphyry opens Book II by addressing the logical gap between the ritual slaughter of animals and the consumption of their meat, arguing that the former does not justify the latter. He dedicates this section to his friend Castricius to explain the true nature of sacrifice.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, Castricius, animal sacrifice, ritual purity, vegetarianism, Neoplatonism, ethics of killing, Giovanni Bernardo Feliciano</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the end of the previous page, where Porphyry noted that while laws allow us to kill enemies, we do not eat them. Here, he extends this logic to the ritual of sacrifice, distinguishing between the act of killing an animal for the gods and the act of consuming it as food.</meta>

### BOOK II.

...their flesh to eat is judged to be far from human nature. Furthermore, it does not follow that if it is appropriate to sacrifice some living being <note>original: *animatum aliquod*</note> to demons, or gods, or certain powers for specific reasons—whether known or unknown to men—we must therefore also feast upon those animals. Indeed, it can be demonstrated that both humans and certain animals are taken for **sacrifices** whose flesh no one would endure to taste, not even those who are accustomed to eating meat.

Moreover, the same error occurs regarding the killing of animals. For if some animals must be killed, it does not follow that all must be killed; nor, if this is done to brute animals, should the same necessarily be done to humans. And **abstinence from living beings**, as we have already stated in the first book, is not commanded absolutely for all people, but for **philosophers**, and especially for those who have placed their happiness in God and in the imitation of Him <note>The Neoplatonic ideal of "likeness to God" (homoiosis theo) as the ultimate goal of the soul</note>.

For even **legislators** have not decreed that the same duties should be performed by the common people and the **priests** within the community of the city. While they have granted many indulgences to the majority regarding diet and the general conduct of life, they have forbidden the priests from these same things, often under the threat of the death penalty or a heavy **fine** <term>mulcta: a financial penalty or forfeit used in Roman law</term>. Therefore, if these distinctions are not confused but are understood with the proper discernment they require, most of the arguments brought forward by our opponents will be found to be empty. For many animals are to be killed either because of the harm they inflict—

<summary>Porphyry argues that the ritual sacrifice of an animal does not mandate its consumption as food, noting that even human sacrifices were never meant to be eaten. He emphasizes that strict dietary abstinence is a specific requirement for philosophers and priests, rather than a universal law for all citizens.</summary>

<keywords>animal sacrifice, ritual purity, Neoplatonism, priestly duties, philosophy, dietary laws, abstinence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument from the previous page, where he emphasized that the rules for common people and priests regarding meat-eating are distinct. Here, he begins to dismantle the logical fallacies used by his opponents to justify meat-eating for everyone, particularly for philosophers.</meta>

# ON ABSTINENCE
5b

...ought to be distinguished <note>The transcription begins mid-word, likely "distinguenda" (distinguished), following the previous page's discussion on discernment.</note>, either because it is assumed that [eating meat] follows as a consequence of its being killed, or because **animals** are also commonly used for **sacrifices**, from which they reason that they must also be eaten by humans. 

And likewise, if certain animals must be removed from our midst due to their wildness, they think it follows that tame animals must also be killed. And if it is permitted for some—such as **athletes**, **soldiers**, and those who perform manual labor—to feed on flesh, they think the same should be allowed for **philosophers**. 

And if they judge it necessary that these [permissions] apply to everyone, they are mistaken; for all such conclusions are both distorted and provide no logical necessity for establishing any rule. The flaws in these arguments are plain to see for anyone who is not simply looking for an argument. 

Therefore, since we have already refuted some of these errors and will go on to disprove others as our discussion proceeds, we shall now turn our attention to explaining this consideration of **sacrifices**. We shall explain their ultimate origin: which ones existed first, and what they were like; in what way they were changed, and when. Then, we will consider whether all things should be sacrificed by a **philosopher**, to whom animal sacrifices are offered, and finally, we will relate everything connected to these matters—partly based on our own reasoning and partly inherited from the ancients—while maintaining, as much as possible, a moderation best suited to our proposed argument.
