# BOOK II.

H

<meta>catchword: let them yield,</meta>

Therefore, it happened in this manner. It was almost an uncountable time ago since the most learned race of all men—as **Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher and student of Aristotle; his work "On Piety" is the likely source here.</note> says—who inhabited the most holy region founded by the **Nile**, were the first to begin sacrificing to the celestial gods from the hearth and home. They did not make offerings of myrrh, nor cassia, nor frankincense mixed with saffron; for these were adopted many ages later, which was the first step toward error. This occurred when man, placed in a state of wanting for his necessary food, offered "drops" of these resins to the gods with much labor and tears. 

But originally, they did not sacrifice such things, but rather the herb itself, which they had plucked with their hands like a kind of soft down of fertile nature. For the earth brought forth trees before it brought forth animals; and likewise, it produced the herb, which arises in annual budding, much earlier than it sent forth the trees. Plucking the leaves and roots, and indeed even the whole stalks themselves, they burned them in the flames. By this sacrifice, they honored the celestial gods who were visible <note>Refers to the sun, moon, and stars, which were often the first objects of worship in ancient "natural" religion.</note>, and they consecrated honors to their immortality with fire. For we are also accustomed to preserve an immortal fire in our temples for these gods, since fire is most like them. 

From the burning <note>original: "suffitu"</note> of those things which were set on fire from the earth—that is, from <term>burning incense</term> <note>original Greek: θυμιατήριον (thymiaterion); a term for a censer or incense burner.</note>—they derived the names for <term>censers</term> <note>original Greek: θυμιατήρια (thymiateria)</note>, as if you were to say "vessels for burning," as well as the verb <term>to sacrifice</term> <note>original Greek: θύειν (thyein)</note> and the word for <term>sacrifices</term> <note>original Greek: θυσίας (thysias)</note>. These are the things which we, as if a later sin...

<meta>The previous page ended with "From the burning of those things which were set on fire from...", leading into the idea that we misinterpret the meaning of sacrifice.</meta>

...yield, we do not interpret correctly when we call the worship shown to the gods through the slaughter of animals a **sacrifice** <note>original Greek: θυσίαν (*thysian*), a word Porphyry suggests is being misapplied to blood-offerings.</note>. Indeed, those ancients took such great care that the old customs were not violated that they used a curse against those who abandoned the old ways to introduce new ones. They called the very things we now use for incense <term>aromatics</term> <note>original Greek: ἀρώματα (*aromata*), which Porphyry links here to a word for "curses" or "execrations."</note>. Anyone can still observe this ancient practice of burning offerings, as many people today still make a habit of burning pieces of scented wood for the gods. 

Thus, after that first appearance of herbs, when the earth began to produce trees and humans first fed upon the acorns of the **oak**, they offered the leaves of the tree in sacrifice, saving the fruit itself because food was so scarce. Later, however, when life had transitioned to a more civilized diet and the era of the oak was deemed "sufficient" <note>This refers to the ancient Greek proverb "enough of the oak," signaling the transition from primitive foraging to the invention of agriculture.</note>, they began to sacrifice nuts. 

When **barley** first appeared among the crops of **Ceres** <note>The goddess of agriculture (Demeter).</note> after the era of legumes, mankind used the whole grain, sprinkled with water, for their sacrifices; they called these offerings <term>barley-groats</term> <note>original Greek: οὐλοχύτας (*oulochytas*)</note>. Later, once they had learned to crush and grind the grain into food, they hid the tools used for this work in a secret place and venerated them as sacred objects, because these tools had brought divine assistance to human life. From this ground meal—which they considered more blessed than all previous foods—they [offered] to the gods in the fire...

### BOOK II.

...they made these offerings <note>original: *oblationes*</note>. This is why, even today, at the end of our drink offerings <note>original: *libationum*</note>, we still have the custom of using ground-up sacrificial meal. In doing this, we bear witness to that ancient and growing way of sustaining life, even if we no longer understand the specific reasons why we perform each of these actions. 

As we progressed from that point, and as wheat <note>original: *tritico*</note> became more abundantly available along with other crops, the "first-fruits" of wheat-porridge and all other harvests were brought to the gods as sacrifices. Since men gathered flowers from many places and mixed other things together—whatever they found beautiful in life or whatever offered a scent that might reach the divine senses—some of these were used for wreaths, while others were cast into the fire to be burned. 

Once they had also discovered the divine drops of wine and honey, as well as oil for human use, they performed sacred rites to the gods who had provided them. The procession of the **Sun** and the **Seasons** <note>original: *horarum*; the Greek *Horai* governed the seasons and the orderly progression of time.</note>, which is celebrated in **Athens** to this day, seems to testify to all of this. In this procession, it is customary to carry various herbs and grass, as well as oak leaves, berries from the strawberry-tree <note>original: *arbuti baccæ*</note>, sorb-apples, wheat, barley, bundles of dried figs, cakes made from wheat and barley flour, sweetmeats, and even pots of legumes. 

However, while the offerings made in these sacrifices were far removed from any injustice, the introduction of "wicked victims" <note>referring to the slaughter of animals</note> followed, a practice full of cruelty. Thus, the curses and execrations <note>original: *detestationes & execrationes*</note> mentioned previously seem to have found their fulfillment against us, once men [began to sacrifice] living souls...

<header>ON ABSTINENCE</header>

...they slaughtered <note>original: *iugularint*; continuing from the previous page's "animalia."</note> animals and defiled the altars with blood. From that point, having first experienced famine and war, they began to be stained with blood themselves. Wherefore the Divinity of the gods, as **Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher and student of Aristotle who wrote extensively on botany and piety.</note> says, being angered, seems to have brought a fitting punishment upon both groups. For some men became godless <note>original: *ἄθεοι* (atheoi), meaning "without gods" or "estranged from the divine."</note>, which is to say, they were utterly alienated from the gods; others became evil-minded <note>original: *κακόφρονες* (kakophrones)</note> or, as they might rightly be called, "perverse-theists" <note>original: *κακόθεοι* (kakotheoi), those holding wicked views of the gods</note>, because they believed the gods to be lowly and in no way superior to us in nature. Consequently, some appeared to be entirely non-sacrificing <note>original: *ἄθυται* (athytai)</note>, for they dedicated no first-fruits of any kind to the gods; others were wicked-sacrificers <note>original: *κακόθυτοι* (kakothytai)</note>, meaning they offered in a perverse manner and employed unlawful sacrifices. For this reason, the **Thoes**, who lived on the borders of **Thrace**, were wiped out from among men because they offered nothing and performed no sacrifices. They vanished so suddenly that no one could find any inhabitants, nor a city, nor even the traces of their houses.

### BOOK II.

They consumed the flesh of slaughtered humans for food, no differently than we do now with other animals when we turn the remains of sacrifices into feasts. Who is unaware of how they were furiously incited against one another, and, attacking each other with bites, did not truly stop feeding on blood until they had destroyed the race of those who had first introduced such sacrifices among them? Therefore, the sacrifice offered through animals is a later and more recent practice; it arose not from a cause that was pleasing, as was the case with the offering of fruits, but from famine or some other calamity.

For the causes of the specific slaughters <term>mactationum</term> <note>From the Latin *mactatio*, referring to the ritual killing of a sacrificial victim.</note> among the **Athenians** are attributed either to ignorance, anger, or fear. For example, they attribute the slaughter of a sow to a non-voluntary error by **Clymene** <note>A figure in Greek lore; her accidental killing of a pig is cited here as a legal and religious precedent for animal sacrifice.</note>, who killed the animal unintentionally when she had aimed her weapons. They say that her husband, terrified by religious dread as if an impious act had been committed, went to **Delphi** <note>The site of the most famous oracle of Apollo, consulted for purification and legal-religious advice.</note> to consult the oracle on the matter. When the god appeared to have pardoned the occurrence, they say the act was thereafter no longer considered a crime.

Likewise, they say that the oracle gave permission to a priest of the lineage of seers who wished to sacrifice sheep, though it did so with great religious caution; for the oracle sang in this manner:

> It is not lawful for you to slaughter the offspring of sheep, [which are the...] of the pious <note>original: *Non tibi fas ovium sobolem mactare, piorum*. The Latin text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

# ON ABSTINENCE

...the offspring of prophets: but that animal which falls of its own accord, the priest will be permitted by right to bring to your altars. <note>original: "Progenies uatum: sed quæ sponte occubet, illá Antistes inferre aris tibi iure licebit." This appears to be a poetic or oracular response regarding which animals are "legal" to sacrifice.</note> Indeed, in **Icarius** <note>A village or "deme" in Attica associated with the introduction of wine.</note> in **Attica**, they first killed a goat because it had nibbled on a grapevine. Likewise, **Diomus**, being a priest of **Jupiter [Zeus] Polieus** <note>Zeus "of the City," the protector of the Athenian acropolis.</note>, is said to have been the first to slaughter an ox on an occasion of this sort: When the **Diipolia** <note>An ancient Athenian festival characterized by the "Bouphonia" or "ox-slaying," which involved a ritualized trial of the knife used to kill the ox.</note>—that is, the festival of **Jupiter**—was being celebrated, and the grain products were prepared for it according to ancient custom, an ox approached and tasted the sacred porridge <term>pulte</term> <note>A thick mash or porridge, often made of emmer or barley, which was a staple food and common offering before the rise of meat sacrifice.</note>. But the priest, assisted by the others who were present, killed the animal.

And among the **Athenians**, these specific causes are asserted; among other peoples, other reasons are usually told. Yet all of these accounts are full of justifications that are by no means pious. For most people blame hunger and the injustice that arises from it as the cause; they argue that because they were forced to eat living creatures, they sacrificed them to the gods—despite the fact that they had previously been accustomed to abstain from eating them. 

Hence, since the custom of such sacrifices is seen to be no older than the time when food from animals became a necessity, it should sufficiently demonstrate to men that animals ought to be excluded from what is considered edible. Nor is it necessary for us to accept as "pious" those practices of eating and sacrificing simply because they did so, for they did not offer these things to the gods in a truly pious manner. That every ritual of this kind took its origin from a sense of injustice is especially indicated by the fact that not all nations sacrifice or eat the same things; rather, they do so based on custom...

### BOOK II.

...from their own customs, people have been accustomed to judge what is right and what is a duty. For among the **Egyptians** and **Phoenicians**, they would sooner taste human flesh than that of a female cow. The cause of this is that there was a great scarcity among them of this animal, which is extremely useful. Therefore, while they both ate bulls and slaughtered them in sacrifices, they spared the females for the sake of breeding; they decreed it to be a sacrilege <term>piaculum</term> <note>A sin or crime requiring a ritual purification or atonement.</note> if anyone laid hands upon them. In this matter, it was undoubtedly custom and utility that established what was considered piety and impiety within one and the same genus of cattle. 

Since things are so, **Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle, whose lost work "On Piety" is a major source for this text's arguments against animal sacrifice.</note> rightly forbids those who truly wish to behave piously from sacrificing animals, for several other reasons: first, because—as we have said—they only began sacrificing them when some greater necessity attacked. For hunger and wars were the cause, bringing about the necessity of eating them. Since, therefore, the fruits of the earth <term>fruges</term> <note>Specifically grain, pulses, and other agricultural products, often seen as the "civilized" food of humanity.</note> are now available to us, what need is there finally to employ a sacrifice born of necessity? 

Next, because since rewards and other thanks for benefits should be returned to others according to the merit of their generosity: to those who have conferred the greatest benefits upon us, the greatest rewards must be returned, and from the most precious things—especially if they themselves provided those things to us. Moreover, the greatest and most precious things with which the gods favor us are the fruits of the earth themselves; for by these they both preserve us and allow us to lead our lives according to law. With these, therefore, to worship...

Now, the soul is far more precious than those things which spring from the earth; therefore, it is not fitting to deprive animals of it so that we may sacrifice.

# BOOK II.
I
<meta>catchword: from</meta>

The produce <note>The Latin catchword from the previous page was *ctus*, completing the word *productus*.</note> of bees, because it also arises from our own labors, is therefore fittingly seen as a shared benefit. For while the bees themselves gather honey by sipping from flowers here and there, we in turn provide them with care. Thus, the utility of the honey is justly divided so that the bees themselves suffer no harm. Indeed, that which is not a necessity for them provides many uses for us; it may be seen as a kind of wage <note>original Latin: *merces*</note> taken from them in exchange for our protection. 

Therefore, one must abstain from using animals in sacrifices. Furthermore, since all other things belong to the gods, agricultural fruits <note>original: *fructus*</note> seem to be truly ours, because it is we who sow them, plant them, and nourish them through our various efforts. We ought, therefore, to sacrifice from what is ours, not from what belongs to another. 

Moreover, that which can be obtained at a small cost is holier and more pleasing to the gods than that which is expensive. It is also easier for those performing the sacrifice, as it allows them to practice piety <term>pietas</term> <note>In the Roman sense, *pietas* refers to the performance of duties toward the gods, family, and homeland.</note> continually. However, that which is neither holy nor obtainable at a low cost should not be used in sacrifices at all, even if it is available. 

That animals are not among those things easily or cheaply acquired can be clearly seen if we look at the majority of mankind. We should not, at this moment, consider only those few who might abound in sheep or oxen. For first, there are many nations that possess none of the animals suitable for sacrifice, except perhaps those of a very lowly sort <note>original: *ignobilia*, meaning common or non-pedigreed animals.</note>; and secondly, that...

# ON ABSTINENCE

Even among those who live in cities, most suffer from a scarcity of these things <note>referring to livestock/animals mentioned on the previous page</note>. For even if someone were to claim a shortage of our own cultivated fruits, they would still not lack other things that spring from the earth; nor will it be as difficult to obtain fruits as it is to acquire animals. Therefore, the acquisition of fruits and things growing from the earth is easier than that of animals. That which is easier and valued at a lower cost contributes to a constant and universal practice of piety <term>pietas</term> <note>In this context, *pietas* refers to the proper performance of religious duties and a respectful attitude toward the divine.</note>. 

The very outcome of historical events testifies that these humble offerings please the gods more than those requiring great expense. For in olden times, when a certain Thessalian <note>Likely referring to a wealthy individual from Thessaly, a region in Greece known for its agriculture and horses.</note> was sacrificing oxen with gilded horns and entire hecatombs <note>A "hecatomb" traditionally refers to the sacrifice of one hundred oxen.</note> to Pythian Apollo, the Pythia <note>The high priestess of the Temple of Apollo at Delphi who delivered oracles.</note> asserted that a man from Hermione had pleased the god more—a man who had merely offered small cakes taken with three fingers from a simple basket. When the Thessalian, upset by this reply, threw all his remaining wealth onto the altar fire to outdo the other, the priestess spoke again, saying that by doing so he had made himself twice as ungrateful as he had previously been pleasing. Thus, that which is easily obtained <note>original Latin: *parabile*</note> is what pleases the gods; the divine power looks more to the mind and the character of those sacrificing than to the abundance of the victims.

Theopompus <note>A Greek historian of the 4th century BCE.</note> also recorded similar accounts. He says there was a certain man from Magnesia in Asia who was extremely wealthy. Because he possessed a vast flock of cattle, he was accustomed to performing many magnificent sacrifices to the gods every year, partly due to his great wealth and partly due to his piety, for he strove exceedingly to please the gods. This man...

## BOOK II

[He], being thus disposed toward the god <note>Likely referring to a wealthy man from Magnesia, mentioned in the broader context of Porphyry's *On Abstinence*, who sought validation for his expensive piety.</note>, set out for Delphi with sacrifices. When he had honored Apollo there with a magnificent procession of hecatombs <term>hecatomb</term> <note>A "hecatomb" was a grand public sacrifice, traditionally involving one hundred oxen.</note>, he entered the temple to consult the oracle. Since he judged that he was offering the most distinguished worship to the gods of all men, he asked the Pythia <note>The high priestess at Delphi who delivered the oracles of Apollo.</note> who it was that worshiped the god best and most readily, and who offered him the most pleasing sacrifices. He fully expected that the prize in this matter would be granted to him without a doubt. 

However, the prophetess replied that Clearchus, who lived in Methydrium (a town in Arcadia <note>A rural, mountainous region of Greece often associated in literature with rustic simplicity and ancient customs.</note>), worshiped the gods best of all. He was wonderfully amazed at this and took it into his mind to travel there to meet the man, so that he might inquire from him in what manner he performed his sacrifices. 

When he had quickly reached Methydrium, he first looked upon the smallness and humbleness of the town and so despised the place that he did not believe any private person from there—nor even the whole city together—could possibly worship the gods more nobly or magnificently than he did himself. Nonetheless, he approached the man and asked him to explain the way in which he worshiped the gods.

Clearchus responded in this way: "I always sacrifice to the gods with the greatest diligence at the appointed times. On the new moons <note>The start of the lunar month was a traditional time for household sacrifices in ancient Greece.</note> of every single month, I crown <note>original: "corono"; the practice of placing floral wreaths or garlands upon the statues of the gods.</note> Mercury <note>The Roman name for the Greek god Hermes, messenger of the gods and protector of the home.</note>, and Hecate <note>A goddess associated with boundaries, crossroads, and protection.</note>, and the rest of the gods whom our ancestors left to us,

->ii I<-

<meta>catchword: To the gods</meta>

...and I adorn [their statues], and offer sacred rites to them with incense, sacrificial cakes, and pastries. Every year I celebrate public offerings, ensuring that I never omit any solemn festival. In these rites, I do not seek to appease the gods by slaughtering oxen or cutting the throats of victims, but with whatever humble things are easily at hand. Above all, I make it my study to return the first fruits to the gods from all the seasonal crops that the earth provides for me. Some of these I take for my own food, while others I am accustomed to burn on their altars. Since I am satisfied with having just enough for my needs, I have never been able to bring myself to sacrifice oxen. 

It has also been recorded in the literary records of some writers that after the Carthaginians were defeated, certain tyrants <note>Likely referring to the rulers of Syracuse, such as Gelon or Hiero, who won a famous victory over the Carthaginians at the Battle of Himera in 480 BCE.</note> engaged in great rivalry and competition with one another. They brought magnificent hecatombs <term>hecatomb</term> <note>A "hecatomb" was a grand public sacrifice, traditionally involving the slaughter of one hundred oxen.</note> to Apollo and asked the god which of them he took the most pleasure in. Contrary to all their expectations, the god replied that the sacrificial cakes of Docimus were most acceptable to him. This Docimus was a resident of a rugged, harsh place in Delphi. On that same day, he had come into the city from his field and had offered a few handfuls of meal from a pouch hanging at his side. With these, he had delighted the god more than those who had presented sacrifices with the most magnificent pageantry. Because of the clarity of this truth, even some of the poets seem to have asserted similar things, just as Antiphanes <note>A celebrated Athenian comic playwright of the 4th century BCE known for Middle Comedy.</note> discusses in his play *The Mystics*:

<summary>Clearchus explains that his piety consists of bloodless offerings of first fruits and cakes, followed by an anecdote where the Oracle prefers the simple flour offering of a poor laborer over the lavish animal sacrifices of wealthy tyrants.</summary>

### BOOK II.

->I iii<-

<meta>catchword: ancient</meta>

For the gods take pleasure in the humble simplicity of things. Providing an argument for this very matter, one poet <note>original: "Nam cū boum, inquit, de grege sacrū quis fecerit..." — This is likely a fragment from a lost Greek drama, translated here into Latin by the scribe/editor.</note> says:
> For when someone makes a sacrifice from a herd of oxen,
> The vapor of incense is added to the altars 
> At the very end: so that among the great abundance of other things, 
> Nothing might be preferred but the glory of the givers,
> And their vain expenses: that which is offered in the last place, 
> Even if very little, is seen to please the gods.

Furthermore, Menander in *The Grouch*—that is the name of the play—says the following:
> From here, a pious offering of incense and libations,
> All of which the god accepts when they are cast into the flames.

For this very reason, they were accustomed to use vessels made of clay, wood, and wicker, especially for public sacrifices, believing that the gods themselves were delighted by them. Because of this, even now, the oldest vessels which were made of clay or wood are considered more divine, both because of their material and the simplicity of the craftsmanship. For they say that when Aeschylus <note>A famous Athenian tragic playwright (c. 525–456 BCE).</note> was asked by his brothers <note>The Latin "fratres" may refer to his literal brothers or, more likely, his fellow citizens or members of a religious guild.</note> to write a paean <term>paean</term> <note>A "paean" was a formal song of praise or healing, usually dedicated to Apollo.</note> in honor of the god, he said that the best ones had already been written by Tynnichus. He claimed that if his own works were compared to the hymns of Tynnichus, the result would be no different than if new statues were compared to old ones. For the old statues, even though they were fashioned in a simple manner, were held to be divine; the new ones, however accurately and elaborately worked, were indeed objects of admiration for their artistry, but they carried a far lesser reputation for divinity within them. And for that reason, Hesiod <note>A Greek poet active around the same time as Homer, known for "Works and Days."</note> is rightly...

# ON ABSTINENCE

...recommending the law of ancient sacrifices, it was said: "The ancient law is the best for the people to perform a holy rite" <note>original: "Vt populus sacrum faciat, uetus optima lex est."</note>. But those who have written about the performance of sacred rites and sacrifices prescribe that diligence should be maintained in libations <term>libation</term> <note>A liquid offering, such as wine or oil, poured out to a deity.</note>, since these are far more pleasing to the gods than the sacrifice which is brought from the slaughter of animals. Likewise, Sophocles, describing a sacrifice pleasing to the gods in his play *Polyidus* <note>A lost tragedy by Sophocles concerning the seer Polyidus.</note>, speaks thus:

> For the wool of a sheep was offered, and there was a libation
> Of the vine, of grapes, and of well-stored juice,
> And a mixture of various fruits;
> There was the liquid of the rich olive, and the honeycomb
> Of the bee, most skillfully wrought in the wax of the hive.

Furthermore, the ancient monuments in Delos <note>A sacred island in Greece, the mythological birthplace of Apollo and Artemis.</note>, from the sheaf-bearing Hyperboreans <note>original: "hyperboreis manipuligeris" — referring to a mythical northern people who sent offerings of grain to Apollo's shrine.</note>, were held in great reverence. It is necessary, therefore, that we approach the gods with purified character, offering them pleasing sacrifices of this kind, rather than expensive ones. Now, however, if we should be clothed in a splendid garment but with an unclean body, people think this is not enough for the purity of sacrifices. But if, when someone has made the body clean along with the clothing, but approaches to offer sacrifices with a soul not yet purified from vices, they think it makes no difference: as if God does not take pleasure above all in that which is most divine in us—the soul—provided it is purely disposed, seeing as it is akin to Him. Indeed, in Epidaurus <note>The site of the most famous healing sanctuary of the ancient world, dedicated to Asclepius.</note>, this inscription <note>original: "proscriptio"</note> was displayed at the temple:

### BOOK II.

It is necessary that everyone who enters a sacred temple be pure. But to be pure is to desire holy things in the soul. That God is delighted not by the abundance and size of sacrifices, but by a pious simplicity <term>pious simplicity</term> <note>original: "pia tenuitate" — the idea that a humble, sincere offering is worth more than an expensive, showy one.</note>, can be clearly seen from this: that from our daily food, whatever is served, before we taste anything, we have all been accustomed to offer a libation <term>libation</term> <note>A ritual pouring of a liquid as an offering to a deity.</note> to the gods. It is indeed a small thing, but of that small thing there is the greatest estimation of all.

Furthermore, when Theophrastus <note>A student of Aristotle who wrote extensively on botany and religion; here he is cited regarding the history of sacrifice.</note> has shown from many ancestral customs of various peoples that in ancient times sacrifices were accustomed to be made from fruits, and even earlier from herbs, he then explains the rite of libations in this manner: "The ancient sacrifices among most people were 'sober.' Those are sober which involve a libation of water. Later, the libation of honey followed; for we first received this liquid fruit prepared by the bees. Then it was offered with oil. Finally, last of all, with wine." These things are not only confirmed by the testimony of legal tablets—which are in truth certain examples of the Corybantic <term>Corybantic rites</term> <note>Wild, rhythmic rituals associated with the Cretan deities who guarded the infant Zeus.</note> rites from Crete—but are also confirmed by Empedocles <note>A Greek philosopher who famously argued that the universe is governed by the forces of Love and Strife.</note>; who, when speaking of sacrifices and the nature of the gods, sings thus:

> For among them there was no Mars <note>The god of war.</note>, nor any tumult,
> Nor Neptune, nor Saturn, nor any Jupiter,
> But Cypris <note>An epithet for Aphrodite, the goddess of love.</note> was queen; she who is indeed Friendship.

afterward he adds

# ON ABSTINENCE

> To her <note>The goddess Cypris, or Aphrodite, representing Love and Harmony in the Golden Age described by the philosopher Empedocles.</note> they brought sacrifices with various painted images,
> And they sprinkled the altars with fragrant ointments,
> They made offerings with pure myrrh and sweet-smelling incense,
> While the earth was soaked with poured libations of honey. 

This is something that even now is preserved among some people as a certain trace of the truth <note>original: "ueritatis uestigium" — Porphyry argues that modern rituals still contain echoes of the original, non-violent worship of the gods.</note>.

> But the altar never bristled with the slaughter of bulls

For since friendship, clearly, and a shared feeling toward kin held sway over everything, no one killed anything, believing that other animals were related <term>kinship</term> <note>original: "cognata" — the Pythagorean and Neoplatonic idea that all living beings share a common soul or biological origin.</note> to them. But after Mars <note>The god of war, symbolizing the onset of strife and the end of the peaceful Golden Age.</note> and tumult, and every occasion for strife and war broke in: then for the first time it came about that no one abstained from the slaughter of their kin. Indeed, these things should also be considered in this way. For just as we believe that, although we share a kinship with other humans, those evildoers who are driven by a certain impulse of their own nature and wickedness to inflict harm on anyone should all be killed and punished: so perhaps it is appropriate to kill those among the unreasoning animals which are unjust by their own nature and harmful, being incited to injure and attack the human race. But to kill the others, which are endowed with no such cruelty for harming, is undoubtedly unjust, no different than killing men who are not harmful. This certainly seems to signify that it is by no means the case that no law <term>law/justice</term> <note>original: "ius" — here referring to the concept of "natural law" or moral rights that might extend to animals.</note> exists between us and the other animals

<summary>Citing the poet Empedocles, the text describes an ancient era of bloodless sacrifice and universal kinship, arguing that while harmful animals may be killed like human criminals, killing harmless animals is a violation of natural justice.</summary>

->### BOOK II.<-

...creature, because <note>original: "propterea q [quod]"</note> some of them are harmful and malicious, while others are not at all, just as is the case among humans. Should those animals which ought to be killed, therefore, be sacrificed to the gods? But how is that divinely permitted, since they are of a wicked nature? For it is no more appropriate to offer these than those which are maimed and injured. We would be making sacrifices from the firstfruits <term>primitias</term> <note>original: "primitias" — literally the first portion of a harvest or flock set aside for a deity; Porphyry argues that offering "bad" things is a contradiction of this holy concept.</note> of evils, not for the sake of honor. 

But if animals must indeed be slaughtered for the gods, then only those that do no harm should be slaughtered. However, it is established that those which do no harm ought not to be killed; therefore, they must not be sacrificed to the gods. But if neither these nor the malicious ones should be sacrificed, is it not clear that, above all, one must abstain from animals, and no sacrifice should be made from them, even if some of them must be killed? 

Add to this that there are three reasons why it seems sacrifice should be offered to the gods. For we believe that offerings should be made to the gods—just as to good men—either for the sake of honor, or gratitude, or out of a need for blessings. We honor the gods either when we strive to be freed from evils and prepared to attain good things; or when, having received some benefit, we conduct ourselves gratefully; or for the purpose of obtaining some profit; or led solely by the veneration of their goodness. Wherefore, if animals are to be offered to the gods, they must be sacrificed for some such reason; since we are accustomed to make sacrifices to the gods for the sake of one of these things. Is it then the case that honor from us...

<summary>Porphyry presents a logical dilemma: if an animal is harmful, it is an unworthy "firstfruit" of evil for the gods; if it is harmless, it is unjust to kill it, rendering animal sacrifice ethically and religiously impossible. He then outlines the three traditional motivations for sacrifice: honor, gratitude, and petition.</summary>

# ON ABSTINENCE

...solemnities, will God consider that He is receiving anything **from us**, if we appear to be acting unjustly right from the very moment of our offering? Or will He not instead consider Himself to be insulted by such a sacrifice? Now, if in the act of sacrificing we slaughter animals that have caused no offense, it is already admitted that we are acting unjustly. Therefore, for the sake of showing **honor**, no animal should be sacrificed to the gods.

Nor should we do so to return **thanks** for benefits received. For anyone who wishes to offer worthy thanks for kindness and favor ought not to return them through the injury of another. Indeed, such a person would appear no more grateful than someone who steals from one person in order to honor another with the stolen goods.

Likewise, it is not fitting to slaughter animals for the sake of obtaining some **protection** <note>original: "praesidii" — can also mean help, defense, or patronage</note> from the gods. For he who strives to gain a favor from another by acting unjustly makes himself suspect; it seems he will feel no true gratitude for any benefits he receives. Thus, even for those desiring a blessing, animal sacrifice is inappropriate for all. For although we might perhaps be able to act so secretly that our deeds are hidden from any human, we can in no way hide from God. 

If, therefore, sacrifice must be offered for one of these reasons—and yet for none of these reasons are animals to be slaughtered—it is clear that animals must not be sacrificed. But we, while we try to suppress the truth of these matters for the sake of the pleasures we enjoy from sacrifices <note>A reference to the fact that the meat of sacrificed animals was usually eaten by the participants.</note>, deceive only ourselves, not God. For from those animal...

<summary>Porphyry argues that animal sacrifice fails all three religious criteria—honor, gratitude, and petition—because an offering based on the "injury" of a harmless creature cannot please a just deity.</summary>

### BOOK II.

...animals, which provide no use for human life and offer no pleasure in their consumption, we never employ in sacrifices to the gods. For who has ever sacrificed snakes, or scorpions, or monkeys, or anything of that sort to the gods? But from those things which are of some use to our lives, and which possess some quality for our enjoyment, we do not abstain; rather, under the pretext of divinity, we actually slit their throats and skin them. 

For we slaughter oxen and sheep, as well as deer and birds; we even sacrifice pigs to the gods, despite the fact that they have no share in cleanliness, simply because they offer us delight in the eating. Indeed, some of these animals are a support to human life through their labor; others supply us with food and other comforts; others provide none of these benefits, but due to a sole desire for enjoyment <note>original: "fruendi cupiditaté" — Porphyry emphasizes that the "pleasure" of sacrifice is actually the pleasure of the feast that follows</note>, they are accustomed to be killed by men in sacrifices just as if they were useful. 

However, we do not sacrifice donkeys or elephants, nor anything else which, though it may be a helpful aid through its labor, is nonetheless unsuitable for eating. Add to this that even when sacrifices are not being performed, we do not abstain from these kinds of useful animals, but kill them solely for the sake of feeding ourselves. 

Furthermore, even among those animals which are considered suitable for sacrifice, we are accustomed to slaughter not the ones that might be more pleasing to the gods, but those that are more delightful for our own desires to enjoy. And thus we testify against ourselves: that it is for the sake of our own pleasure in eating, and not...

...persist in the observation of sacrifices for the sake of the gods. But among the Syrians, the Jews—who, according to Theophrastus <note>original: "Theophrastus" — a student of Aristotle whose lost work "On Piety" is being quoted here</note>, still continue that ancient first style of sacrifice by offering living animals—if they wanted us to sacrifice in the same manner they do, we would undoubtedly abandon the practice. For they do not feast upon the flesh of the sacrificed animals, but instead, having cast the entire victim into the fire, they pour a great deal of honey and wine over it and consume it quickly in the flames at night. They do this so that the sun, which sees all things, might not be made a spectator of the grim deed <note>original: "facinoris" — Porphyry uses a word that implies a crime or a serious transgression, highlighting his view that killing animals is inherently wrong</note>. They perform this ritual while spending the intervening days in fasting. During this entire time, being a **race of philosophers** <note>In the Hellenistic world, Jews were often described by Greek writers as "philosophers" because of their monotheism and devotion to their Law</note>, they converse with one another about the divine. At night, they contemplate the stars, gazing up at them and imploring God with certain prayers and vows. For these people offered sacrifices of other animals, and even of themselves <note>This may refer to the concept of circumcision or a misunderstanding of Jewish martyrdom/self-consecration in Porphyry's source</note>, led by necessity rather than by any desire for food.

One might also recognize this principle by looking at the **Egyptians**, the most learned of all peoples. They were so far removed from killing other animals that they established the images of animals as representations of the gods; so much did they believe that animals were related and akin to both gods and men. 

In the beginning, sacrifices of <term>fruits</term> <note>These "unbloody" offerings of grains and fruits are central to Porphyry's argument for a return to vegetarianism</note> were offered to the gods. ## BOOK II.

...with many prayers and supplications poured out to God, they even performed sacrifices from among themselves. Not only did they consecrate whatever was most beautiful among them to the gods, but this slaughter <term>mactatio</term> of their own kind even went beyond those things that were most beautiful.

From this custom, even to this day, not only in **Arcadia** <note>A mountainous region in the Greek Peloponnese</note> at the festivals of **Lycean Zeus** <note>original: "Lycaeis" — referring to Mount Lycaeus, where legends claimed human sacrifice occurred in honor of Zeus</note>, nor only in **Carthage** to **Saturn** <note>The Roman name for the Phoenician god Baal Hammon, to whom the Carthaginians were famously accused of sacrificing their children</note> do people publicly sacrifice human victims; but even in the annual cycle, in memory of the original institution, they sprinkle the altars with the blood of their own kin. This is true even though their own ritual law excludes from the sacred rites—by means of ceremonial boundaries and edicts—anyone who has been an author of human blood or murder.

From that point, however, they progressed further and substituted the bodies of other animals for their own in sacrifices. Later, when they had become satiated with lawful food and fallen into a forgetfulness of piety, they were led by an insatiable desire to taste and devour all things. This is exactly what usually happens now with food derived from all kinds of fruits. For when they have relieved their natural hunger by consuming these, they seek an unnecessary fullness and season many things for food which exceed the boundaries of **temperance** <term>temperantiae — the virtue of self-restraint</term>.

Consequently, as if they were offering noble sacrifices to the gods, they proceeded to eat those very victims. And from the beginning of this practice, the eating of animals was joined by men to their diet of fruits. For just as in ancient times they offered fruits to the gods, and from...

# ON ABSTINENCE
<meta>catchword: while</meta>

...they would eat those offerings with pleasure after the ritual sanctification: thus, when they had sacrificed animals, they thought they should do the same. However, in ancient times, holiness was not established in this way; instead, they worshipped the gods with the fruits of the earth, which delighted both nature and every sense of the human soul.

> But the altar did not bristle with the slaughter of bulls:
> Instead, mortals then considered it a great crime
> To devour the very limbs of cattle once their life was snatched away.
<note>This verse is attributed to the philosopher Empedocles, describing a "Golden Age" where blood sacrifice was unknown.</note>

This can be seen from the altar which is still preserved at **Delos** <note>The sacred island in the Aegean Sea, legendary birthplace of Apollo and Artemis</note>: which, since no animal was ever offered or slaughtered upon it, was called the **Altar of the Pious**. Thus, they not only abstained from slaughtering animals, but they also considered those who had dedicated the altar and those who used it to be endowed with <term>piety</term> <note>original: "pietate" — a sense of duty and religious devotion</note>. For this reason, the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who taught that animals have souls and that humans should not eat them</note>, having received this kind of instruction, abstained from eating animals throughout the entire course of their lives. When, however, they offered some animal to the gods on their own behalf as a sacrifice, they were accustomed to eat only from that specific offering; otherwise, they lived completely apart from the tasting of other meats.

But we do not act in this way; instead, filled beyond measure, we have advanced into an extremely unlawful custom. For neither should the altars of the gods be stained with slaughter, nor should humans feed on this kind of food—just as one must not eat the flesh of our own bodies—but that ancient way of life should be made an example...

<summary>Porphyry argues that ancient worship consisted of bloodless offerings (fruits) and highlights the "Altar of the Pious" at Delos as proof. He contrasts the disciplined vegetarianism of the Pythagoreans with the excessive meat-eating of his contemporaries.</summary>
<keywords>Delos, Altar of the Pious, Pythagoreans, blood sacrifice, vegetarianism, Empedocles, piety, ancient customs</keywords>

## BOOK II.

...while, which is still also accustomed to be observed at **Athens** <note>The cultural and religious capital of ancient Greece</note>. For in ancient times, as we have also said above, when men offered the fruits of the earth to the gods, but neither sacrificed animals nor took them for food, a certain public ritual was being celebrated at Athens. They say that a certain **Diomus** or **Sopatrus**—who was not a native citizen, but farmed fields in **Attica** <note>The region surrounding Athens</note>—witnessed an ox, returning from its labor, approach the table where the sacred <term>porridge</term> <note>original: "puls" — a thick meal made from grains, a staple of early Roman and Greek diets</note> and other <term>libations</term> <note>original: "libamina" — liquid offerings or small portions of food poured or set out for the gods</note> had been placed to be sacrificed. The ox ate some of these offerings and trampled others. 

Indignant at this, Sopatrus ran up and, seizing an axe from a neighbor that happened to be being sharpened at that moment, struck the beast. When the ox died from the wound, and his anger had cooled, Sopatrus realized what a great crime he had committed. After the ox was buried, they say that he went into voluntary exile because of the charge of <term>impiety</term> <note>original: "impietatis crimen" — a serious offense involving the violation of sacred laws or disrespect toward the gods</note> and fled to **Crete**. 

However, when a drought and a resulting great sterility of crops invaded Attica, the people decided the **Oracle** <note>The divine guidance sought at holy sites like Delphi</note> should be consulted publicly. The **Pythia** <note>The High Priestess of the Temple of Apollo at Delphi, who delivered oracles</note> responded that the exile in Crete would provide expiation for these troubles: if they both punished the killer and "raised the dead" in the same place where it had died. This was to be achieved if they both tasted of the dead animal and did not refrain from it. Therefore, an inquiry was held regarding this matter and, Sopatrus having been found, he—thinking he would be freed from that crime if everyone performed the act together publicly—said to those who were sent to bring him back...

<meta>This page continues the legend of Sopatrus and the origin of the "Bouphonia" (Ox-slaying) ritual. Following his exile to Crete, Sopatrus is consulted by the Athenians, who are suffering from a famine, on how to appease the gods for the death of the first agricultural ox.</meta>

# ON ABSTINENCE

...when they had come, he said that an ox must be killed by the city. When they hesitated as to who would be the one to wound the beast, he promised he would do it, provided he was granted citizenship and that they themselves were also made partners in the slaughter. Once this was granted, they returned to the city and established the matter just as it is still observed among them today.

They chose **Water-carrying** <term>maidens</term> <note>original: "aquatrices uirgines" — young women tasked with bringing water for the ritual cleansing and sharpening of tools</note> to bring water for those who would sharpen the axe and the knife. Once the axe and knife were sharpened, one man handed over the axe; another, having received it, struck the ox; and yet another cut its throat. When they had skinned it, everyone feasted upon its flesh.

Afterward, however, having sewn the hide back together and stuffed it with hay and straw, they "raised" the ox in the same form it had when it was alive, and they attached a plow to it as if it were working. When this was done, they summoned all those who had been partners in the action to a trial for the murder. When the water-carriers cast the blame upon those who had sharpened the tools, and those who had sharpened them upon the one who had handed over the axe, and he upon the one who had cut the throat, the throat-cutter threw the crime upon the knife. Since the knife lacked a voice, they condemned it for the murder.

And from that time until now, in the festival of the **Diipolia** <note>A festival held in honor of Zeus Polieus ("Zeus of the City") on the Athenian Acropolis</note> at **Athens**, they celebrate the sacrifice of the ox in this manner. For, having placed <term>porridge</term> <note>original: "pulte" — a thick grain-based meal</note> and sacrificial cakes upon a bronze table, they lead oxen around; whichever of them tastes the offerings from the table, they slay. And the families of those who perform these rites still exist today, descended from **Sopatrus** who first wounded the beast, who are called the **Ox-slayers** <note>original: "bouicidae" — literally "ox-killers," the hereditary title of the family participating in the ritual</note>...

<meta>catchword: dæ appel=</meta>

<summary>This page describes the ritualized "Trial of the Knife" at the Athenian festival of Diipolia, where the guilt for killing a sacrificial ox is passed from person to person until it is legally placed upon the inanimate weapon. This ceremony serves as a foundational myth explaining how animal sacrifice became permissible despite the initial taboo against killing laboring animals.</summary>

# BOOK II. 41

...they are called **[Keryki]dae** <note>original: "dae appellantur" — likely referring to the Kerykes, a priestly family in Athens who held hereditary roles in various city rituals</note>: from the one who drove the ox in circles, they were named **Goaders** <term>stimulatores</term>; from the throat-cutter come those whom they call **Dividers** <term>diuisores</term>, named for the feast that was established by the division of the meat. When the hide had been stuffed and they came to the trial, they sank the knife by throwing it into the sea. 

Thus, in ancient times it was not considered holy to kill animals that were helpers to human life; and today we must be careful to do the same. And just as it was not formerly pious to slaughter these for sacrifices, so now we must not believe it holy to kill animals of this kind for the sake of eating. 

If indeed this must be done for the sake of atonement toward the gods, every **defilement** <note>original: "contagio" — ritual pollution or spiritual stain</note> from these bodies must nevertheless be rejected. We must ensure that food obtained from an improper source does not become a kind of incitement for us to inflict violence upon one another. For even if the matter had no other evil in itself, it would certainly cause us to be more ready to attack each other. It is clear that those whose feelings shrink from harming **other species** <note>original: "alienigenis" — literally "those born elsewhere" or foreign-born; here referring to non-human animals as a distinct category from humans</note> will keep their minds from doing injury to their own kin. 

Perhaps, then, it would be best of all if we were to abstain immediately for the future. But since there is no one who is free from **sins** <note>original: "peccatis" — moral errors or failings</note>, what remains is that the sins of our ancestors in the taking of food be at least left to posterity through rituals of **expiation** <term>expiationes</term> <note>Acts of religious atonement meant to cleanse a person or community of guilt</note>. This would be achieved if, having placed the crime before our eyes, we were to lament our misery together with **Empedocles** <note>Empedocles (c. 490–430 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who taught that all living things possessed souls and that killing animals was a grave crime</note>.

# ON ABSTINENCE

> Woe is me, that the lot of fate did not destroy me before
> the fathers had defiled their mouths with wicked feasts.
<note>original: "Hei mihi q̃ fati ſors me non ante peremit, / Quàm dapibus patres temeraſſent ora ſceleſtis." — This is a quote from the Greek philosopher Empedocles, lamenting the historical shift toward eating meat.</note>

For to be so affected by one's own sense of sin is the mark of men seeking some remedy for present evils: it ensures that everyone, in offering pious and pure sacrifices to the divinity, does so with holiness and obtains benefit from the gods. The first and foremost benefit comes from the **fruits of the earth** <term>frugum</term>, from which alone **first-fruits** <term>primitiæ</term> should be offered to the gods, and even to the earth itself which produced them. For she is the common **hearth and home** <note>original: "ueſta ac focus" — literally "Vesta and hearth," referring to the sacred center of both the family and the state</note> of both gods and men. We all, reclining upon her as our nurse and mother, ought to celebrate her with praises, and love and honor her just like a parent. 

For by doing so, having achieved a transformation of our lives, we might be made worthy so that the way back to heaven and the whole company of the celestial gods might lie open to us once more. We, looking up to them now, must venerate them with these fruits that they supply to us, honoring them all with their first-fruits; yet we must not think that all of us are fit to offer sacrifices to the gods. For just as not all things are to be sacrificed to the gods, so also it must be thought that sacrifices made by everyone are perhaps not pleasing to the gods. 

The summary of the arguments, therefore, as to why animals should not be sacrificed—excluding the myths that have been interspersed and the few points briefly added by us—is this, according to **Theophrastus** <note>A student of Aristotle who wrote extensively on piety and botanical subjects; Porphyry uses him here to support the idea that bloodless offerings are more ancient and more divine</note>. However, I would not strive to dissolve the customs that have become established among various peoples.

<vocab>abstinence, sacrifices, of fruits, hearth/Vesta, Theophrastus, animals, first-fruits</vocab>

->### BOOK II.<-

...[I might] seem <note>The text begins with the fragment "derim," concluding the word "uiderim" from the previous page.</note>; for it is not my purpose now to discourse upon the republic. But since the laws under which we live allow us to worship the divinity even with the simplest and **inanimate** <term>inanimatis</term> things, choosing that which is most frugal, we shall both sacrifice according to the law of the city, and we ourselves shall take care that, approaching the gods pure in every respect, we offer a suitable sacrifice. 

For altogether, if sacrifice holds the nature of **first-fruits** <term>primitiæ</term> and an act of thanksgiving for those things which we have received from the gods for the use of life, it would seem most absurd if we, who abstain from living beings, were to make offerings to the gods from those very animals—especially since the gods are by no means inferior to us. It is hardly likely that they should require what we ourselves do not need. 

It is added that it is not holy <note>original: "sanctum"</note> that we should offer first-fruits from that food from which we abstain. For we find that such was the custom of men: that when they did not eat of living things, they did not perform sacrifices from them either; rather, they offered first-fruits from that which they actually ate. Therefore, it is also fitting now that he who abstains from animals should make offerings from those things which he touches <note>meaning: the plant-based foods he consumes</note>. 

We shall therefore also sacrifice, but we shall sacrifice, as is becoming, different sacrifices, since we are offering to different powers. To that God indeed who is above all, as a certain **wise man** <note>This "wise man" is likely Apollonius of Tyana, a Neo-Pythagorean philosopher known for advocating silent, mental worship of the supreme deity.</note> says, we offer nothing that is **perceptible to the senses** <term>sensibile</term>, neither burning incense nor even naming Him. For nothing is material which [is offered] to the **immaterial**...

...material <note>The text begins with the fragment "riali," completing the word "materiali" (material) from the previous page.</note> is not continually impure. Therefore, neither the spoken word is suitable for Him, nor even the internal word, if it has been defiled by the passions of the soul. Instead, we worship Him with a pure soul and with pure conceptions of Him. It is necessary, then, that having been joined with Him and made like Him, we offer the lifting up of our own mind as a sacred **sacrifice** <term>hostiam</term> to God—this being both our hymn and our salvation. Thus, the sacrifice to this God is perfected through the contemplation of a soul free from passions.

However, for the gods who are born from Him and for the **intelligible** <term>intelligibilibus</term> <note>In Neoplatonic philosophy, "intelligible" refers to realities that can only be understood by the intellect, rather than perceived by the senses.</note> gods, a hymn expressed in words is also appropriate. To each of these, a sacrifice is offered from the things they have given us, and by which they nourish and preserve us. Just as a farmer offers the **first-fruits** <term>primitias</term> from handfuls of crops and berries, so we shall offer our thoughts and words about them, giving thanks because they have granted us the power of contemplation, and because they truly nourish us by allowing us to behold them, by dwelling with us, appearing to us, and illuminating our salvation.

But now, even many of those who have studied **philosophy** <term>philosophiæ</term> refuse to do this; instead, chasing glory rather than worshiping the divine, they cast themselves down before statues. They give no thought to where they ought or ought not to go, nor do they make any effort to learn from those gifted with wisdom in divine matters how far or to what extent they should proceed in this regard. But we shall not contend with them—

[neglect]; <note>The text begins with "demus," completing the word "negli-gemus" (we shall neglect) from the previous page.</note> nor shall we strive to discern anything of this kind. Instead, by imitating the holy and ancient men, we shall offer to the gods the fruits of contemplation, which they have handed down to us, and which we most require for attaining true salvation. Indeed, the **Pythagoreans** <term>Pythagorei</term> <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed that numbers and geometry were the fundamental building blocks of reality and the divine.</note>, being devoted to the study of numbers and lines, dedicated many things to the gods through these studies. They called a certain number **Minerva** <term>Mineruam</term>, another **Diana** <term>Dianam</term>, and another **Apollo** <term>Apollinem</term>. They likewise named one number after **justice** <term>iustitiæ</term> and another after **temperance** <term>temperantiæ</term>, and they used similar methods with geometric figures and diagrams. They were so well-received by the gods with "first-fruits" of this kind that, by invoking them through these dedications, they enjoyed their presence and often used their help for prophecy or any other matter requiring inquiry.

Furthermore, to the gods who are within heaven itself—both the **wandering** <note>The planets.</note> and the **non-wandering** <note>The fixed stars.</note> stars, among whom the **sun** <term>solem</term> must be thought to hold the first rank, and then the **moon** <term>lunam</term>—it is permitted to kindle a fire akin to them, provided we also do those things which the **Theologian** <term>Theologus</term> <note>Likely referring to Orpheus, the legendary poet to whom many "theological" and ritual texts were attributed in antiquity.</note> says. For he says that no living creature should be sacrificed, but one should proceed with offerings of meal, honey, and the fruits and flowers that rise from the earth. He says that fire should not arise from a "blood-stained hearth," and adds other similar instructions. For why is there any need for us to transcribe all those things here? However, anyone who has any concern for **piety** <term>pietatis</term> knows that for the gods—

<header>*ON ABSTINENCE*</header>

[In]deed <note>original: "dem," the conclusion of "quidem" from the previous page</note> that no living being <term>animatum</term> <note>Referring to any creature possessing a "breath of life" or soul, specifically animals.</note> ought to be sacrificed, but rather that such things are offered to spirits <term>dæmonibus</term> <note>In the Neoplatonic sense, "daemons" are intermediary spirits between humans and gods, and can be either helpful or harmful.</note> and others, whether good or evil; and to what extent it pertains to those who sacrifice to them, and how much they themselves stand in need of such things. But as for me, let these other matters be wrapped in silence. However, those things which certain **Platonists** <term>Platonici</term> have made public, I believe I must bring forward for those who are wise, in order to explain the matter at hand. For they speak in this manner:

The first God <term>Primus Deus</term> <note>In Neoplatonism, the "One" or the supreme source of all existence.</note>, since He is without a body, and is immovable and indivisible—being neither within any other thing nor bound within Himself—requires nothing from the outside, just as has been said. Furthermore, not even the **Soul of the World** <term>múdi anima</term> <note>The "Anima Mundi," a force that connects all living things and the physical universe.</note> requires anything external; for it possesses within itself the capacity to be complete in three dimensions and to move naturally by its own power. It proposes and chooses by its nature to be moved in a good and orderly fashion, and to move the body of the world according to the highest reason. It has admitted the body of the world into itself and embraced it, even though the Soul itself is held to be incorporeal and free from all worldly passion. 

As for the remaining gods—the world itself, and the **wandering** <note>The planets.</note> and **non-wandering** <note>The fixed stars.</note> stars, which consist of both soul and body and are "visible gods"—thanks ought to be given to them through inanimate <note>Bloodless offerings, such as grains, honey, or incense.</note> sacrifices in the manner that has been described. There remains a multitude of invisible beings, whom **Plato** called "undifferentiated spirits" <term>dæmonas</term>. Among these, those who have been named by men receive honors equal to the gods and other forms of worship from various individuals. However, there are others who, for the most part, have not been given names,

<meta>catchword: to not-</meta>

...by some in villages, or in certain cities, they secretly obtain both a name and veneration. The rest of the multitude is called by the common name of **daemons** <term>dæmonum</term> <note>In this context, these are not "demons" in the modern sense, but any spirit existing between the divine and the human realms.</note>. Regarding all of them, however, men are persuaded of this: that if they become angry because they are neglected or defrauded of their established worship, they will inflict harm; on the other hand, they will do good to those who make them favorable through vows, supplications, sacrifices, and other things of this kind. But since there is a confused knowledge regarding them, and it leads to much false accusation <note>original: "calumniam," implying a misunderstanding or misrepresentation of their true nature</note>, it is necessary that we distinguish their nature in our discourse. For perhaps, they say, it is necessary to reveal from where the error concerning these beings arose among men. Therefore, a division <note>a classification or categorization</note> must be used in this manner:

Whatever souls, having arisen from that universal [source] <note>Referring to the World Soul mentioned on the previous page.</note>, govern the great regions situated below the moon—clinging indeed to the spirit <term>spiritui</term> <note>The *pneuma*, or the "airy" vehicle that serves as a body for the soul.</note>, but themselves ruling according to reason <term>rationem</term>—these are to be considered **good daemons** <term>dæmones boni</term>. They provide all things for the benefit of those over whom they preside, whether they preside over certain animals, or certain fruits, or those things which exist for the sake of these: such as rains, moderate winds, clear skies, the proper balance of the seasons, and other things which are conducive to these. Likewise, they may preside over the arts and those things pertaining to music, all branches of education, medicine, athletics, and similar pursuits. For it is in no way possible that these same beings should both provide benefits and be the authors of harm...

### BOOK II.

...matters are carried out in them. Among these should also be numbered those who, as Plato says <note>A reference to Plato’s *Symposium*, where the character Diotima describes Love as a "great daemon" who conveys messages between gods and men.</note>, act as messengers, reporting human deeds to the gods and divine commands to humans. For they carry our vows to the gods as if to judges, and explain the gods’ counsels and warnings to us through prophecies <term>uaticinia</term>.

However, those souls that do not dominate the spirit <term>spiritum</term> <note>The "spirit" here refers to the *pneuma*, a subtle, airy vehicle or "body" that Neoplatonists believed the soul inhabited before and after being in a physical body.</note> joined to them, but are instead often overcome by it, and are violently driven and carried along by it whenever the angers and desires of that spirit are stirred up—these too are called daemons, but they are rightly termed **malevolent daemons**. Truly, both these and those endowed with the opposite power are altogether invisible and of such a nature that they cannot be perceived by human senses in any way. For they do not have a solid body surrounding them, nor do they all possess a single form, but rather are marked by many different shapes.

The forms that characterize their spirit sometimes appear, and sometimes they withdraw from sight, becoming obscure. Those among them who are of a worse nature even change their shapes at times. Because their spirit <term>spiritus</term> is corporeal, it is subject to passions and is liable to decay. However, because it is bound by the souls in this way, their form persists for a longer time, though it is not eternal; for it is likely that something is constantly flowing away from it and that it requires nourishment <note>original: "nutritionem admittere." Porphyry later argues that some spirits "feed" on the vapors and smoke of blood sacrifices, which is why they encourage human warfare and animal slaughter.</note>. The bodies of the good spirits are composed in balanced proportion, just like the bodies...

<summary>Porphyry describes daemons as messengers between gods and humans and distinguishes between good spirits and malevolent ones driven by passion. He also explains that their "spirit bodies" are subtle, changing, and require nourishment, making them long-lived but not eternal.</summary>

# BOOK II.

...[the bo]dies of those that appear; those of the **malevolent daemons** are unstable <note>original: "inæquabilia". In Neoplatonic thought, "unevenness" or instability is a sign of being further from the divine, which is perfectly stable and unified.</note>. For this reason, being more subject to passions, they occupy the region neighboring the earth. Therefore, there is no evil that they do not attempt to bring about. For they possess entirely violent and deceitful characters; and whenever they are free from the guardianship of a better daemon, they make forceful and sudden attacks as if from an ambush; and at one moment they strive to remain hidden, while at another they use open force. 

From this, the evils that come from them are swift; however, the remedies and corrections which are provided by the better daemons seem for this reason to be slower: because everything that is good, since it is easy and balanced when it is right, proceeds in an orderly fashion and does not miss the proper opportunity. If you think in this way, you will never fall into that most absurd opinion of believing that either evil arises from the good, or good from the evil. 

For the absurdity of the matter does not consist in this alone; it also happens that many people, having conceived the worst opinions about the gods, spread them to everyone. For this also must be established as one of the greatest harms brought by malevolent daemons: that while they themselves are the cause of the calamities that occur around the earth—such as plagues <term>pestilentiæ</term>, famines <term>sterilitates</term>, earthquakes <term>terræ motus</term>, droughts <term>siccitates</term>, and others of that kind—they also persuade us that the authors of these things are those who are actually the source of their opposites—that is, of fertility <term>fertilitatum</term> and prosperity <term>prosperitatum</term>. They [hide] themselves from the [ca]...

<summary>Porphyry describes malevolent daemons as unstable, earthly spirits who cause natural disasters and then deceive humanity by blaming the benevolent gods for these calamities.</summary>

-># BOOK II.<-

...harm <note>The Latin text begins with the suffix "-num," likely completing the word "damnum" (harm or loss) from the previous page.</note>, since they use an eloquence tailored to induce admiration and amazement, and of a kind that can both soothe minds and instill belief in things that are least possible of all. However, we ought to be firmly persuaded of this: that the good never harms, nor does the evil ever help. For it is not the nature of heat, as **Plato** says <note>Referring to *The Republic* 335d: "It is not the function of heat to cool, but of its opposite."</note>, to make things cold, but rather its opposite; likewise, it is not the nature of a just being to inflict any injury. 

The divine power <term>numen</term> <note>original: "numen"; refers to the divine will or presence of a deity.</note> is surely, by its very nature, the most just of all things; otherwise, nothing would exist. Therefore, this [harmful] force and chance must be cut away from the **beneficent daemons**. For a faculty that is accustomed by nature and will to cause harm is undoubtedly contrary to one that is beneficent; and contrary qualities can in no way exist in the same being. 

And so, while these [malevolent spirits] harm the mortal race of humans in many areas, and sometimes in the greatest matters, the **good daemons** never cease their individual duties. Instead, they show us the dangers hanging over us from the **malevolent ones** as much as they are able, making signs to us through **dreams**, through a soul divinely inspired, and through many other means. Thus, if anyone were able to distinguish the things being signified, they would recognize and guard against all frauds. For they signify to everyone, but not all perceive what is being signified; just as not everyone can read what is written, but only those who have learned their letters. 

Yet through these contrary daemons, all deceptions <term>præstigiæ</term> <note>original: "præstigiæ"; refers to illusions, conjuring tricks, or "sleight of hand" used to mislead the senses.</note> are also performed. For it is these especially, and their leader...

...a part <note>The Latin text begins with the suffix "-tem," likely completing the word "partem" (a part or share) from the previous page.</note> to those who worship them, who procure evils through sorceries <term>ueneficiis</term> <note>original: "ueneficiis"; refers to magic involving potions, poisons, or charms.</note> and incantations. For they are full of specters and phantoms, and are skilled in deceptive, marvelous fallacies. Through these [spirits], unfortunate men fashion enticements and love charms. For all intemperance, all hope for riches and for glory, exists through them—and especially deception, since lying is characteristic of them. For they desire to be considered gods; and the power that presides over them wishes to seem to be the greatest god. 

These are the ones who are delighted by libations and the smell of burnt offerings <note>original: "nidor"; the heavy, fatty smoke from animal sacrifices that was thought to nourish lower spirits.</note>, by which their spiritual body grows fat. For that body lives on vapors and fumigations in various ways, and it is strengthened by the smells of blood and flesh. Therefore, a wise and temperate man will be careful not to use sacrifices of this kind, by which he might attract such daemons to himself. Instead, he will strive to purify his mind in every way; because these spirits are not accustomed to invade a pure mind, on account of the lack of similarity they have with it. 

But if it is necessary for cities to appease even these [daemons], it has nothing to do with us. For in cities, riches, external status, and things pertaining to the body are considered "goods," and their opposites are considered "evils," while there is very little concern for the care of the soul. We, however, as far as it is possible, will not need what these spirits offer; instead, we will devote all our effort—both through the soul and through our external life—to become like God and those who surround Him. That which...

### BOOK II.

[It] arises from a state of being unaffected <note>original: "impatibilitate"; referring to the philosophical concept of *apatheia*, where the soul is no longer disturbed by worldly passions.</note>, and from a clear perception of things that truly exist, and from a life oriented toward these very things. This is so that we might turn out unlike evil men and daemons, and indeed unlike any mortal thing that delights in material reality. Therefore, we too shall sacrifice according to the words spoken by **Theophrastus** <note>A student of Aristotle; Porphyry frequently cites his lost work *On Piety* to argue against animal sacrifice.</note>. 

The theologians <note>In this context, "theologi" refers to ancient authors of sacred rites or the "divine men" of the mystery traditions, rather than modern religious scholars.</note> have also agreed with these things, since they knew that to the same degree we neglect the purification of the soul's passions, we are joined to a wicked power—to the point that we even find it necessary to appease <note>original: "demulcere"; literally to stroke or soothe, suggesting a ritual attempt to calm a hostile force.</note> that power. For as the theologians say, for those who are bound to external things and do not yet rule over their passions, it is necessary to turn away the harmful influence of this power; otherwise, they will never find rest from their labors. Let what has been explained thus far suffice regarding the matter of sacrifices.

Now we shall demonstrate that which we stated at the beginning: that it is not necessary to believe that if animals must be sacrificed, they must then also be eaten. We will show that one must not eat of animals even if it is sometimes necessary to sacrifice them. For all the theologians agree in asserting that one must not taste anything from sacrifices performed for the purpose of averting evils <term>apotropaic</term> <note>original: "auertendorum malorum"; rituals intended to turn away or ward off malevolent spirits or bad fortune.</note>, but rather they must be treated as expiations <term>purifications</term>. For they say that no one should return to a city or a private home unless they have first purified their clothes and body in rivers or springs. For this reason, even those to whom they permitted sacrifice were forbidden from the sacrifi—
