### BOOK II.

->LIBER II.<-

N

<warning>Significant ink bleed-through from the reverse side of the page.</warning>

[He] observes [human] actions and counsels. Therefore, the purity of a divine man is both internal and external. Just as he strives to abstain from the passions of the soul <note>original: "animae affectibus"; the emotional disturbances that cloud reason.</note> and from the foods that incite those passions, so too does he enjoy the food of divine wisdom. He wins for himself a likeness to God through right thoughts about Him. He is one who is consecrated by an intellectual sacrifice <note>original: "intellectuali sacrificio"; a "mental" or "spiritual" offering rather than a physical, bloody one.</note>, approaching God clothed in a white garment—the truly bright state of the soul’s impassivity <term>impatibilitas: a state where the soul is no longer agitated by worldly desires or suffering</term> and the lightness of the body. He comes to God unburdened by the foreign and external juices <note>The "juices" of animal flesh which were thought to thicken the physical body and weigh down the spirit.</note> within the body or disturbances within the soul.

For surely we should not be required to wear pure shoes, free from every stain, in those temples dedicated to the gods by men, while in the temple of the Father—which is this world—we take no care to keep our outermost skin-tunic <note>original: "cutanea tunica"; a Neoplatonic metaphor for the physical body, which "clothes" the immortal soul.</note> pure, so that we might enter it in a clean and bright state. If the danger lay only in the defilement of the body itself, perhaps it would be permissible to neglect it. But since every physical body perceptible to the senses carries with it certain outpourings of material demons <note>original: "dæmonum"; here referring to lower spirits or energetic forces associated with base matter.</note>, it happens that alongside the impurity arising from the eating of flesh and blood, a power that is friendly and familiar to that impurity is also present, drawn by its likeness and kinship to it.

For this reason, the theologians <term>theologi: ancient sages or authors of sacred mysteries, not modern academic theologians</term> rightly applied the greatest care to abstinence. The Egyptian <note>Likely a reference to Hermes Trismegistus or an Egyptian priest like Chaeremon, whom Porphyry frequently cites as an authority on ritual purity.</note> revealed these things to us, bringing forward a most natural cause which experience had confirmed. For since a wicked and irrational soul, which has been violently torn away and has left the body,

He warns that eating flesh attracts lower, material spirits that share a kinship with the impurity of blood.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument in Book II of *On Abstinence from Animal Food*. The previous page discussed the physical and spiritual "clothing" of the philosopher and began to describe the dangers of ritual impurity. # ON ABSTINENCE

[the soul] serves and assists it. For it is common that the souls of those who perish by a violent death are accustomed to linger near the body itself; this is why [divine law] forbids anyone to violently cast themselves out of their own body. Since, therefore, the violent slaughter of living beings forces their souls to take pleasure in the very things they have abandoned—for nothing prevents a soul from being present where a kindred object draws it—many such souls have been seen to lament near their remains. The souls of the unburied also sit near their bodies; sorcerers <term>uenefici: practitioners of "low" magic or "poisoners" who used animal parts and spirits for spells</term> abuse this fact, compelling these souls to their service by detaining either the whole body or some specific part of it. 

Because they <note>Porphyry is referring to the ancient priests and "divine men" mentioned previously.</note> had observed the nature of a base soul, its kinship [with the physical], and the pleasure it is accustomed to feel toward the body from which it was torn away, they—not without reason—avoided the eating of meat. They did this so that they might not be disturbed by foreign souls violently snatched away, nor hindered from approaching God alone by the presence of demons <note>original: "dæmonum"; in this context, intermediary spirits that are attracted to the "heavy" energy of blood and death.</note> who are attracted to such impure things. 

For experience taught them in many instances that the nature of a kindred body acts as a lure for attracting a soul. Indeed, those who desire to receive into themselves the souls of prophetic animals <note>original: "uaticinatoriorum animalium"; animals believed to have natural or divine foresight.</note> devour their principal members—namely, the hearts of crows, or moles, or hawks—inasmuch as these parts possess a lingering soul that prophesies like a god, which insinuates itself into the people at the same time the body is ingested.

<summary>Porphyry argues that violent death traps souls near their remains, leading sorcerers to exploit them and philosophers to avoid meat to escape spiritual contamination. He notes that some even eat animal hearts to ingest the animal's prophetic soul.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, On Abstinence, soul, violent death, demons, sorcery, divination, ritual purity, animal hearts</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument in Book II of *On Abstinence from Animal Food*. Following the discussion on how souls linger near bodies after violent death, Porphyry now contrasts the purity required of a philosopher-priest with the ritual requirements of ordinary priests.</meta>

# BOOK II.

Therefore, with good reason, the philosopher—who is the priest of the God who is above all—abstains from the eating of all living creatures. This he does while he strives to approach the one God alone by himself, without any disturbance from a retinue <note>original: "comitatuum"; Porphyry refers to the swarm of lower spirits or passions that follow a person who is not purified.</note> of distractions. He is cautious, perceives the signs of things to come, and understands the natures of things; he is prudent, temperate, modest, and in every way providing for his own spiritual well-being.

And just as a man who is a priest of some particular deity is skilled in the dedication of their statues, and in their sacrifices, ceremonies, purifications, and similar rites; so the priest of the God who presides over all things is skilled through experience in His images <term>simulacra: in Neoplatonic thought, "images" can refer to physical statues or the intellectual forms within the soul that reflect the divine</term>, in purifications, and in all other matters through which he is joined to Him.

Now, if the priests and sacrificers of the gods of this world <note>Literally "the gods who are here," referring to the lower, terrestrial, or planetary deities as opposed to the Supreme God.</note> command both themselves and others to abstain from tombs, from profane men, from menstruating women, from sexual intercourse, and even from shameful or mournful sights—not to mention hearing things that stir the passions—it is because often, due to the presence of impure things, something appears that disturbs the sacrifice and the diviner <term>aruspex: a priest in antiquity who interpreted the will of the gods, traditionally by inspecting the entrails of sacrificed animals</term>. This is why they say that sacrificing at an inopportune time brings more harm than gain.

Should not he, then, who is the priest of the Supreme Father, refuse to become a living tomb for dead bodies? Should he, full of filth himself, endure such a thing when he desires to converse with a more excellent Being? Is it not enough that we must take into this life of ours the elements of death found in the fruits of the earth?

But this is not yet the place to discuss these things. One must return...

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument in Book II of *On Abstinence from Animal Food*. ->### ON ABSTINENCE<-

...is indeed for the purpose of more accurately distinguishing those things which pertain to sacrifices. For someone might say that we would be doing away with a great part of divination—that which is customarily performed through entrails <term>exta: the internal organs of a sacrificial animal, such as the liver, heart, and lungs, used for prophecy</term>—if we were to abstain from the slaughter of animals. But by that logic, such a person might as well kill human beings, since it is said that the future is seen even more clearly in human entrails; indeed, many among the barbarians <note>original: "barbaris"; in this context, referring to non-Greek cultures such as the Carthaginians or certain nomadic tribes who were rumored to practice human sacrifice</note> practice divination through the entrails of men. 

But just as it would be an act of injustice and monstrous cruelty to kill one's own kind for the sake of divination, so it is unjust to slaughter an irrational animal for the sake of prophecy. Whether it is the gods or spirits <term>daemon: in Neoplatonic thought, these are intermediary beings between the divine and the human, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense</term> who show these signs, or whether the soul departing from the animal responds to the inquiry through the signs in the entrails, is not a matter for the present discussion. 

Nevertheless, for those whose lives are devoted to external things, we permit them to be carried where they are usually carried, once they have taken this impiety upon themselves. But as for the philosopher we are describing—the one who abstains from external concerns—we rightly assert that he will neither bring business to these spirits, nor will he have any need for prophets or the entrails of animals. This is because he strives to abstain from those very things for which divinations are usually performed. For it is not regarding a marriage or a commercial venture that he will go to rouse a prophet; he has no need to consult divination about a slave, or his fortune, or an increase in his honors, or any other such human ambitions. But what he seeks...

### BOOK II.

...[has lived], are of such a kind that neither any prophet nor any animal entrails <term>exta: the internal organs of a sacrificial animal used for prophecy</term> are able to indicate their certainty. Instead, he himself, as we have said, approaching God by his own self—who is located within his own true inner parts <note>Porphyry suggests that the true "viscera" are not physical organs, but the internal spiritual essence where the divine resides</note>—receives warnings concerning eternal life, being entirely drawn inward there, and desiring to become, in the place of a prophet, a speaker with the great Jupiter <note>original: "Iouis"; in this Neoplatonic context, Jupiter represents the supreme governing Mind or Demiurge of the cosmos</note>. For even if some necessity should press him, there are good spirits <term>dæmones: benevolent intermediary beings between gods and humans</term> who meet with one who lives in this way and is a friend of God, foretelling through dreams, signs, and visions what is to happen and what is to be avoided. 

One must only abstain from evil: and acknowledge that which is most precious and most honorable in all things, and that all the good which is in the universe is friendly and approachable. But it is a wonder how readily wickedness and ignorance of divine matters are accustomed to despise and condemn those things which they do not know. Yet to those who are fit to hear, nature cries out: since she is intellectual, she initiates into sacred rites through the intellect those who worship her.

Add to this that even if someone should allow the rite of sacrificing for the sake of foreknowing the future, it does not follow from this that the meat must also necessarily be eaten. Just as a sacrifice that is made otherwise, whether to gods or to spirits, does not immediately introduce the eating of flesh. For history records not only those things which Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BC), Aristotle's successor, who argued that animal sacrifice was a late and corrupt development in religion</note> mentioned, but also many other things concerning the slaughter <note>original: "mactatione"</note> of humans,

# ON ABSTINENCE

...[which the ancients] used, has been handed down to memory: yet it does not follow from this that humans ought to be eaten. Since these examples are indeed many and various, I believe these few which I will add below will be sufficient to prove this point. For in Rhodes, on the sixth of July <note>original: "pridie nonas Iulias"; the day before the Nones of July</note>, a human used to be sacrificed to Saturn <note>original: "Saturno"; the Roman equivalent of the Greek Titan Cronus</note>. And after that custom had lasted for quite a long time, it was later changed into a more humane fashion. They would hold someone already condemned to death by public judgment in prison until the <term>Saturnalia: a winter festival characterized by the overturning of social norms</term>; when that solemn day arrived, they led him outside the gates opposite the temple of Aristobule <note>original: "Aristobulæ"; an epithet meaning "The Best Counselor," usually referring to the goddess Diana</note>, gave him a drink of wine, and cut his throat. 

In the city that is now called Salamis, but was previously called Coronis, a human was slaughtered in sacrifice during the month of Venus <note>original: "mense... Venereo"</note> according to the Cyprian custom, to Agraulus, the daughter of Cecrops <note>the mythical founder of Athens</note> and the nymph Agraulis. This custom indeed persisted until the times of Diomedes; then it was so changed that the human was sacrificed to Diomedes. For within one and the same circuit of walls, the small shrines <term>sacellum: a small, often roofless, consecrated space</term> of Minerva, Agraulus, and Diomedes were enclosed. The one who was to be sacrificed, led by young men, first ran around the altar three times; then he was struck by the priest with a spear driven into his throat; and thus, cast upon a piled-up pyre, he was burned whole. King Diphilus of Cyprus, who lived in the times of Seleucus the theologian, abolished this institution, changing the sacrifice to the slaughter of an ox. But the spirit <term>dæmon: in this Neoplatonic context, a supernatural being that mediates between gods and humans</term>...

### BOOK II.

...[admitted] an ox in place of a human, so that from this he might undoubtedly signify that both acts possessed equal weight. Likewise in Heliopolis <note>The "City of the Sun" in Egypt</note>, Amosis <note>Amasis I or Ahmose I, an Egyptian pharaoh</note> abolished the law regarding the slaughter of humans, as Manetho <note>An Egyptian priest and historian</note> testifies in his book *On Antiquity and Piety*. They were slaughtered for Juno <note>The Greek Hera</note>, and were inspected just like pure calves, which it was customary to examine and mark with a seal. Indeed, three were sacrificed in a day, in whose place Amosis ordered an equal number of wax images to be substituted. 

On Chios as well, they used to offer a human in sacrifice to Omadius Bacchus <note>original: "Omadio Bacco"; Dionysus the "Raw-Eater"</note>, tearing them apart; likewise in Tenedos, as Euelpis of Carystus says. Furthermore, Apollodorus has recorded that the Spartans <note>original: "Lacedæmonios"</note> sacrificed a human to Mars <note>the Greek Ares</note>. The Phoenicians also, during great calamities of either war, drought, or pestilence, would slaughter someone from among their dearest, devoting them to Saturn <note>original: "Saturno"; identified here with the Phoenician god El or Baal</note>. History is filled with such sacrifices of the Phoenicians: which Philo of Byblos translated into Greek and arranged into eight books, having been written in the Phoenician language by Sanchuniathon. 

Ister, however, in his collection of Cretan sacrifices, says that the Curetes <note>mythical attendants of the infant Zeus</note> were anciently accustomed to slaughter boys for Saturn. Most of these human slaughters were abolished among almost all peoples under the Emperor Hadrian, as stated by the author who best collected the mysteries of Mithras <note>referring to the historian Pallas</note>. For in Laodicea in Syria as well, a virgin used to be killed every year for Minerva <note>the Greek Athena</note>: now

<meta>This page continues from page 51, extending Porphyry's catalog of historical human sacrifices. Having previously discussed Cyprus, Egypt, and Phoenicia, the text now moves to rituals in Carthage, Arabia, and Greece, eventually transitioning into an argument that ritual sacrifice—often born of dire necessity—does not justify the consumption of meat or cannibalism, citing the horrors of the Mercenary War.</meta>

->ON ABSTINENCE<-

...it was customary for a doe <note>original: "cerua"</note> to be sacrificed. Likewise in Africa, the Carthaginians performed a sacrifice <note>This likely refers to the ritual of the *Moloch* or sacrifice of infants, which Roman-era writers frequently cited</note>, which Iphicrates <note>An Athenian general of the 4th century BCE</note> abolished. The Dumatii, a people of Arabia, also used to sacrifice a young girl every year, whom they would bury beneath the altar, which they used as an idol. Indeed, Phylarchus <note>A Greek historian of the 3rd century BCE</note> relates that it was commonly the custom for all the Greeks to sacrifice a human before they went out to war. I omit the Thracians and Scythians. I pass over the fact that the Athenians slaughtered the daughter of Erechtheus and Praxithea in their sacred rites. Who even now is unaware that in Megalopolis <note>A city in Arcadia; the text here conflates a Greek location with the Roman festival of Jupiter Latiaris</note>, at the festival of Jupiter Latiaris, it is customary for a man to have his throat slit?

Yet it does not follow from this that human flesh must be eaten. For it was by some necessity that it came about that a human was taken for sacrifice. For some, suffering from hunger during sieges, have eaten one another; and in those cases, both the people themselves were stained with crime, and the deed was considered impious. Indeed, after the first war waged by the Romans against the Carthaginians over Sicily, when the Phoenician mercenary soldiers had revolted and simultaneously led other Carthaginians <note>original: "pœnos"</note> into defection, Hamilcar, surnamed Barca <note>original: "Balcas"; the father of Hannibal</note>, having led an expedition against them, reduced those he defeated to such a state of want that they first took those who fell in battle for food. Then, when those also failed, they killed the captives and fed on their flesh. Later, they laid hands on their slaves. Finally, when everything else had already been consumed...

### BOOK II.

...having turned against each other, they by chance ate their fellow soldiers who had been drawn out <note>This refers to the "Famine of the Saw," where besieged mercenaries drew lots to decide who would be killed and eaten by the rest</note>. When Hamilcar <note>Hamilcar Barca, the Carthaginian general and father of Hannibal</note> finally defeated these men and took them into his power, he saw to it that they were trampled by elephants; as if it were not right for them to mingle with other men any further. 

And yet, he himself never allowed the eating of humans <note>original: "hominum esum"</note> just because some had dared to perform it; nor did his son Hannibal, when a certain man advised him while leading his army into Italy to accustom his soldiers to eating humans so that they might never be pressed by a lack of food <note>This anecdote is also recorded by the historian Polybius; a general named Hannibal Monomachus suggested this extreme measure to the famous Hannibal</note>. Therefore, just because hunger and wars were the reason that we began to use other animals for food, it does not mean the same should be done for the sake of pleasure—just as we have never allowed the eating of human flesh. 

Nor, because they sacrificed animals to certain powers <note>Refers to spiritual or divine authorities</note>, should they for that reason also be eaten. Indeed, even those who used to sacrifice humans did not, for that reason, feed upon their flesh as well. Therefore, it has been sufficiently demonstrated by these points, I think, that it does not immediately follow from the sacrifice of animals that we must also eat their meat. 

Furthermore, this has also been confirmed by the theologians <note>original: "theologis"; Porphyry refers here to ancient authorities on ritual and the nature of the divine</note> themselves: that sacrifices of this kind, which consist of flesh and blood, were customarily offered not to the gods, but to demons <note>original: "daemonibus"; in Porphyry's Neoplatonic view, these are intermediary spirits, some of whom are "lower" and crave the energy of blood</note> by those who had explored the nature of the powers that exist in the universe. For some of these demons are malicious, while others are good and kind, who [avert] the disturbance...

<summary>Porphyry argues that neither the desperate cannibalism of soldiers nor the practice of ritual sacrifice justifies the consumption of meat for pleasure, concluding that blood sacrifices are intended for lower spirits (demons) rather than the true gods.</summary>

...yet they will bring us no justification <note>original: "rationem," completing the word from the previous page</note> if we only offer as first-fruits those things which we eat, and with which we nourish either the body or the soul; it is established that this was also asserted by those same ancients. Therefore, if we only add this much—to show that the opinions of any men that are least corrupted and perverse do not wander from the right opinion concerning the gods—we shall bring an end to this book. > Who is so foolish, who so credulous,
> And so ignorant, as to think the gods themselves

> Incense, and cakes, and sacrificial wafers are the only things I shall offer here.
> For I do not slaughter for friends, but for the heavenly ones. <note>The poet distinguishes between a feast for humans (friends) where meat is served, and a gift for the gods (heavenly ones) where bloodless offerings are appropriate</note>

For the ancient custom of sacrificing, as we have already shown, was that they used only cakes and the fruits of the earth. From which sacrifices—that is, *thysiai* <note>original: θυσίαι</note>, and *thuelai* <note>original: θυηλαὶ</note>, and *thymata* <note>original: θύματα</note>, which mean libations and incense-offerings—and the very verb *thyein* <note>original: θύειν</note>, which is "to sacrifice," was said to come from "perfuming" or "burning incense" <note>original: "a suffiendo," identifying the Greek "apo tou thymian" (ἀπὸ τοῦ θυμιᾶν)</note>. This is what we now call *epithyein* <note>original: ἐπιθύειν</note>, which means "to sacrifice upon." For what we now call *thyein* <note>original: θύειν</note>, that is, "to sacrifice,"

# BOOK II.

-># ON ABSTINENCE<-

...we sacrificed <note>original: "sacrificabamus," completing the word from the previous page</note> <gloss>they were making</gloss> <note>original: ἐποίουν</note> in ancient times; that is, they were accustomed to call the act [of sacrifice] "to make."

For indeed, as the poet says: "They performed <note>original: "faciebant," here meaning "to sacrifice"</note> magnificent sacred rites for great Phoebus <note>Apollo</note> with bulls and goats."

But those who introduced such expensive and magnificent sacrifices are ignorant of how great a quantity of evils they brought into human life along with them: superstition, luxury, and the belief about the gods that they can be corrupted by bribery—that men can redeem their own injustice through sacrifices. For what other reason did some offer <term>gold-horned triads<gloss>terniones auricornes; a sacrifice involving three victims</gloss></term>, while others performed <term>hecatombs<gloss>centibouia; literally "a hundred oxen"</gloss></term>? And Olympias, the mother of Alexander the Great, used to sacrifice thousands of whatever animals she chose. What was the reason for this, if not that a costly display, once begun, leads entirely to superstition? 

For when a young person learns that the gods are delighted by the magnificence of expenses and—as they say—by feasts of oxen and other animals, he will surely become very temperate indeed! <note>The author is being sarcastic; such a belief would actually encourage a youth to be reckless and immoral.</note> In what way, indeed, if he believes such sacrifices are pleasing to the gods, will he not think himself permitted to act unjustly in everything? He will think he can easily redeem all his sins with sacrifices. 

But if, on the contrary, he is persuaded that the gods have no need for these empty overflows of wealth, and that they instead look to the character of those who approach them—valuing most highly a right and true contemplation of the gods themselves and of the nature of things...

<summary>This page argues that the transition from simple offerings to extravagant animal sacrifices fostered superstition and the false belief that the gods can be bribed to overlook human injustice.</summary>

# BOOK II.

-># ON ABSTINENCE<-

...to accept injustice in place of the **highest** sacrifice, he will by no means be self-controlled <note>original: "temperans"</note>, and he will not turn out holy and just. But to the gods, a **pure mind** and a soul **free from passions** is the best offering. It is also appropriate to them that from other things we should also dedicate <term>first-fruits<gloss>primitias; the first part of a harvest offered to a deity</gloss></term> moderately—not negligently, but with all readiness. 

For honors ought to be like the precedence shown to good men, when we yield the way to them and stand up in their presence, rather than like the payment of debts. For a man will obviously not say: "If you are mindful of a benefit received and love me, I wish to love you in return, increasing the benefit, since I bestowed it on you for this very reason"; but for God, these same things will not be enough. <note>The author argues that while humans trade favors, the divine relationship is not a simple transaction of "debts" or "payments."</note>

Therefore **Plato** says that it is fitting for a good man to sacrifice, and to be always in the company of the gods through vows, dedications, sacrifices, and every form of worship; but for a bad man, it is a great labor undertaken toward the gods in vain. <note>referencing Plato's *Laws* Book IV, 716d</note> For the good man knows best both what should be sacrificed and from which things one must abstain, which things are to be pursued, and from which things first-fruits should be offered to the gods. 

But the wicked man, offering honors to the gods based on his own disposition and from the things he himself craves, conducts himself impiously rather than piously. For this reason, Plato considers that a philosopher ought not to associate with those who have been poorly habituated; for this would be neither pleasing to the gods nor useful to men. Instead, he wishes the philosopher to strive so that men might be changed for the better; but if he is unable to do so, he...

# BOOK II.

...himself so as not to fall into their evil habits. Instead, he believes he must proceed along the straight path, taking no account of any danger or the bad reputation <note>original: "infamiæ"; literally infamy or disgrace in the eyes of the public</note> assigned by the common crowd. 

For it would indeed be an absurd thing if—while the **Syrians** refuse to eat fish, and the **Hebrews** avoid pigs, and many of the **Phoenicians** and **Egyptians** will in no way taste the meat of female cattle—and while they have chosen to undergo death rather than violate their ancestral traditions even when many kings used force against them, we should not fear to transgress the **laws of nature** and **divine precepts** simply because of human terrors or some slanderous talk. 

Truly, the divine assembly—of both the gods and of divine men—could deservedly be moved to great indignation if they saw us turned toward the opinions of wicked men and terrified by their empty rumors. This is especially true for us, who are accustomed to display to others in our daily lives the **meditation on death**. <term>meditation on death<gloss>Latin: "mortis meditationem"; a central Platonic concept (meletē thanatou) where the philosopher practices detaching the soul from the physical needs and passions of the body.</gloss></term>

# PORPHYRY ON
## ABSTINENCE FROM EATING
### ANIMALS
# BOOK THREE.

->Translated by Giovanni Bernardo Feliciano.<- <note>original: "Ioanne Bernardo Feliciano interprete"; Feliciano was a 16th-century scholar known for translating Greek works into Latin.</note>

**N**either to temperance and frugality, nor to piety—which are of the greatest importance for the contemplative life—does the eating of animals contribute; rather, as we have demonstrated in the two previous books, O **Firmus Castricius**, it actually opposes these virtues. <note>Firmus Castricius was a friend and student of Porphyry who had abandoned vegetarianism, prompting Porphyry to write this defense.</note> Since true justice finds its chief beauty in piety toward the gods, and since that piety arises primarily from abstinence, there is no need to fear that we will in any way violate our duty toward men <note>original: "ius erga homines"; referring to the "rights" or "laws" governing human interaction</note> if we preserve our holiness toward the gods. 

Indeed, **Socrates**—speaking against those who asserted that pleasure is the highest good—said that even if every pig and goat were to agree with them, he would never believe that our happiness is situated in pleasure, so long as the intellect should rule over all things. <note>Socrates refers to animals here to suggest that a life based purely on physical pleasure is fit for beasts, not rational humans.</note> As for us, neither...

<meta>This page continues the transition into Book III of Porphyry’s "On Abstinence." Having established that meat-eating hinders the spiritual life in Books I and II, Porphyry now begins his systematic defense of the idea that justice must be extended to animals. # BOOK III.

If all wolves and vultures were to praise the eating of meat, we would never grant that they say what is just, so long as man is harmless by nature and refrains from securing pleasures for himself through the injury of others. <note>Porphyry is continuing an analogy from the previous page: just because a predator finds meat "good" does not mean it is "just" for a rational human to follow suit.</note> Therefore, let us pass to the discussion of justice. Because those who oppose this decree <note>The "decree" refers to the rule of abstinence from animal flesh.</note> assert that justice only extends toward beings similar to ourselves, and for that reason exclude irrational animals from all rights regarding humans, come: let us put forward the true and **Pythagorean** opinion on this matter. <note>Pythagoras and his followers famously believed in the "kinship" of all living things, often based on the idea that souls could migrate between human and animal bodies.</note> We shall demonstrate that every soul which shares in sense-perception and memory is also rational. For if this is demonstrated, it will be established—even according to the opinion of our opponents—that justice pertains to all animals.

We shall briefly report those things which have been handed down in the records of the ancients. Since, then, there is a twofold sense of <term>reason</term> <note>original: "ratio, seu oratio"; here Porphyry refers to the Greek concept of **Logos**, which means both the internal thought and the external speech.</note> according to the opinion of the **Stoics**, one is internal—that is, *internal reason* <note>original Greek: "ἐνδιάθετος" (endiathetos); this refers to the thought process or "the word residing in the mind."</note>—which is arranged in the mind; the other is external, which is expressed by the voice. Furthermore, one kind of reason is right and the other is depraved. We must distinguish which of these they claim animals lack. Do they say that animals lack only "right" reason, and do not absolutely lack all reason, or do they say they lack every kind of reason entirely—both that which is internal and that which proceeds externally?

They certainly seem to attribute the deprivation of all reason to animals, not just "right" reason; for otherwise, they would not call them "irrational." If animals possessed reason but it was merely "depraved" (not "right"), then they would be rational beings, in the same way that almost all men are considered rational by the Stoics...

<meta>catchword: fere</meta>

<summary>Porphyry begins Book III by arguing that if animals possess memory and sense-perception, they must also possess reason. He introduces the Stoic distinction between internal thought and external speech to prepare a counter-argument against the claim that animals are "irrational."</summary>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE</header>
<sig>iii O</sig>
<meta>catchword: For; mirrored page numbers 102 and 201 from facing page/verso are visible at the bottom margin due to bleed-through</meta>

would be held <note>The word "fos" is the end of "stultos," meaning "fools." The sentence completes the thought that if "reason" only meant "perfect reason," almost all humans would be considered fools.</note> as such: for since, according to their opinion, only one or at most two **Wise Men** are ever found—in whom alone "right reason" is seen—all the rest are depraved. Even though some make a little progress, others are held back, wallowing in the chaos of depravity. Yet, nevertheless, all of these humans are considered rational by them.

Therefore, the **Stoics**, led by an excessive self-love, call all other animals <term>irrational</term> <note>original: "irrationalia"</note>. By this term "irrational," they want to express that which is completely deprived of reason. But if we are to speak the truth, reason is not only seen absolutely in all animals, but in many of them, a form of reason is observed which displays signs approaching perfection.

Since, then, <term>reason and speech</term> <note>original: "ratio atque oratio"; Porphyry is translating the Greek concept of **Logos**, which encompasses both internal thought and external expression.</note> are twofold, as we have said—one consisting in the disposition of the mind, the other in pronunciation—we shall begin first with that which is uttered and consists in the voice. If, therefore, speech which is uttered outwardly is a sound that signifies and expresses with the tongue the impressions that are within the soul (for this is the common definition, not yet tied to any philosophical school, but simply declaring the concept of speech), what, I ask, is missing from the other animals endowed with voice? Do they not also first experience impressions in the soul, and do they not think before they express themselves with the voice? For I call "thought" <note>original: "cogitationem"</note> that which is spoken in the soul through silence.

<summary>Porphyry challenges the Stoic denial of animal reason by arguing that if "reason" is defined as the ability to translate internal soul-impressions into vocal signals, then animals clearly possess it. He distinguishes between internal thought (the "silent" word) and external speech.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument from Book III of *On Abstinence*, where he challenges the Stoic belief that only humans possess "reason" (logos). Having established that reason exists both as an internal thought and an external expression, he now argues that animal vocalizations constitute a form of "speech" governed by natural laws, comparing the human inability to understand animals to the way Greeks fail to understand foreign "barbarian" languages.</meta>

### BOOK III.

Since, therefore, whatever is uttered by the voice through the tongue—in whatever way it is expressed, whether in a barbarian, Greek, <term>canine</term> <note>original: "canine"; dog-like</note>, or <term>equine</term> <note>original: "equine"; horse-like</note> manner—is speech <note>original: "oratio"; here Porphyry uses "oratio" to translate the Greek *logos prophorikos*, or uttered speech</note>, all other animals that possess a voice are capable of it. However, while humans speak according to human law <note>original: "lege humana"; referring to human social conventions and linguistic rules</note>, other animals speak according to the law that the gods and nature have granted to each of them. 

If we do not understand them, what does that matter? For the **Greeks** do not understand the language of the **Indians**, nor that of the **Scythians**, the **Thracians**, or the **Syrians** who were raised in **Attica** <note>The region surrounding Athens, known for the "purest" Greek dialect; Porphyry is noting that even foreigners living in Greece remain unintelligible to the locals</note>. Rather, the sound of one person's voice strikes the ears of another just like the <term>clanging of cranes</term> <note>original: "gruum clangor"; a common classical trope used to describe the "noise" of foreign languages</note>. 

And yet, each person's voice is literate and articulate to themselves, just as ours is to us; but the voice of **Syrians** or **Persians**, if we happen upon it, seems "unlettered" and inarticulate to us, just as the voice of all other animals seems to humans. For just as we—being ignorant of the speech of the **Scythians**, for example—perceive only a certain noise and clamor, so that they seem to us to merely emit a "clang" and express nothing clearly, but rather use a single sound that is either longer or shorter; and just as that variety of sound carries no meaning at all for us, yet to them their own speech is easy to perceive and full of distinction (just as ours is to us); in the same way, it happens with all other animals. To them, the voice of each species is clear and [percepti-] <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely completing as "perceptible" on the following page.</note> according to their kind.

<meta>catchword: -ble (perceptibilis)</meta>

<summary>Porphyry argues that animal vocalizations are a form of speech governed by natural law, comparing the human inability to understand animals to the mutual unintelligibility of different human languages.</summary>

...unintelligible <note>original: "bilis est"; completing the word "inintelligibilis" from the previous page</note>: to us, it is heard only as a hollow sound, empty of all meaning. And this happens precisely because no one from among the animals, being learned in our tongue, has been able to act as an interpreter to explain to us the things that are said among the other living creatures. 

Yet, if we are to believe the ancients, as well as those who have emerged in our own age and the age of our ancestors, there are some who are said to have perceived and understood the speech <note>original: "loquutionem"</note> of other animals. Such were **Melampus** and **Tiresias** <note>Famous seers of Greek mythology; Melampus was said to have had his ears licked by snakes, giving him the power to understand creatures</note> within the memory of the ancients, and others of this kind; and, not much before our time, **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>A 1st-century Greek philosopher and reputed miracle-worker often compared to Pythagoras</note>. 

Of **Apollonius**, indeed, it is told that while he was in the company of friends, a swallow flew toward them and chirped something; he then told the others that the bird was announcing to its fellows that they should hurry, because a donkey loaded with a sack of grain had fallen outside the city, and the grain lay scattered and spread across the ground. 

Indeed, a certain friend of ours used to tell us that he happened to have a servant boy who understood the speech of birds exceptionally well. He claimed they were all <term>prophetic</term> <note>original: "divinatrices"; literally "diviners," referring to the ancient belief that birds were messengers of the gods (augury)</note> and foretold things that were about to happen shortly thereafter. However, the friend said the boy was later deprived of this understanding by his mother; fearing that he might be sent as a gift to the King <note>likely referring to the Persian King, who sought out talented or "magical" servants</note>, she had urinated into his ears while he was sleeping <note>A bit of ancient folk magic; it was believed that certain substances could "close" the spiritual or physical channels of the senses</note>. 

However, let us set these stories aside for the present, owing to the feeling of disbelief that is natural to us. Nevertheless, there is no one who [does not know] <margin>some nations have a kinship for perceiving animal speech</margin>...

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument in *On Abstinence*, Book III, regarding the rationality of animals. ### BOOK III.

...who does not know? The Arabs understand the cawing of crows, and the Tyrrhenians <note>The Etruscans of ancient Italy, who were famous for their mastery of divination and interpreting nature</note> understand the cries of eagles. Perhaps we, too—and all other human beings—would perceive the voices of every animal if only a dragon <note>original: "draco"; in Greek and Roman myth, serpents were often associated with secret knowledge. This refers specifically to the legend of Melampus, whose ears were licked clean by snakes, granting him the ability to understand animal speech.</note> had cleansed our ears. 

Indeed, the variety and diversity of their voices undoubtedly seem to carry a specific meaning. For they are heard to cry out in one way when they are afraid; in another when they call to one another; in another when they invite others to eat; in another when they kindly greet one another; and in another when they provoke one another to battle. 

And so great is this diversity that it is extremely difficult to distinguish between their sounds because of their sheer number, even for those who have devoted their entire lives to this study. For the <term>augurs</term> <note>Priests in the ancient world who interpreted the will of the gods by observing the flight and songs of birds</note>, after making observations of the chatter of the crow and the raven up to a certain point, omitted the rest as things that could not easily be perceived by a human being. 

Moreover, even when they are not "speaking" in the human sense, they clearly communicate meaning, even if those meanings are not known to us all. If they are observed to imitate us, to learn the Greek language, and to understand those who look after them, who would be so shameless as to deny that they are <term>rational beings</term> <note>original: "rationalia"; possessing the faculty of 'logos' or reason, a key debate in ancient philosophy regarding whether animals have souls similar to humans</note> simply because he himself cannot understand what they are saying? 

For ravens, magpies, robins <note>original: "erithaci"; likely the European robin or redbreast</note>, and parrots imitate human beings; they remember what they hear, and while being taught, they listen to and understand their teacher. Indeed, many animals have even used what they learned to expose those who were committing crimes within a household,

<sig>P</sig>
<meta>catchword: ac (and)</meta>

<summary>Porphyry argues that animal vocalizations are a complex language with specific meanings for fear, greeting, and hunger. He asserts that the ability of birds like parrots and ravens to imitate and understand human speech proves they possess rational minds.</summary>
<keywords>animal intelligence, language, augury, rationality, Porphyry, birds, communication, ancient philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument in Book III of *On Abstinence*, where he asserts that animals possess a form of reason and language. -># ON ABSTINENCE<-

->9<-

...and made them manifest. Truly, the Indian hyena, which the natives call the **corocotta**, even without any teacher, speaks in such a human manner that it is accustomed to frequent houses and call out to anyone it recognizes can be easily overpowered. For it imitates the speech of a person's dearest friends, or of someone the called person would definitely obey; thus, the Indians—even though they know of this trick—are deceived by the similarity. Obeying the voice and stepping outside, they are devoured and perish. 

If not all animals imitate us, or if not all are suited to learn our speech, what does it matter? For not even all humans are quick to learn, or capable of imitating—I will not even mention the voices of other animals—but they cannot even master five of the various languages <note>original: "loquutiones"; Porphyry is arguing that human linguistic ability is also limited and variable, so animal limitations should not be used to prove a lack of reason.</note> found among men. 

This is not to mention that some animals perhaps do not speak because they are never taught, or because they are hindered by the inadequacy of their <term>vocal organs</term> <note>original: "instrumentorum uocis"; referring to the physical anatomy of the throat and tongue.</note>. When I was in Carthage <note>A major Roman city in North Africa (modern Tunisia) where Porphyry lived and studied.</note>, I raised a certain tame partridge that had flown to my home. In time, it was brought to such a state of tameness that it would not only play, fawn, and act affectionately toward me, but it would even call back in response to my voice. As far as it was able, it would even answer me—using a different sound than the one partridges usually use to call one another. Indeed, it was not while I was silent, but while I was speaking and calling to it, that it in turn [gave its] voice

-># BOOK II.<-

...was emitting. Likewise, it is recorded that some animals lacking a human voice have obeyed their masters so promptly that no human could match them. For the <term>Muraena</term> <note>A type of moray eel; pet eels were a famous luxury and curiosity in ancient Rome.</note> belonging to the Roman **Crassus** <note>Lucius Licinius Crassus (140–91 BCE), a celebrated orator who famously mourned the death of his pet eel.</note> was so accustomed to him that whenever it was called by name, it would come to him. By this obedience, it had so affected his mind that Crassus—who had previously borne the loss of his <term>triumphs</term> <note>Public celebrations and honors for military victories.</note> with moderation—actually wept for the creature when it died. Many have also handed down to memory that they heard people calling to eels in **Arethusa** and to <term>saperdae</term> <note>A type of small fish, possibly a variety of perch or herring.</note> near the **Meander** river.

Therefore, the **imagination** <note>original: "imaginatio"; here referring to the internal thought or mental concept that precedes speech.</note> of the speaker—whether it comes forth through the tongue or does not—is undoubtedly the same. For this reason, I do not see how it is anything but absurd if we wish to call only the human voice "speech" simply because it is perceived by us, while we reject the communications of all other animals. 

It is exactly as if crows were to judge that only their own speech is true language, and were to think us irrational because we do not speak in a way that is clear to them; or as if the **Athenians** were to say that only the Attic dialect is true speech, and consider all others who lacked the Attic way of talking to be irrational. And yet, an Athenian would perhaps perceive the meaning of a crow's voice more quickly than he would understand the speech of **Syrians** or **Persians** speaking in their own tongues. But it is not consistent to judge whether a being is rational or irrational based on our perception or ignorance of their speech, or based on their silence or voice. For by that same logic, one would have to say that the God who is above all, and

...all others <note>This continues the thought from the previous page: just as Greeks might judge non-Greeks, humans might judge animals.</note> to be irrational because they do not speak. But the gods, certainly, indicate things by keeping silent; and birds perceive their meanings more quickly than humans do, and having perceived them, they announce them to us in the way they are able. Different birds are messengers of different gods to humans: the eagle for **Jupiter** <note>original: "Iouis" (Jove/Zeus)</note>; the hawk and the crow for **Apollo**; the stork for **Juno**; the <term>corncrake</term> <note>original: "crex"; a rail-like bird known for its distinctive grating call.</note> and the owl for **Minerva**; the crane for **Ceres**, and others for the rest of the gods. 

Furthermore, those among us who observe and spend their lives alongside certain animals recognize what their voices mean. For a hunter notices, from the variety of barking, whether the dog is searching for a hare, has found it, has caught it, or is just wandering aimlessly. Likewise, a cowherd understands from the various lowing of an ox whether it is hungry, thirsty, or exhausted; whether it is <term>in heat</term> <note>original: "in uenerem incitari"; literally "incited toward Venus."</note>, or looking for its calf. A lion also displays threats with its roar, just as a wolf indicates its distress with a howl. 

Through bleating, it is also clear to shepherds what their sheep require. But human voices are not hidden from the animals either—whether people are angry, or flattering, or calling, or driving them away, or wishing to seek or give something. Instead, they are observed to obey all such voices appropriately; this could in no way happen unless a similar intelligence <note>original: "intelligentia"; Porphyry argues that communication requires a shared cognitive spark between the speaker and the listener.</note> responded to something similar. Deer and bulls are even soothed by certain melodies,

...[tamed] <note>The text begins mid-word, likely "domari" (to be tamed), continuing from the previous page's discussion on human-animal interaction.</note>, and other animals, and even those from the wild are made tame. Add to this that even those very people who judge other animals to be irrational assert that dogs are, in a certain way, capable of **dialectic** <note>original: "dialecticae"; the art of logical discussion and reasoning.</note>. They point specifically to the proposition which they call a <term>disjunctive syllogism</term> <note>original: "ex pluribus disiunctum"; a logical argument that presents several alternatives and eliminates all but one.</note> involving several options, which occurs when dogs, while tracking wild beasts, come upon a <term>crossroads</term> <note>original: "triuium"; literally a place where three roads meet.</note>. 

For they say that the dogs reason in this way: "The beast went either this way, or that way, or the other way; but it went neither this way, nor that way; therefore, it fled the other way." Once they have gathered this conclusion, they are accustomed to throw themselves into a run down that remaining path. Even though a response is readily available to the critics—that dogs have these behaviors implanted by nature because they were taught by no one—it is as if we ourselves did not also have reason or speech implanted by nature, even if we later impose certain names on things because we are naturally suited for that task. 

If, however, **Aristotle** is to be believed, other animals are observed to teach their young, not only how they might do other things, but also how they should emit their voice; just as the nightingale teaches her chicks to sing. And as he himself also says, there are many things which animals have become accustomed to learn from each other, and likewise many things from humans. Everyone bears witness that he speaks the truth in this: all tamers of horse colts, all grooms, horsemen, charioteers, hunters, ox-drivers, elephant keepers, and finally all masters of beasts and birds. From the consideration of these indeed...

# ON ABSTINENCE

No one who is a prudent and fair judge of the matter will doubt that other animals are also capable of intelligence; truly, only an unfair and thoughtless person would be rashly driven to advocate for his own convenience <note>The author suggests that people deny animal intelligence simply because it is more "convenient" to treat them as unthinking objects.</note> derived from them. For will someone who does not fear slaughtering and crushing them—just as if they were stones—refrain, of course, from slandering and misrepresenting them? But indeed, **Aristotle**, **Plato**, **Empedocles**, **Pythagoras**, **Democritus**, and all who have striven to investigate the truth about them, have judged that other living creatures also participate in reason.

It remains for us to demonstrate that reason and speech <note>original: "rationem atq; orationem"; in the original Greek of Porphyry, this refers to *logos endiathetos* (internal thought) and *logos prophorikos* (spoken word).</note>, which are arranged within the soul, also exist in them. However, there appears to be a difference—as **Aristotle** himself also says—not in essence, but in what is considered "more or less" <note>This refers to a difference in degree rather than a difference in kind; animals possess the same quality of reason as humans, but perhaps to a lesser extent.</note>. In the same way, some believe the gods also differ from us, so that the distinction between us and them is understood to be established not in substance, but in a more or less refined reason. 

And as far as the senses are concerned, as well as the mechanical arrangement of both the sensing parts and the entire flesh, there is almost no one who does not concede that other animals are affected in the same way as we are. For they not only share a likeness with us in the natural emotions and movements that exist within them, but also in those things which occur contrary to nature and are diseased,

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section where the author discussed the shared faculties of the soul between humans and animals. ### BOOK III.

Since these qualities are present, there is a great kinship <note>original: "communicatio"; implies a shared nature or fellowship.</note> between us and them. Nor indeed, on account of the diversity of their behavior and the form of their bodies, should anyone who thinks correctly assert that they are devoid of reason and speech. For among humans, too, one may observe much variety of this kind according to the diversity of nations and peoples, and yet one concedes that all of them are nonetheless rational.

To come to the point, the **donkey**, for example, is accustomed to being seized by a dripping of phlegm <note>original: "pituitae destillatione"; likely referring to catarrh or a respiratory infection.</note>; if this flows down into the lungs, the animal is finished by the ailment and dies, just like a man. The **horse** suffers from abscesses, and like a man, it is consumed and wastes away. Likewise, stiffness attacks the horse, as do gout <note>original: "podagra"</note>, fever, and madness <note>original: "rabies"</note>, when it is even said to be "head-heavy" <note>original: "καρωπᾶν" (karopan); a Greek term describing an animal being dazed or lethargic with its head hanging toward the ground.</note>, that is, to be carried along with its head lowered to the earth. 

A **mare** carrying a fetus in her womb, if she happens to smell an extinguished lamp, suffers a miscarriage—a thing which is also known to happen to a human woman. The **ox** also is seized by fever and goes mad, just as the **camel** does. Furthermore, the **crow** is affected by both mange and leprosy, as is the **dog**; but the dog also suffers from gout and is driven by madness. The **pig** is troubled by hoarseness, and even more so the dog; for this reason, the disease in humans is even named "dog-strangle" <note>original: "κυνάγχη" (kynanche), often Latinized as *caninangina*. It refers to a severe inflammation of the throat or "angina" that makes the sufferer gasp like a dog.</note> after the dog. 

And these things are known to us because they occur in animals that live among us; however, we are ignorant of many other creatures because of our lack of familiarity with them. Moreover, if animals are "cut" <note>This refers to castration.</note>, they become effeminate; and even **roosters**...

<summary>The author argues for the kinship between humans and animals by highlighting their shared susceptibility to the same physical diseases and biological reactions, such as gout, fever, and miscarriage.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the biological and physiological similarities between humans and animals. Following the previous page's list of shared diseases (like mange and leprosy), the text now examines how physical changes—specifically castration—affect animals and humans in identical ways. It then shifts from the body to the soul, arguing that animal senses are not only present but often superior to those of humans.</meta>

...they no longer sing, but change their voice to the manner of a female, 
just as men are accustomed to do. Similarly, it is not possible to 
distinguish the horns or the voice of a **castrated ox** from those of a 
female. **Stags**, however, no longer shed their antlers, but 
retain them, just as eunuchs retain their hair; but if they 
happen to lack antlers when they are castrated, they never 
produce them again, just as those who are made eunuchs before 
they have grown a beard. And thus, in almost every respect, 
their bodies behave like ours when it comes to contracting 
diseases. As for the **affections of the soul**, see if they 
are not also similar in every way.

And first, to speak of the **senses**: it is certainly not the case that 
the human sense of taste perceives flavors <note>original: "succos"; literally juices or saps.</note>, sight perceives colors, 
smell perceives odors, hearing perceives sounds, or touch 
perceives heat, cold, and the like, while the senses of 
other animals do not. For other animals should not be 
said to lack senses simply because they are not human, 
just as reason should not be stripped from us just because 
the gods are rational beings <note>original: "rationales dii"; the author argues that just as humans are rational even if gods are more so, animals possess senses (and reason) even if they differ from humans.</note>. On the contrary, it is evident 
that we are far surpassed by other animals. 

For what man is there—even if he were the legendary 
**Lynceus** <note>In Greek mythology, an Argonaut famous for his superhuman sight, capable of seeing through trees, stones, and into the ground.</note>—who can see as far as a **dragon** <note>original: "draco"; in the ancient world, this term referred to large serpents, which were believed to have exceptionally keen, unblinking eyes.</note>? From which the poets 
even derived the word "to see" <note>original: "δρακειν" (drakein); a Greek verb meaning to see or gaze clearly, from which the word for serpent is etymologically derived.</note>. The **eagle**, 
looking down even from the high air, does not miss the sight 
of a deer. Who hears more sharply than **cranes**? They 
are accustomed to perceiving sounds from a distance so 
great that humans cannot even see that far.

### BOOK III.

...can be. For in the sense of **smell**, almost all other animals so greatly excel humans that those things which are hidden from us by no means escape their keenness; indeed, they recognize each thing individually by smelling the very track <note>original: "vestigio"; referring to the scent trail left by a footprint.</note> itself. From this, humans are also accustomed to use the **guidance of dogs**, so that they may understand which way they must go to find a **boar** or a **stag**. Indeed, a change in the state of the air is felt late by us, but quickly by other animals, so that we take signs of future storms from them. Moreover, they have so exact a distinction of **juices** <note>original: "succorum"; in this context, it refers to the flavors, saps, or medicinal properties of plants.</note> that they discern the diseased, the healthy, and the poisonous better and more exquisitely than any human physician can. But **Aristotle** asserts that those who are endowed with more vigorous senses are also more **prudent** <note>original: "prudentiora"; meaning possessing practical wisdom or intelligence.</note>. The diversity of bodies can indeed cause them to be affected either easily or with difficulty, or to have a more or less prompt use of **reason**; but it cannot change the **soul** in its essence, since it has neither changed nor entirely taken away the senses or the passions. Therefore, it must be conceded that the difference lies in this: that they are endowed with more or less **reason** <term>reason: "ratio", often translating the Greek "logos", implying internal thought</term> or **speech** <term>speech: "oratio", implying the outward expression of thought</term>, not that they lack them entirely; nor that we possess it while they do not. For just as in one and the same kind of body there is great diversity in both the fitness and unfitness for receiving **health** or **disease**, so it is in souls...

## BOOK III.

...also others <note>This continues a thought from the previous page, likely referring to types of reason or faculties.</note> are found to be good, while others are base; and among the base, some are more so and some less. Even among the good, there is no perfect equality. For **Socrates**, **Aristotle**, and **Plato** were not good in the exact same manner. Furthermore, even among those who hold the same school of thought, there is not an entirely identical conformity. Therefore, if we also possess more understanding than other animals, intelligence <note>original: "intelligentia"</note> is not for that reason to be denied to those other animals.

Just as **partridges** are not to be deprived of the quality of **flight** <term>flight: "uolatu", here referring to the essential ability to fly</term> simply because **hawks** fly more powerfully than they do; nor should other hawks be thought to lack flight because the bird called the **goshawk** <note>original: "palumbarius"; a bird of prey specialized for hunting pigeons and known for its superior speed.</note> surpasses them and all others. One might therefore concede that the **soul** has a certain harmony with the body, such that the soul is affected by whether the body is in a good or bad state, but its fundamental nature does not change. 

<margin>soup</margin> <note>This appears to be a later handwritten addition to the manuscript, possibly a librarian's mark or an abbreviated reference.</note>

If the soul is only affected alongside the body and uses it as an **instrument**, then if that body is arranged differently than our own, the soul will be able to perform many things through it that we cannot. At the same time, though the soul is affected when the body is in a certain state, it will not change its underlying nature. It must therefore be demonstrated that the **rational power** of the soul exists in other animals too, and that they do not lack **practical wisdom** <term>practical wisdom: "prudentia", translating the Greek "phronesis", meaning the ability to act intelligently and with foresight for one's own preservation.</term> 

For first, every animal knows best whether it is weak or robust; and because of this, it guards against some and uses others for its own defense. The **leopard**, for instance, [uses its] teeth...

# BOOK II.

...defends itself: the **lion** with claws and also with teeth; the **horse** with hooves; the **ox** with horns; the **rooster** with its spur; the **scorpion** with its sting <note>original: "acu", literally a needle</note>. **Egyptian serpents**, however, defend themselves with spit, for which reason they are even called **ptyades** <note>original: "πτυάδες", from the Greek for "to spit"</note>, that is, "spitters"; they aim their spit at the eyes of those attacking and blind them.

And so it is clear that different creatures use different means to preserve their own safety. Furthermore, whatever animals are strong and robust graze and live far away from human beings. Conversely, the weaker and more timid ones, just as they live far from other more ferocious beasts, are accustomed to leading their lives among humans. Of these, some live a bit further off, like **sparrows** and **swallows** on rooftops; others live closer, even together with humans themselves, such as **dogs**.

Add to this that they also change their locations according to the seasons and know all things that pertain to their own advantage. In **fish** also, and in **birds**, this kind of reason and planning can be perceived no less. Indeed, all these matters are treated broadly and at length by the ancients in the books they wrote concerning the **prudence of animals** <term>prudence of animals: "animalium prudentia," a concept in ancient philosophy suggesting that animals possess a practical, innate intelligence for survival and social life</term>.

Among these authors is **Aristotle**, who investigated these matters more accurately than anyone else. He says that all things have been excellently devised for all animals to ensure their sustenance and safety. But he who claims these qualities are in them "by nature" fails to realize he is asserting either that nature itself is rational, or that reason is not in us by nature; for reason does not reach the growth of its perfection simply because we are naturally suited for it. God, certainly, in no way...

### ii 9

...rational discipline <note>original: "disciplina rationalis," referring to an ordered system of training or knowledge</note> emerged: for there never was a time when it was irrational; rather, as soon as it existed, it also stood forth as rational. Nothing could stand in the way of it being so, since no discipline has ever "acquired" reason <note>The author argues that reason is an inherent quality of the soul's training, not a skill added later</note>. But in animals, just as in humans, there are many things which nature has taught directly, and others which are obtained later through training. For they learn certain things from one another and, as has already been said, even from humans. They are also endowed with **memory**, which is a principal part of the capacity for reason and **prudence** <term>prudence: "prudentia," or practical wisdom—the ability to apply reason to everyday survival and social conduct</term>. 

Added to this is the fact that **vices** and **envies** are also observed in them, although they are not as widespread as they are among humans; indeed, their vices are much lighter than those of humans. This is something we can easily observe from the following: if a man were building a house, he would never lay its foundations unless he were sober and in control of his mind; nor, if he were constructing a ship, would he set the keel unless he were of sound mind; nor, if he were tilling a field, would he plant a vine unless he turned his mind specifically to that task. Yet, for the **procreating of children**, almost all men occupy themselves while **drunk**.

But other animals do not behave this way; rather, they produce offspring solely for the sake of progeny. And most of them, once they have made the female pregnant, do not attempt to mate further; nor, if they should try, does the female tolerate it. In this matter, no one can doubt how much human insolence and **intemperance** exists. Furthermore, among other animals, the labor-pains of one...

# BOOK III.

...recognizes <note>original: "gnoscit"; the subject refers back to the animal parent's ability to identify its own kind or offspring</note>: and most even bring forth their young at the same time, just as poultry do; some even incubate eggs together, like doves; and they take care to seek out the places where they are to give birth. Likewise, once each animal has produced its offspring, it cleanses <note>original: "expiat," which implies both physical cleaning and a ritual-like purification after birth</note> both itself and the fetus brought into the light. In these matters, if anyone observes correctly, they will notice that everything proceeds with order and reason. 

For everyone knows that those creatures which are social <note>original: "gregalia," referring to animals that live in herds or colonies</note> run to meet their parent with fawning, and recognize their master, and signal against the ambushes of strangers, so as to preserve the law which exists among them. This is most clearly evident among ants and bees, and other similar creatures. 

Who is unaware of the **chastity** <note>The author uses "castitas" to describe the lifelong fidelity often attributed to certain birds in ancient natural history</note> of wood-pigeons toward their mates? If they happen to have been forced by an adulterer, and the mate catches him, they peck and kill him. Who has not perceived the **justice** of storks toward their parents? <term>justice: "iustitia," here referring to the famous ancient belief in "stork-law" (antipelargosis), where young storks were said to feed and carry their elderly parents</term> 

For a certain proper virtue excels in each one, for which it has been made suitable by nature: so that neither nature, nor the stability of these virtues, should take away from them their **rational power**. For that point might perhaps be refuted if the works of virtue and rational skill were not clearly seen. Although we do not understand how these things are done—because we are not able to penetrate into their thoughts—they should not, for that reason, be condemned by us as if they were thought to be entirely lacking in reason. For since we are not able to enter into the mind of God in any way, we agree [that He is wise] only by looking at His works...

...to those who have asserted that they <note>original: "ipsum"; referring to the animal nature or soul discussed in the preceding lines</note> are intellectual and rational. One might justly wonder at those people who, although they establish justice based on reason, call those animals that have no fellowship with humans "wild" and "unjust," yet do not extend justice so far as to say it reaches even those animals with whom a **fellowship** <note>original: "societas"; a technical term in ancient philosophy for a community bound by mutual rights and duties</note> does exist. For it is necessary that for these animals, no differently than for humans, if fellowship is removed, life itself is also taken away.

For birds, dogs, and many other quadrupeds—such as goats, horses, sheep, donkeys, and mules—perish if they are deprived of human fellowship. It seems that Nature, which produced them, established them in need of humans, and humans in need of them, for the very purpose of engendering a **common law** <term>common law: "commune ius," referring here to a shared moral or natural bond that exists between different species that live together</term> both for them toward us, and for us toward them. 

If there are some animals that behave wildly toward humans, it is not to be wondered at. For that which is said by **Aristotle** <note>The author cites Aristotle’s "History of Animals" (Book IX) to explain that animal behavior is often a response to environment rather than innate malice</note> is true: "If," he says, "there were an abundance of food for all, they would exercise no ferocity toward one another, nor toward humans." 

Indeed, all their friendships and enmities arise for the sake of food—however meager and necessary—and for the sake of shelter. For if humans were reduced to that same scarcity of necessities by which other animals are oppressed, how much more fierce and monstrous must we think they would become, even compared to those creatures which seem wild? This war indica... <note>original catchword: "cat"; likely for "indicat" (indicates) or "indicit" (declares) on the following page</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that humans and domestic animals share a natural "fellowship" and mutual dependence that necessitates a common sense of justice. He cites Aristotle to suggest that animal aggression is not a sign of irrationality, but a result of competition for scarce resources like food and shelter.</summary>

# BOOK III.

...nother <note>original: "cat," completing "ferocitatem"; ferocity</note>, a state in which they do not even abstain from devouring one another. Furthermore, even in the absence of war or famine, they do not hesitate to kill and eat the **tame** animals that live alongside them. But someone might say that while these animals are indeed **rational** <note>original: "rationalia"; possessing the faculty of reason (logos)</note>, they nevertheless have no regard for us. 

Initially, they [the opponents of animal rights] tried to make them out to be irrational, and because they were irrational, they asserted that animals have no regard for us. Then, they claimed that any **fellowship** <note>original: "societatem"; a social and moral bond</note> we have with them is based on the utility we derive from them, rather than on reason. Our purpose, however, is to demonstrate whether they are rational, not whether they have entered into any formal pact with us. Indeed, not even all humans are joined to us by a formal agreement, yet no one would say that those who do not share our agreements are therefore irrational. 

And yet, many of these animals have even served humans; and as a certain author quite rightly said, although they may serve through the ignorance and injustice of men, the animals themselves—through their own **wisdom and justice**—have turned their masters into their own servants and caretakers. 

Even their vices are clearly visible, and from these, the rational faculty shines through. For they experience envy, and the males pursue the females—and the females the males—with **jealousy** <term>jealousy: "zelotypia," a complex social emotion that Porphyry argues requires a level of cognitive awareness and "rational" calculation of social standing</term>. However, they lack one particular vice: they do not attack those who are kind to them with treachery. Instead, they are so well-disposed toward them and trust them so completely that they follow them anywhere, even if they are being led to the **slaughter** <note>original: "iugulationem"; literally "throat-cutting," referring to the common end of livestock</note> or to manifest danger.

# ON ABSTINENCE

And although humans do not nourish them for the animals' own sake, but for their own benefit, the animals are moved with the greatest benevolence toward those who feed them. Humans, however, conspire so treacherously against no one as against those who sustain them; they desire the death of no one as much as they desire his. Furthermore, it is clear that they act with **deliberation and reason** <note>original: "consilio & ratione"; the capacity to plan and think logically</note> from this fact: they often notice that the food offered to them is part of a trap. Even so, they are accustomed to approach these baits through a lack of self-control or driven by intense hunger. 

Indeed, some do not approach immediately; others hesitate; others test whether they can depart safely after they have snatched and devoured the food. Thus it happens that often, when their appetite is overcome by **reason**, they abstain and go away. In fact, they sometimes even mock and urinate upon the fraudulent trickery of men. Others, however, succumbing to desire and gluttony—even though they are not unaware that they are about to be captured—nevertheless, just like the **companions of Ulysses** <note>A reference to Homer’s *Odyssey*; Ulysses’ men often succumbed to their appetites to their own ruin, such as when they were transformed into swine by Circe or ate the sacred cattle of the Sun god.</note>, do not hesitate to expose themselves to death, provided they may gorge themselves and eat.

There are some, moreover, who attempt to demonstrate—and not incorrectly—that many creatures are more prudent than we are, based on the locations they have been allotted. For just as those beings inhabiting the **ether** <term>ether: "aether," the purest, highest layer of the atmosphere, often thought to be the home of the gods and celestial bodies</term> are rational, they say that the same is true for those who occupy the next adjacent place, such as those who live their lives in the air. To these they join aquatic creatures, and to the aquatic they join terrestrial ones, in a proportion based on their distance from the highest realms. We, of course, inhabit the lowest of these regions. For it is evident that among the gods, it is not merely because of their location <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "quoq;" indicates the next page begins with the word "also."</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals demonstrate rational judgment by recognizing traps and showing more gratitude to their caretakers than humans do. He also introduces a cosmic hierarchy suggesting that a creature's capacity for reason is linked to its elemental habitat (ether, air, water, or earth).</summary>
