### BOOK III.

their excellence must also be considered; however, in the case of mortals <note>original: "mortalibus"; here referring to humans</note>, the same thing should not be done. Since they even acquire arts, and human ones at that: they learn to dance, to drive chariots, to enter gladiatorial contests, and to walk on ropes; furthermore, even to write and read, to play the pipe and the lyre, and also to shoot arrows and to ride horses. Can you any longer doubt whether they possess that faculty which receives knowledge, when that which is received is seen within them? For where do they receive it, if reason <note>original: "ratio"; the logical and thinking part of the soul</note>—in which the arts reside—is not present?

For they do not hear our voice merely as a sound, but they also have a perception of the diversity of signs, which arises from rational intelligence <note>original: "intelligentia rationali"</note>. But they say, "they perform these human tasks poorly"? Yet not even all humans perform them correctly; otherwise, it would be in vain that some are victors in contests while others are defeated.

But they do not consult, nor do they deliberate in assemblies, nor do they hold trials. What is so strange about that, when not even all humans do those things? Do not many act before they have consulted? Furthermore, how can it be demonstrated that other animals do *not* consult? Indeed, no argument exists for this matter that can be brought forward; on the contrary, many arguments exist, provided to us by those who have written about the specific details of animals. Indeed, the other things they cry out against animals are rotten. "They have no cities," they say. But neither do the Scythians <note>A nomadic people of ancient Central Eurasia, often cited by ancient writers as examples of humans who lived without permanent cities.</note>, who live in wagons

...they wander about; nor even for the gods. They have no written laws. But not even humans had any, as long as they persisted in a state of happiness. For Apis <note>A mythical king of Argos, sometimes credited with the first laws</note> is said to have been the first among the Greeks to establish laws, when they finally had need of them. Indeed, to humans—on account of their greed and gluttony—the other animals seem to possess no reason <note>original: "rationem"; the capacity for logic and understanding</note>. But to the gods and to divine men, they are held in veneration just like sacred ministers.

Since the God <note>Apollo at Delphi</note>, when giving an answer to Aristodicus of Cyme <note>Aristodicus once removed a sparrow's nest from Apollo’s temple to test the god; Apollo spoke from the oracle to rebuke him for evicting his "suppliants"</note>, said that the sparrows were his ministers. Indeed, Socrates even used to swear by them <note>Socrates was famous for his unusual oaths, such as "by the dog" or "by the plane tree"</note>, and Rhadamanthus <note>A judge of the underworld known for his strict justice</note> did so before him. The Egyptians, however, even classified animals among the gods, whether because they truly thought they were gods, or because they intentionally assigned the faces of oxen, birds, and other animals to the gods so they might warn people to abstain from harming them no less than they abstain from humans; or perhaps they were led by some other more hidden reason.

In this same way, the Greeks added the horns of a ram to the statue of Jupiter <note>original: "Iouis"; specifically the syncretic god Zeus-Ammon</note>, and those of a bull to the image of Bacchus; likewise, they composed Pan from a human and a goat; they equipped the Muses and also the Sirens with wings. In a similar way, they fashioned Victory, Iris, Cupid, and Mercury as winged. Pindar indeed, in his odes, represented all the gods—when they were being pursued by Typhon—not as humans, but as resembling other animals <note>This refers to the myth where the Greek gods fled to Egypt and took animal forms to hide from the monster Typhon</note>. They claim that Jupiter, captured by love for Pasiphae <note>The text likely confuses Pasiphae with Europa, whom Jupiter approached as a bull, or perhaps refers to the broader theme of divine transformations</note>, took the form of a bull; at other times becoming an eagle, or a swan...

### BOOK III.

...are told <note>original: "fabulantur"; the text continues from the previous page's discussion of myths</note>: and in all these things, the ancients clearly displayed the honor they believed should be shown to animals. This is even more evident when they say that Jupiter <note>The Roman name for Zeus, the king of the gods</note> was raised by a goat. Moreover, among the Cretans, there was a law of Rhadamanthus <note>A legendary judge known for his inflexible integrity</note> which commanded that all animals be included when taking an oath. 

Nor did Socrates act out of mere playfulness when he swore "by the dog" and "by the goose"; rather, he swore seriously according to the decree of the just son of Jupiter <note>referring to Rhadamanthus</note>. Nor was it a joke when he called swans his "fellow-servants." Furthermore, there are stories that tell how, through the indignation of the gods, humans were transformed into animals, and that the gods later treated them with mercy and loved them. These stories seem to signify that other animals are endowed with the same soul as we are. Such are the accounts related about dolphins, kingfishers, nightingales, and swallows. 

Likewise, each of the ancients who had successfully received their upbringing from animals boasted not so much of their human parents as of their animal nurturers: one spoke of a she-wolf <note>referring to Romulus and Remus</note>, another of a deer, another of a goat, and another of a bee. Semiramis <note>A legendary queen of Assyria</note> boasted of doves, Cyrus <note>Cyrus the Great, founder of the Persian Empire</note> of a dog, and the Thracian of a swan—from which he even took his name <note>Cycnus, whose name means "swan" in Greek</note>. 

From this, the gods also received their various epithets: for Bacchus <note>Dionysus</note>, the "fawn"; for Apollo, the "wolf-like" and "dolphin-like"; for Neptune <note>Poseidon</note>, the "equestrian" <note>relating to horses</note>; and likewise for Minerva <note>Athena</note>. And indeed, it is said that Hecate <note>Goddess of magic and the crossroads</note> hears more readily if she is addressed as a bull, a dog, or a lioness.

But if those who sacrifice animals eat them, and do so in order to absolve themselves of guilt by claiming that the animals are irrational <note>original: "irrationalia"; lacking the power of speech or reason</note>, then in the same way, those Scythians <note>A group of ancient nomadic peoples known in Greek and Roman literature for their supposedly "barbaric" customs</note> who feast upon their parents could likewise say that their elders were devoid of reason. Yet by these arguments, and others which we will mention as we review the opinions of the ancients, it is demonstrated that the other animals are indeed rational: so much so that although their reason may be imperfect in most cases, it is not considered to be entirely lacking. Since, therefore, there ought to be justice <note>original: "ius"; right or law</note> toward rational beings, as our adversaries claim, why shall we not also have it toward animals? 

For justice is not to be extended to plants, in which a very great inconsistency with respect to reason is observed. Even so, we conduct ourselves more moderately toward plants: for we are accustomed only to use their fruits; we do not usually cut down the trunks themselves along with the fruit. Indeed, we are typically accustomed to collect the crops of grain and legumes only when they have already flowered and fallen to the earth and died. 

But no one would take the meat of dead animals for food—excepting fish—unless they had been killed by force <note>The author argues that while we eat plants when they die naturally or only take their "offspring" (fruit), we must violently kill animals to eat them, which he views as a moral distinction.</note>. In this matter, there is undoubtedly much injustice. Add to this, as Plutarch <note>A famous Greek philosopher and biographer (c. 46 – 119 AD) who wrote extensively on the cleverness of animals and the ethics of eating meat</note> also says, that just because our nature lacks certain things and uses them, it does not mean that injury must be continuously extended to all things. ### BOOK III. <note>original: "LIBER III."</note>

...even if they remain alive, taking anything from them is a kind of harm. However, to take and destroy other things <note>The author refers here to the killing of animals as opposed to the harvesting of plants</note> out of arrogance or for the sake of indulging in pleasures appears to be an act of the greatest savagery and injustice. This is especially true since abstaining from them is not an impediment to our living, nor to our living well. For if we stood in needed of the slaughter of animals and the eating of meat—just as we need air, water, plants, and fruits, without which it is impossible to lead a life—then our very nature would necessarily be bound to this injustice. 

But if many priests of the gods and kings of the barbarians <note>original: "barbarorum"; in this context, non-Greek peoples whose religious or dietary customs were often cited by philosophers</note> lead lives of piety and purity; and indeed, if countless species of animals live without ever tasting food of this kind at all, and yet are not frustrated in their natural purpose or well-being: how then is it not absurd for a person to claim that, because we are forced to wage war with some, we should not be commanded to live peacefully with those with whom it is possible? Such a person would suggest that we must either live by practicing justice toward no one, or that by practicing it toward everyone, we cannot live at all. 

Therefore, just as among men, he who for the sake of his own safety, his children, or his country either seizes the money of others or oppresses a region and city is considered to have a necessity that in some way excuses his injustice; but he who does the same thing led by the arrogance of wealth and pleasures, and not induced by any necessity, is judged to be unjust, lacking in self-control, and wicked. # ON ABSTINENCE <note>original: "DE ABSTINENTIA"</note>

...the detriments of many things, as well as the use of fire and water, the shearing of sheep, the gathering of milk, and the yoking of oxen, God grants to men. Since they use these things for their own safety and preservation, He forgives it. But to slaughter animals, and to eat their remains—cut up and full of carnage and blood—not for the sake of nourishment or the filling of a void, but for the sake of pleasure and gluttony, that indeed is unjust and wicked beyond measure. For is it not enough that we use them until they are exhausted, even though they ought not to be crushed by any such labors? We use horses and donkeys for carrying burdens; and the offspring of bulls, as Aeschylus <note>A famous Greek tragic playwright (c. 525–456 BCE).</note> says:

> Brought into servitude, to become the receiver 
> Of our labor, so that they may also submit to the yoke.

For he who judges that we should not feed upon the ox, nor seek seasonings for our tables from any breath <term>spiritu; here referring to a living being possessing "anima" or the breath of life</term> or destroyed life, in what way does he deprive human life of things necessary for health or honorable for virtue? For to wish to compare plants to animals seems exceedingly absurd and forced. This is because animals possess sensation <term>sentiant; the ability to perceive and feel through the senses</term>; they feel pain, they fear, and they are harmed, and for that reason, they can be victims of injustice. Plants, however, possess no sensation, and therefore no estrangement, nor evil, nor any wound or injustice can happen to them. For sensation is the starting point of all kinship <term>Conciliationis; likely translating the Stoic concept of "oikeiosis," the natural affinity or sense of belonging one feels toward oneself and those of a similar nature</term> and alienation. Indeed, Zeno <note>Zeno of Citium (c. 334–262 BCE), the founder of Stoic philosophy.</note> established kinship as the foundation of justice. But how, then, is it not absurd, since many men live by sensation alone, while their mind...

# PLUTARCH, BOOK III <note>Although the header names Plutarch, this text is part of Porphyry’s "On Abstinence," which cites Plutarch’s arguments extensively.</note>

...and we see that they have almost no reason. Likewise, there are many people who, in their cruelty, anger, and insolence, surpass even the most horrific wild beasts—parricides <note>Those who kill their own parents or close relatives.</note>, criminals, and tyrants. How can we assert that we have rights in relation to these men, yet say we have no rights toward the ox that plows, or the domestic dog, or the flocks that nourish us with milk and clothe us with their wool?

But that idea of Chrysippus <note>Chrysippus (c. 279–206 BCE) was a leading Stoic philosopher known for his rigid distinction between rational humans and "irrational" animals.</note> is certainly deemed "plausible" by such people: that we were made for our own sake and for the sake of the gods, while all other animals were made by the gods for our sake. They claim that horses were created to wage war with us, and dogs to hunt with us; that leopards, bears, and lions were created to provide us with manly exercise. 

As for the pig—and here, of course, the most "delightful" grace resides—they say it was created for no other purpose than its meat, so that it might provide us with a side dish <term>opsonium; originally any food eaten with bread, but specifically used here to mean meat or delicacies</term>. They suggest that God mixed a soul into the pig merely to serve as salt <note>original: "animam loco salis miscuisse." A famous Stoic paradox: the pig's soul is not a spiritual essence but a biological preservative to keep the meat fresh until it is slaughtered.</note>, to prevent the meat from rotting. Indeed, so that we might have an abundance of broths and delicacies, He produced various kinds of oysters, purple-fish, sea-nettles, and diverse species of birds. He did not prepare these from elsewhere, but as if turning a great part of Himself toward these sweetnesses, and surpassing even the best nurses with the allurements of seasonings, He filled the entire world with pleasures and delights. 

Now, does any of this seem to have even a hint of probability, or to befit God in any way? But let anyone consider what answer they might give to that reasoning of Carneades <note>Carneades (c. 214–129 BCE) was a Skeptic philosopher who often challenged Stoic "common sense" and their views on natural justice.</note>. "Each thing," he says, "which has been produced by nature,"

<summary>This page critiques the Stoic belief that animals were created solely for human utility, specifically attacking Chrysippus's claim that animals like pigs only possess souls to keep their meat fresh.</summary>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Stoic teleology—the belief that everything in nature was designed specifically for human benefit. The previous page ended with the Stoic claim that a pig's soul is merely "salt" intended to keep its meat from rotting until humans are ready to eat it. This page refutes that logic by pointing out the existence of "useless" or harmful animals.</meta>

# ON ABSTINENCE

...as it were, to keep the meat fresh. For a thing is seasoned when it attains the end for which nature created it; then it attains its utility. Utility, however, in its more common meaning, should be understood as the "good use" <note>The author is likely referring to the Stoic technical term *chreia*, meaning the beneficial function of a thing.</note> as those men call it. Now, the pig was created for this: to be slaughtered and eaten. Therefore, when the pig is eaten, it attains its own end and is fulfilled in its utility. 

But if God produced animals for the use of humans, of what use to us are flies, gnats, bats, beetles, scorpions, and vipers? Some of these are foul to look upon, filthy to touch, intolerable in their smell, and unpleasant or horrifying in their cry; others are entirely deadly to those who encounter them. 

What then? In the case of whales, and sharks <note>Latin: *pistricibus*—large, predatory sea-beasts or sawfish.</note>, and the massive sea-monsters which "the breezy Amphitrite <note>In Greek mythology, the goddess of the sea and wife of Poseidon. The quote is a reference to Homer’s *Odyssey*.</note> nourishes in countless numbers," as Homer says: has the supreme Creator taught us in what way they were useful to nature? 

But if they should say that not *all* things were created for us and for our use—aside from the fact that such a distinction is quite confused and obscure—we will not even be able to avoid acting unjustly if we inflict violence upon them and use them harmfully. For these were not created for our sake, but were created according to nature just as we were. 

I set aside, moreover, that if we wish to measure things by their utility—considering only those things that relate to us and exist for our sake—there will be no reason why we should not also have to concede that **we** were made for the sake of the most dangerous animals, such as crocodiles, whales, and serpents <note>Latin: *dracones*—referring here to large predatory reptiles or sea monsters.</note>.

<summary>The author refutes the Stoic idea that all animals were made for human use by highlighting "useless" or dangerous creatures like vipers and sharks. He argues that if we aren't the purpose of their existence, then killing them is an act of injustice.</summary>

# BOOK III.

...to which we are entitled <note>original: *debeamus*. This completes the thought from the previous page regarding what we owe to animals and what we expect from them.</note>. For no utility at all comes to us from them. Indeed, they seize and tear apart any humans they happen to encounter, and they are accustomed to using them for food. And they do not behave any more cruelly in this than we do, except that it is sheer necessity and hunger that lead them to this injustice; whereas we, out of arrogance and luxury, often kill most animals for sport in theaters and during hunts. 

From this very habit, those who first dared to do such things strengthened in us a readiness for slaughter, ferocity, and ruthlessness; in the same way, they stripped away a great deal of our natural gentleness. The <term>Pythagoreans</term> <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who were famous in antiquity for their vegetarianism and belief that animals possess souls.</note>, however, believed that practicing gentleness toward beasts would serve as an exercise for us in humanity and mercy. 

Therefore, how do these men not incite us toward justice more than those who claim that customary justice is corrupted by these practices? For habit has a wonderful power to drive a person further into dispositions that take root and grow little by little. 

"To be sure," they say: "but just as the immortal is the opposite of the mortal, the incorruptible of the corruptible, and the incorporeal of the corporeal, so too must there be an **irrational** nature to oppose the **rational** nature seen to exist in the world." They assert that this pairing of opposites <note>The Stoic argument of *isostheneia* or balance: that for every quality in nature, an opposite must exist to maintain the cosmic order.</note> must not be left incomplete. 

As if we were denying this! Or as if we were not showing that many irrational things are found in the world. For a great and abundant irrational force offers itself in all things...

# ON ABSTINENCE

...nature to oppose it <note>original: *opponatur*. This completes the sentence from the previous page: "...so too must there be an **irrational** nature to oppose [the rational]."</note>. Nature offers things which lack a soul <note>original: *anima*. In this context, it refers to the "animating principle" or life force that distinguishes living things from rocks or water.</note>, so that nothing else should be sought from elsewhere to serve as the opposite to the rational. Indeed, since every inanimate thing is immediately devoid of reason and mind, it stands as the proper opposite to that which is endowed with reason and mind. 

Even if someone were to judge that this opposition exists not in nature generally, but specifically within "animate nature"—claiming that one living thing possesses **imagination** <note>original: *imaginatione*. Here, it refers to the capacity to form mental images or representations of the world.</note> while another lacks it, or that one is sensible <note>Capable of feeling or perceiving through the senses.</note> while another is insensitive—they would be suggesting that nature maintains a balance of these "habits and privations" within the same category of living things. This, however, is truly absurd.

For it would be ridiculous if someone claimed that among living creatures, one was capable of sensation and another was not, or that one used imagination and another did not. By its very nature, every living, animate being is immediately capable of both sensation and imagination. 

Therefore, no one can rightly demand that among living beings, one must be defined as **rational** and another as **irrational**. This is especially true when disputing against those who believe that nothing can participate in sensation without also participating in intelligence—and that no animal exists which does not also possess some degree of opinion and **reasoning** <note>original: *ratiocinatio*.</note>. 

This is because every sense and every natural impulse exists according to the laws of nature. Indeed, philosophers rightly say that Nature does all things for a purpose and toward a specific end. Nature did not create animals endowed with sense merely so they could passively experience or feel things in a simple way; rather, because many things are suitable for them and...

<vocab>soul, rational, inanimate, imagination, sensible, insensitive, animate, senses, reasoning, nature</vocab>

<summary>Porphyry argues that all living (animate) beings naturally possess sensation and imagination, making it illogical to classify some animals as "irrational" when their true opposite is the inanimate world. He suggests that sensation itself implies a level of intelligence and purpose granted by Nature.</summary>

### BOOK III.

->S ii<-

...[that some things] are beneficial while many others are foreign and harmful, such that a creature could not exist for even a short time unless it had learned to avoid the latter and secure the former for itself. Nature has bestowed the knowledge of both to every creature in a similar way through the gift of sensation. 

However, the behaviors that follow from sensation—namely, the pursuit and grasping of what is useful, as well as the rejection and flight from what is destructive or painful—could not possibly exist except in those beings naturally constituted to **reason**, **judge**, **remember**, and **pay attention**. 

For if you were to completely strip away expectation, memory, attention, preparation, hope, fear, desire, and indignation, you would leave the creature with no use for its eyes, ears, or any other senses it possesses. Indeed, it would be better to lack all sensation and **imagination** <note>original: *imaginatione*. The capacity to form internal mental images or representations of the world.</note> if there were no faculty to make use of them, rather than to toil and suffer pain while having no ability to drive away these troubles. 

Furthermore, there is the reasoning of **Strato the physicist** <note>Strato of Lampsacus (c. 335 – c. 269 BCE), a philosopher who headed the Lyceum after Theophrastus. He was known for his "physicalist" approach to the soul, arguing that even basic perception is a mental, not just physical, act.</note>, in which he demonstrates that it is impossible for anyone to perceive anything at all without **intelligence** <note>original: *intelligentia*. Here, it refers to the mind's active processing of sensory data.</note>. For it is common for letters appearing before our eyes or speeches falling upon our ears to go unnoticed and escape us when our mind is focused on something else. Then, once the mind returns and refocuses, it follows up on each of the things previously mentioned by recollecting them. 

From this comes the famous saying: *"The mind sees, the mind hears; everything else is deaf and blind"* <note>A fragment from the poet-philosopher Epicharmus. It suggests that the physical organs (eyes and ears) are merely instruments, and the mind is the true perceiver.</note>. This is because the physical impression that occurs in the eyes and ears [is useless] unless the mind is present...

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals must possess intelligence and reasoning because sensation is useless without the mental capacity to process it, judge it, and act upon it. He cites the philosopher Strato to prove that the mind, not the physical organ, is the true source of perception.</summary>

<header>### ON ABSTINENCE</header>

...[the mind] understands, it [the sense] does not produce sensation. For this reason, it is also said of **King Cleomenes** <note>Cleomenes III (c. 235–219 BCE), a reformist King of Sparta. This anecdote illustrates that hearing is a mental act; if the mind is preoccupied with war in the Peloponnese, it cannot "hear" music.</note> that when a singer was being praised at a banquet and he was asked whether he also liked the man, he ordered others to judge it, saying that his mind was away in the **Peloponnese**. It is necessary, therefore, that in all things where sensation exists, intelligence is also present. 

But even if we were to grant that sensation does not require the mind for its own specific function, nonetheless, when sensation operates to distinguish between what is beneficial and what is foreign, it already possesses the faculty of **memory**. This faculty fears those things that bring pain and desires useful things when they are absent, ensuring they are provided for. It prepares the methods for pursuit and hunting, as well as the means for fleeing and avoiding those things that have attacked. 

Furthermore, they <note>The author refers here to Stoic philosophers, who often used complex technical definitions to distinguish human "reason" from animal "instinct."</note> themselves, in their introductory handbooks, drill these points home everywhere to the point of boredom when they define **purpose** as the "marking of perfection," or **impulse** <note>original: *aggressionem*. In Stoic terms, this refers to the mental "assault" or drive toward an object.</note> as "incitation before incitation," or **preparation** as "action before action," or **memory** as the "grasping of a past thing which the sense had previously perceived as present." 

There is nothing among these definitions that is not **rational**, and all of them are present in all animals. This is clearly what they do regarding understandings: when these are stored they call them **notions** <note>original: *εννοίας* (ennoias). These are stored concepts or "inner ideas."</note>, and while they are being moved and agitated, they call them **thoughts** <note>original: *διανοήσεις* (dianoēseis). Discursive or active reasoning.</note>. Since they commonly admit that all **emotions** <note>original: *affectus*. Specifically the "passions" or disturbances of the soul.</note> are actually "depraved natures and opinions," it is a wonder...

# BOOK III.
->S iii<-
<meta>catchword: becomes:</meta>

it is strange how they do not notice in beasts many operations and motions of anger, fear, envy, and jealousy. Indeed, they themselves punish dogs and horses that misbehave—not in vain, surely, but to make them more moderate, inflicting a distress from pain upon them which we call <term>repentance <note>original: μετάνοιαν (*metanoia*); literally a "change of mind." The author argues that punishment works because animals can feel regret or change their intent.</note></term>. Likewise, since the name for pleasure presented to the ears is "soothing" and to the eyes "enchantment," people are accustomed to use both with beasts. For deer and horses are soothed by the sounds of pipes and flutes. Likewise, they call crabs out of their hiding places by playing melodic pipes. They even assert that the <term>shad <note>original: *Thrissam*; a type of migratory fish known in antiquity for being sensitive to sound.</note></term> fish comes out of its holes because of song.

Yet they <note>Referring to the Stoic philosophers, who argued that animals lacked true reason (*logos*) and therefore only mimicked human emotions.</note> affirm quite foolishly that beasts neither rejoice, nor are angry, nor fear, nor prepare, nor remember. Instead, they say the bee "as-if" remembers, the swallow "as-if" prepares, the lion is "as-if" angry, and the deer is "as-if" afraid. I do not know what they would answer to those who might say that animals do not see or hear, but "as-if" see and "as-if" hear; that they do not emit a voice, but "as-if" emit a voice; and that they do not live at all, but "as-if" live. For a fair listener would easily notice that these latter things are said with no more evidence than the former, and against all obvious reality. 

But if, comparing the life of other animals to human customs, the actions of life, and diet, I were to see much depravity in them, and no manifest inclina— <note>The text cuts off here mid-word; the Latin *propensionem* (inclination) is completed on the following page.</note>

# BOOK III.
## ON ABSTINENCE
->S iii<-

...perfection, no progress, and no appetite. I would indeed wonder why nature had given them the beginning of these traits if they could never reach their fulfillment. Yet, to those same thinkers <note>Referring to the Stoic philosophers, who were Porphyry’s primary intellectual opponents regarding animal rights.</note>, this does not seem absurd at all. For while they claim that love for one's offspring is the very origin of human society and justice, and while they observe that this same love is quite powerful in other animals, they still maintain that we share no bond of justice or society with them.

Furthermore, although mules lack no reproductive organs—for they possess both penis and womb and use them with pleasure—they never reach the natural goal of reproduction <note>Mules are the sterile offspring of a horse and a donkey. Porphyry uses this biological dead-end as an analogy: just because a faculty (like reason) doesn't reach its "perfect" human form in an animal doesn't mean the faculty is entirely absent.</note>. 

In addition to this, does it not seem laughable when they argue that men like *Socrates*, *Plato*, and *Zeno* behaved no better than any common slave, calling them equally unwise, intemperate, and unjust <note>The Stoics famously held that any man who was not a perfectly wise "Sage" was equally a fool and a sinner, regardless of their reputation.</note>? Yet, when it comes to beasts—simply because they do not act with perfect and refined virtue—these same thinkers claim the animals lack reason entirely. They attribute this to a total "deprivation" of reason rather than a mere "depravity" or "weakness" of it. This is especially strange since they admit that <term>vices <note>original: *vitia*; in ancient philosophy, a vice is not just a bad habit but a failure or "sickness" of the rational mind.</note></term> are rational in nature—and it is obvious that other animals are full of such flaws. Indeed, we clearly see cowardice, lack of restraint, injustice, spite, and other such traits in many of them.

To judge that a creature is incapable of reason simply because nature has not made it perfectly suited for <term>right reason <note>original: *rectitudinem rationis*; the Stoic ideal of logic used perfectly to achieve virtue.</note></term> is like claiming that a monkey is incapable of ugliness or a tortoise is incapable of slow-

...perfection <note>The OCR begins with the suffix "-cem," completing the word "perfectionem" (perfection) from the previous page.</note> by nature, because they can admit neither beauty nor speed in their absolute forms. Furthermore, this is the argument of men who do not notice the difference between things. 

For reason <note>original: *Ratio*</note> is innate; but right reason and perfected reason are acquired through study and discipline. Therefore, all living beings are participants in reason. However, as for its rectitude and wisdom, they cannot even assert that any man is fully endowed with them, even if there were countless men to choose from <note>Porphyry is pointing out the Stoic paradox: they deny animals reason because animals aren't "perfectly" wise, yet they admit that almost no human is perfectly wise either.</note>. 

Just as there is a difference between sight and sight, or flight and flight—for hawks do not see in the same way as cicadas <note>original: *cicadæ*</note>, nor do eagles and partridges <note>original: *perdices*</note> fly in the same manner—so too, not every rational faculty has the same ability and readiness to reach the highest point of perfection. 

Indeed, many signs of social organization, courage, and also cleverness <term>calliditas</term> <note>often used for "shrewdness" or the ability to navigate complex situations</note> in acquiring and managing resources—as well as their opposites: injustice, cowardice, and stupidity—are observed in animals to such a degree that debates have arisen among some thinkers as to whether land animals or sea animals are superior in virtue <note>original: *uirtus*; in this context, it refers to natural excellence or character rather than human morality.</note>. 

This can be seen clearly if land horses are compared with river horses <note>original: *equi fluuiatilibus*; likely referring to hippopotamuses, which were often called "river horses" in antiquity.</note>: for the latter care for their fathers, while the former kill them so that they may mate with their mothers. Partridges also act quite differently than doves. Male partridges hide and even crush the eggs because the female, while she is brooding, will not admit the male for mating. Male doves, however, are accustomed to taking turns in the duty of brooding; and they are the first to [bring] food to the chicks...

# BOOK III.
## ON ABSTINENCE

...bring them back. Indeed, if a female wanders away too long, they <note>Referring to male birds, likely doves or pigeons, mentioned in the preceding text.</note> strike her with their beaks to direct her toward caring for the eggs and chicks. Antipater <note>Antipater of Tarsus (2nd century BCE), a Stoic philosopher who argued that animals were created solely for the use of humans and lacked rational souls.</note>, however, while he condemned the lack of cleanliness in donkeys and sheep, failed to notice the habits of lynxes and swallows. The former cast their filth far away from themselves, hiding and burying it entirely; swallows, moreover, teach their young to turn outward when casting out their droppings.

Indeed, we do not say one tree is more "stupid" or "unteachable" than another tree, just as we do not compare a sheep to a dog in that way, or say a vegetable smells more "lazily" than another, as we might compare a deer to a lion. This is because just as among things that lack all motion, one cannot be "slower" than another, nor can one thing have a "fainter voice" than another among things that have no voice at all; so too, nothing can be called more cowardly, or more sluggish, or more lacking in self-control <term>incontinentius</term> <note>original: *incontinentius*; the state of being unable to restrain one's impulses or desires.</note> unless the faculty of understanding is present in all of them to some degree. Because this faculty is present in different beings in different ways—to a greater or lesser degree—it produces those differences we observe among animals. 

In this regard, it should not surprise us that man excels other animals by such a vast distance in teachability <term>docilitas</term>, cleverness <term>solertia</term>, and other matters regarding justice and social life. For many of those animals also far surpass all men in physical size and speed, as well as in sharpness of vision and keenness of hearing. Nor...

<summary>Porphyry argues that because we apply comparative traits like "cleverness" or "cleanliness" to animals, they must possess the underlying faculty of reason. He notes that while humans excel in social intelligence, animals often surpass humans in physical senses and strength.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument in Book III of *On Abstinence from Animal Food*. He is refuting the Stoic claim that animals are "irrational" by explaining that differences in the degree or quality of a faculty (like reason or sight) do not imply the total absence of that faculty. He concludes by arguing that for a creature to suffer from a defect of a faculty (like madness or dullness), it must naturally possess that faculty in the first place.</meta>

## ON ABSTINENCE

...nevertheless, a man is considered deaf, or blind, or infirm on that account <note>Continuing the thought from the previous page: just as a human is not "non-human" for having a sensory defect, an animal is not "non-rational" for having a different degree of reason.</note>. But we too run, although more slowly than deer; nor do we fail to see, even if not as sharply as hawks; nor are we entirely deprived of strength and size by nature, although if we are compared to the elephant and the camel <note>The elephant and camel were frequently used in classical literature as standard examples of massive, powerful beasts.</note>, we seem to be nothing in these regards. In a similar way, therefore, it must not be said that beasts—just because they understand more dully and think worse than we do—think or understand nothing at all, or are endowed with no reason <term>ratio</term> <note>original: *ratio*; representing the Greek *logos*, meaning both the internal faculty of thought and the external expression of speech.</note>. Rather, they themselves also possess it, though weak and clouded, just as one might have a dull and troubled eye. 

There are countless examples that I could bring forward in proof of the intelligence of animals, were it not that many things have already been collected and handed down to memory by many others regarding this opinion <note>Porphyry likely refers to authors like Plutarch, who wrote *On the Cleverness of Animals*, or Aristotle’s *History of Animals*.</note>. That, furthermore, is worthy of consideration: it seems to belong to the same part or faculty which receives something according to nature, that it can also fall into that which is contrary to nature, whether impaired or diseased. Just as the eyes may fall into blindness, the leg into lameness <term>claudicationem</term>, or the tongue into stuttering. For nothing else can suffer this; blindness does not belong to that which is unable to see anyway, nor does lameness belong to that which is not naturally fit for walking, nor is anything stuttering, or lisping, or mute, which does not also possess a tongue. In a similar way, therefore, neither...

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument in Book III of On Abstinence from Animal Food. He is building on the previous argument that animals possess the faculty of reason (ratio) by demonstrating that they can suffer from "madness." His philosophical premise is that one cannot suffer from the corruption of a faculty (like insanity) unless one naturally possesses that faculty (sanity/reason) to begin with.</meta>

-># ON ABSTINENCE<-

...you would not call anyone delirious, nor out of their mind, nor insane, if it were not also natural for them to understand, and to think, and to reason according to their nature. For it is not possible for anyone to be placed in a certain state <note>original: *affectione*; here referring to a psychological or physical condition.</note> unless they are endowed with a faculty of which that state is either a privation, a corruption, or some other defect. 

But have you ever encountered mad <note>original: *rabientes*; refers to the state of "rabies," or raging madness.</note> dogs? Indeed, have you seen it in horses? Some say that even oxen and foxes can go mad. But it is enough that it is well-established regarding dogs, for it is beyond controversy: the animal testifies that it possesses a reason and a mind that are not ignoble. Truly, that which is called "madness" and "insanity" is a disturbance of that very mind; especially since we see that neither their sight nor their hearing is changed in that state. 

Therefore, just as a person would be absurd to claim that the faculty of understanding, reasoning, and remembering is not disturbed and corrupted in a man who is raging and delirious—since it is common usage for us to say that delirious people are "not with themselves" <note>original: *non esse apud se*; a Latin idiom meaning to be out of one's mind or to have lost self-control.</note>, but are disturbed in mind and judgment—so too it is with those who think rabid dogs suffer from something else. Those who do not believe that the dogs are disturbed and removed from that part of the soul which is naturally suited for understanding, reasoning, and remembering—causing them to fail to recognize their dearest companions and to flee their usual haunts—seem either to ignore what is clearly apparent, or if they do notice it, they choose to fight against the truth. And indeed those things...

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the mental faculties of animals. Porphyry is now moving from the specific argument about animal "madness" (as proof of their underlying reason) to a broader argument by Plutarch and Theophrastus concerning the biological and psychological kinship between humans and animals.</meta>

-># BOOK III<-

<meta>Signature: T ii</meta>
<meta>catchword: cor-</meta>

...which Plutarch wrote in various books against the Stoics and Peripatetics <note>The Stoics and Peripatetics (followers of Aristotle) generally argued that animals lacked reason and therefore humans had no moral obligations toward them.</note>, are of this sort. 

Theophrastus <term>Theophrastus: Aristotle's successor as head of the Peripatetic school; he argued more strongly than his teacher for the shared traits between humans and animals.</term>, however, also employs this reasoning. He says that we call those who are born from the same parents—namely, a father and a mother—naturally related to one another. By the same token, we shall also call those kin who are descended from the same ancestors; and likewise, the citizens of the same city, because they share the same land and a mutual social bond. We do not consider these people to have a kinship simply because they were born from the same [immediate parents], unless we mean that some of the first ancestors were the original founders of their race, or that they themselves descended from those same ancestors. 

In this way, certainly, we assert that Greek is kin to Greek, and "barbarian" <note>original: *barbarum*; in this context, the term refers to any non-Greek person.</note> to "barbarian," and likewise all humans are related to one another for one of two reasons: either because they come from the same ancestors, or because they share a commonality in food, customs, and the overall way of life. Thus we establish that all human beings are kin. 

But in all animals, the basic principles of their bodies are naturally the same. I do not think this should be understood as referring to the primary elements—for plants, too, are composed of those—but rather to their seed, their flesh, and the types of humors <term>humors: original *humorum*; the vital fluids like blood and bile which ancient medicine believed composed the animal body.</term> that are inherent in animals. This is even more true because the souls <note>original: *animæ*; the animating life-force.</note> within them do not differ from one another; they share the same desires, angers, deliberations, and above all, the same senses. Just as...

<summary>This page outlines Theophrastus's argument for the universal kinship of all humans based on shared ancestry and social bonds, and extends this kinship to all animals based on shared physical humors and psychological traits like emotion and sensation.</summary>
<keywords>Theophrastus, Plutarch, kinship, biology of animals, animal souls, humors, Stoics, Peripatetics</keywords>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE</header>
<sig>T</sig>

...bodies, so also some animals possess souls that are more refined and perfect, while others are less so. Nevertheless, the basic principles of nature are the same for all, as is shown by the similarity and affinity of their emotions. 

If it is true, as they say, that the development of character is of this sort, and that all kinds of animals possess wisdom and understanding—though they differ in their upbringing and the various mixtures of their primary elements <term>temperatures: in ancient philosophy, this refers to the specific "blend" of qualities (hot, cold, wet, dry) that determined a creature's temperament and physical health.</term>—then we must consider the genus of other animals to be entirely akin and related to us. This is especially true since the nourishment for all of them is the same, and they possess breath <note>original: *spiritum*</note> (as Euripides says) as well as a red flow <note>original: *fluorem rubrum*; a poetic way of referring to blood, emphasizing its shared nature across species.</note>. 

All animals indicate that the sky and the earth are, in a sense, common parents to everyone. Therefore, since they are related in this way, if they also seem to have been allotted the same kind of soul (as Pythagoras <note>Pythagoras was a Greek philosopher who famously taught that souls are immortal and migrate between human and animal bodies after death.</note> believed), then any person who does not abstain from injuring his kin might rightly be judged impious. 

Nor truly should the law of kinship be considered severed just because some animals are wild. For some men are found to be no less—and indeed even more—injurious toward other men than wild beasts are. These are men driven by a certain spirit of their own nature and depravity to inflict harm on those they encounter. Although we may believe that such people ought to be removed and put to death, we do not for that reason...

-># BOOK III.<-

->T iii<-

<meta>catchword: to himself</meta>

...society toward those that are gentle to us. Thus, if there are some among the animals that are monstrous and wild, they ought to be killed because they are of that nature, just as humans of that character are killed. Yet, we must not abandon our duty of justice <note>original: *iure*; here referring to the moral law or right conduct that governs relationships between beings.</note> toward the rest of the gentler animals. Truly, neither kind should be eaten, since no law permits even unjust humans to be used as food. 

But we behave with far greater injustice when we kill the gentle species just as we do the savage and the harmful, and then consume them as food. We sin in two ways: first, by killing those that are gentle, and second, by feasting upon them after they are slain. Their slaughter serves no purpose other than to satisfy our own gluttony <note>original: *ingluuiem*; a term for excessive, voracious eating.</note>.

Furthermore, we must add this point: anyone who claims that extending justice to other animals actually loses or corrupts justice itself is unaware that he is not preserving justice, but rather indulging in pleasure—which is the natural enemy and foe of justice. It is clear that when pleasure is the ultimate goal, justice is destroyed. 

Who can doubt that justice is strengthened through abstinence? For someone who abstains from harming any living creature, even if that creature offers no benefit to his society, will be much more careful to avoid harming those of his own kind. If he loves the entire genus <term>genus: the broad biological category that includes many different species; Porphyry argues that respecting the "animal" category leads to respecting the "human" species.</term> of living things, he will not be able to look upon any particular species with hatred. On the contrary, the wider the scope of animal life to which he extends his care, the more faithfully he will preserve justice toward every part of it. For he who recognizes kinship toward an animal...

<summary>Porphyry argues that while dangerous animals may be killed for safety, they should never be eaten, and that true justice is expanded—not corrupted—by showing mercy to all living creatures.</summary>

<keywords>justice, abstinence, gluttony, kinship, moral law, pleasure vs. virtue, gentle animals, Porphyry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument from Book III of "On Abstinence from Animal Food." The previous page concluded by noting that a person who practices justice toward themselves will naturally extend that care to others. This section contrasts the ethical philosophies of Socrates and Pythagoras regarding the "seasoning" of life and food, arguing that true justice is universal and not limited to human-to-human interactions.</meta>

-># ON ABSTINENCE<-

...to himself, he will also not affect any animal with injury. But he who restricts the whole of right <note>original: *ius*; meaning legal or moral right.</note> only to man—being enclosed, as it were, within very narrow boundaries—can easily throw away his caution against committing injustice. This is why the Pythagorean seasoning <note>original: *opsonium*; a Greek-derived term for a side-dish, relish, or anything eaten with bread to add flavor.</note> is far sweeter than the Socratic one. For Socrates used to say that hunger was the seasoning for food. Pythagoras, however, believed that the sweetest condiment of justice for one’s seasoning was the knowledge that no injury was being inflicted on anyone. For while we avoid taking food from living beings <note>original: *animatis*; literally "those with souls," referring to animals.</note>, we undoubtedly avoid the injustices that occur concerning food. Nor should it be thought that God brought it about that we are in no way able to protect our own health without the insult and injury of others. For in that way, He would have granted us a nature that served as the very origin of injustice. 

Furthermore, those who believe that justice should be established solely on the basis of kinship toward other humans seem to me to be ignorant of the true nature of justice. If that were the case, what they describe would merely be called "humanity" or a certain "kindness" toward humans. But justice is not like that; rather, it consists in the abstinence from harming anything that does not harm us. And this—not that other way—is how the concept of the "just" is understood: so that since justice is established by the principle that injury should be inflicted on no one at all, it follows that it must be extended even to the rest of the animals. Hence, its essence consists in the dominion of reason over the irrational part of the soul, and the obedience of that irrational part...

### BOOK III.
<meta>catchword: while</meta>

...it is said to be; for while this [reason] commands and that [the soul] obeys, it is necessary for a man to be innocent toward all things. For when disturbances and desires have been pacified, and anger calmed, and reason <term>ratio; the faculty of rational thought and divine order</term> obtains its command, immediately a likeness <note>original: *similitudo*; a resemblance or reflection of the divine</note> to that more excellent nature shines forth in us. Moreover, the most excellent nature in the universe <note>Referring to the Divine or the Source of all existence</note> is most innocent toward all things. And indeed, by its power, it is the preserver of all things, and beneficent to all, and in need of nothing. 

But we, while innocent toward all things on account of justice, are in need of necessities on account of our mortality. Now, the taking of necessities brings no injury—not to plants, when we take from them those things which they discard; nor to fruits, when we use them once they are dead; nor to sheep, when we benefit them more through shearing, and by providing them care, we in turn become partakers of their milk. Therefore, a just man seems as if he were a diminisher of himself in those things which pertain to the body; yet he does not affect himself with injury. For by the chastening <term>castigatio; disciplined restraint of the physical self</term> of the body and by self-control <term>continentia; the ability to restrain one's appetites</term>, he increases his internal good, which is a likeness to God. 

Therefore, neither if pleasure <term>uoluptas; sensual delight or hedonism</term> be the ultimate goal is that which is truly justice preserved; nor if the "first principles of nature" <note>original: *prima secundum naturam*; a technical term referring to basic survival instincts like health and strength</note> fulfill happiness, or all those things which have been set forth. For in many cases, the impulses and needs of an irrational nature <term>irrationalis natura; the animalistic or unreasoning part of nature</term> are the cause of injustice. Indeed, when they have needed the eating of animals, so that their nature, just as...

<summary>Porphyry argues that true justice arises when reason governs the soul, creating a likeness to the divine "innocence" that preserves all things without need. He explains that humans can satisfy their mortal needs without injustice by taking only what plants and animals can spare (like fruit or wool) rather than taking their lives.</summary>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE</header>
<sig>m iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: ribus</meta>

...while, they say, they might preserve [the soul] without any trouble, and without any lack of those things which it desires. But if the goal is to become like God as far as is possible, innocence <note>original: *innocentia*; in this context, it refers to "harmlessness" or the principle of non-injury toward all living beings</note> toward all things must be maintained. For in what way does he who is led by his passions practice innocence toward only his children and his wife, while he despises and harms all others? This is because the irrational <term>irrationalis; the part of the soul driven by instincts and emotions rather than logic</term> part dominates within him; being stirred up toward mortal things, he admires and is captivated by them. 

But he who is ruled by reason <term>ratio; the faculty of rational thought and divine order</term> behaves innocently toward citizens as well as strangers, and indeed toward all human beings. Because he has the irrational faculty subjected to him, he is for that reason more rational and more divine than those others. Thus, he who does not limit the boundaries of innocence to humans alone, but extends them even to the other animals, is more similar to God. And if he were able to extend those boundaries even to plants, he would preserve the divine image and likeness even more fully.

Because he cannot do so, the defect of our nature is clearly seen. From this comes the misery lamented by the ancients: that we were born amidst such adverse and discordant strifes <note>A reference to the "Strife" (Neikos) described by the philosopher Empedocles, which causes the fall of souls into the physical world</note> that we are unable to preserve divinity, incorruptibility, and innocence in all things. For we have not existed in all things without any need <term>indigentia; a state of physical lack or dependency</term>, which is the very reason why our physical birth occurred; and because our original abundance has now flowed away, we have been placed in a state of want <term>inopia; the poverty or scarcity inherent in being a mortal creature</term>. This want, in turn, seeks to acquire from others the safety and beauty from which we received our very being. He who therefore [takes] more...

<summary>Porphyry argues that true godliness requires universal harmlessness (innocence) toward all living beings, including animals. He explains that human cruelty and the need to consume others arise from the "poverty" and "strife" inherent in our fallen, mortal nature.</summary>

<header>BOOK III.</header>
<sig>m T</sig>
<sig>V</sig>
<meta>catchword: ta</meta>

...needs external things, the more he is bound to poverty <note>original: *inopia*; in this philosophical context, it refers to a state of "lack" or "neediness" rather than just a lack of money</note>. The more things anyone needs, the further he is from God, and the more he is enslaved to poverty. For he who is similar to God possesses true riches as a continuous result of that likeness. No one who is truly rich and in need of nothing inflicts injury on anyone. For as long as he causes harm—even if he overflows with money or possesses every acre of the earth—because he is enslaved to lack, he is undoubtedly poor. And for that reason, he is also unjust, impious, alienated from God, and finally subject to every vice. Indeed, the descent of the soul into <term>matter; *materia*, the physical world, which Neoplatonists viewed as the source of limitation and evil for the soul</term> brought about the nature of this vice through the deprivation of the Good. Therefore, all things are mere trifles as long as one wanders from the first principle. Likewise, everyone is in need of everything as long as they do not look toward satiety <note>original: *satietas*; referring here to spiritual fullness or completion</note>, and they indulge the mortality of their nature as long as they do not truly know themselves. Injustice is also very adept at persuading itself and judging those who are held under its power, because it dwells with pleasure among its own pupils and companions. Just as in the choice of lifestyles, he who is experienced in both is accustomed to judge more correctly than he who has experienced only one; so too in the choices and avoidances of duty, the judgment of that person who discerns lower things from a higher vantage point is more certain than the judgment of one who, from lower things, [views] those which are preferred...

<summary>Porphyry argues that true wealth is spiritual self-sufficiency and that those who harm others for material gain are actually "poor" and alienated from God. He explains that vice arises when the soul descends into matter and forgets its true, divine nature.</summary>
<keywords>poverty of spirit, likeness to God, soul's descent, matter, injustice, spiritual satiety, self-knowledge</keywords>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE</header>
<sig>V</sig>
<meta>catchword: Earth</meta>

...are established. Therefore, he who organizes his life based on the mind and intellect can more correctly and precisely determine those things which should be sought or avoided than one who is accustomed to living according to his irrational faculty. For the former has also passed through the irrational life, since he was bound to it from the very beginning; but the latter, being ignorant of those things which proceed from the mind—like a boy playing among boys of his own kind—easily persuades his peers of everything. 

But if everyone, they say, were to agree with these arguments, what would happen to us then? This, undoubtedly: we would be happy. For then injustice would be banished far from humanity, and justice would dwell among us just as it does in heaven. 

But as it is, the situation is just as if the <term>Danaïdes; in Greek mythology, forty-nine sisters punished in the underworld for murdering their husbands by being forced to eternally fill a perforated vessel with water</term> were to ask what kind of life they would lead if they were freed from the task of drawing water in those leaky jars. For they ask and doubt what would happen to us if we were content with a life established on and for the sake of necessities, and if we stopped indulging in disturbances and desires—the fruits of which all flow away and perish because of a lack of honorable things <note>original: *inopia honestorum*; refers to a spiritual or moral poverty that leads people to seek fulfillment in temporary, physical desires</note>. 

What then shall we do, you ask? We shall imitate that <term>Golden Age; a mythical primordial era of peace and harmony when humans lived in simple virtue and did not kill animals</term>; we shall imitate those who have been claimed for freedom. For among those people dwelt Shame, <term>Nemesis; the personification of divine indignation against wrongdoing</term>, and Justice, because they were content with the fruits of the earth. For the earth bore fruit for them of its own accord...

<summary>Porphyry argues that an intellectual life leads to better moral choices and that if humanity returned to the simple, bloodless diet of the Golden Age, justice and happiness would return to the world.</summary>

### BOOK III

The earth is abundantly fertile. But those who have been granted freedom <note>original: *libertate donati*</note> now acquire for themselves those things which they formerly produced while serving their masters. In the same way, if you were freed from the slavery of the body and from that service you pay to the passions <note>original: *affectibus*; in Neoplatonic philosophy, these are the irrational impulses and emotions that bind the soul to the material world</note>—just as you used to nourish the body with various external things—you will instead nourish yourself with internal, spiritual things. In this state, your own inherent goods will justly suffice for you, so that you no longer need to seize the belongings of others by force <note>Porphyry suggests that by desiring less, we stop the "violence" of killing animals for food</note>.

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Book IV, moving from the philosophical arguments of the previous books to historical evidence. Porphyry begins to document specific nations and eras that practiced vegetarianism, starting with the Greek "Golden Age" as described by the historian Dicaearchus.</meta>

### BOOK IV.

We shall now attempt to provide solutions regarding the question of utility <note>original: *utilitatem*; the argument that eating meat is "useful" for human health or social development</note> and other related issues. We will take our starting point from the practice of abstinence among certain nations; among these, the Greeks shall be presented first, as they will serve as the most substantial witnesses to this matter. Among those who have compiled Greek history both concisely and accurately is Dicaearchus the Peripatetic <note>original: *Dicæarchus Peripateticus*; a student of Aristotle known for his work on the history of Greek civilization</note>. Recounting the ancient life of Greece, he affirms that those early people—who, by their antiquity, stood closest to the gods, possessed the best nature, and led such a holy life that their era was considered a "golden age" compared to our own corrupted age made of the most depraved matter—were accustomed to kill no animal. He says that the poets also testify to this when they speak of that golden race of men:

> To whom, as they say,
> all good things were present, and the fertile
> Earth bore fruit of its own accord; but they themselves,
> at rest in great peace, enjoyed the abundant crops freely.

Explaining these lines, Dicaearchus says that such a life existed in the times of Saturn <note>original: *Saturni temporibus*; referring to the mythical Golden Age ruled by the god Saturn (Kronos), characterized by eternal spring and peace</note>: if indeed it must be admitted that such a state truly existed and was not celebrated in vain. Setting aside those elements which are purely mythical, the matter must be returned to a consideration of nature. For everything grew "of its own accord" by merit, clearly because the people themselves performed no labor, since they had not yet discovered the art of cultivating fields or any other craft. This

<meta>catchword: that</meta>

# ON ABSTINENCE

-> iii V <-

This was also the reason why they lived in leisure, free from any care, and without any labor; and if we are to agree with the opinions of the most refined physicians, they did not even fall ill. For there is no precept of theirs which contributes more to health than when they advise that every effort should be made to ensure that as few "excrements" <note>original: *excrementa*; in ancient medicine, this refers not just to waste but to any harmful surplus or residues produced by digestion, such as phlegm or bile, which were thought to cause disease</note> as possible are produced. Those early people indeed kept their bodies perpetually purged of such things, since they consumed food that was neither stronger than the nature of their bodies but rather weaker <note>meaning the food was easily processed and did not overwhelm their systems</note>, nor was it more than was moderate because of its easy availability—indeed, it was often less than was enough due to its scarcity.

Furthermore, there were neither wars nor seditions among them, since there was no prize proposed in their midst of a kind that anyone would wish to undertake a struggle to obtain. Thus, the sum of their whole life was nothing other than leisure, rest from the need to provide for themselves, health, peace, and friendship. For this reason, such a life appeared deservedly desirable to posterity who, because they had desired greater things, had fallen into many evils. Moreover, the well-known later saying, "Enough of the oak" <note>original: *satis quercus*; a Latin translation of the Greek proverb *halis druos*, used when moving away from primitive simplicity (eating acorns) toward "civilized" agriculture</note>, sufficiently declares the thinness of the diet of those first men, which required no elaborate preparation; for it is likely that the person who first attempted to change that frugal life was the one who said it. At the same time as that word, the pastoral life began to...

<meta>This page continues the historical narrative of Dicaearchus regarding the decline of the human condition from the "Golden Age." The previous page described the health and peace of the earliest humans who lived simply on the fruits of the earth. This page marks the transition to the second and third stages of civilization, characterized by the domestication of animals and the invention of agriculture.</meta>

### BOOK IV.

[...ex]troduced <note>The catchword "ex-" from the previous page suggests the full word was *extroducta*, meaning "brought forth" or "introduced."</note>: in which they now began to acquire for themselves superfluous possessions, and also began to lay their hands upon animals. For when they had observed that some animals were harmless, while others were wild and harmful, they applied their minds to tending the former, and attempted to suppress the latter by inflicting violence upon them. And thus, together with that new way of life, war was also introduced. And these things, he says, are not our own claims, but the words of those who have pursued these matters through diligent inquiry. For when possessions were now sufficiently numerous, some people gathered together and, inviting others to join them, strove to acquire such things for themselves, while others struggled to preserve what they already held. As time gradually progressed in this manner, and they noticed from time to time what seemed to be useful, they finally arrived at the third way of life: the cultivation of the fields.

And these are the details which **Dicaearchus** <note>original: *Dicæarchus*; a Greek philosopher and historian who argued that civilization's "progress" actually led to moral and physical decline</note> relates in his record of the ancient history of the Greeks, while he praises that blessed life of the ancients, to which **abstinence from living beings** <note>original: *abstinentia... ab animatis*; the practice of not eating animals</note> brought a perfection no less than its other virtues. For this reason, there was no war in that era, as it was entirely free from all injustice. But afterward, alongside the injustice committed against animals, both war and the contention of **greed** <term>the desire to have more (original: *plus habendi*); a translation of the Greek *pleonexia*, the insatiable desire for more than one's share, which ancient philosophers saw as the root of all social conflict</term> also crept in among them. ## *ON ABSTINENCE*

...experience clearly testifies that, together with the slaughter of these animals, luxury, war, and injustice invaded the lives of men. Truly, when **Lycurgus the Lacedaemonian** <note>Lycurgus was the legendary lawgiver of Sparta, famous for establishing the city's austere and militaristic social system</note> had later noticed this, although the eating of animals had already become prevalent, he is said to have established his republic in such a way that it required as little food sought from animals as possible. For he had prescribed the census <note>A formal assessment of wealth used to determine social rank and duty</note> of each individual's resources not in herds of oxen, or sheep, or goats, or horses, nor in an abundance of money, but in the possession of fields, which would produce a yield of seventy **bushels of barley** <note>original: *hordei modios*; a *modius* was a Roman unit of dry volume, roughly equivalent to 9 liters or 2 gallons</note> for a man and twelve for a woman, and likewise an equal portion of liquid produce <note>Likely referring to olive oil and wine</note>. For he considered such an amount of food to be sufficient both for a good physical condition and for the preservation of health, and he thought that nothing else was needed from outside.

Whence they also say that when he was traveling through the region after a long journey abroad, and noticed that the harvest had recently been completed, and saw the stacks of grain set against one another and equal, he smiled and said to his friends who were present that all of **Laconia** <note>The region in the southeastern Peloponnese surrounding the city of Sparta</note> seemed to him like the estate of many brothers who had recently divided their resources among themselves. Therefore, when he had thus cast out all luxury from **Sparta**, he was later permitted to abolish all use of both gold and silver coinage and to decree that they use only **iron currency**; and that of such a kind that just as ma... <note>original: *moneta ferrea*; the Spartan iron coins were intentionally made heavy and brittle to discourage the accumulation of wealth and foreign trade</note>

# BOOK IV.

...consisted of such great weight and mass, yet possessed so little value, that an exchange of ten minae <note>A *mina* was an ancient Greek unit of weight and currency. Ten minae was a significant sum, equivalent to roughly 1,000 drachmae, but in Lycurgus's system, it would have been physically massive.</note> would require a very large storage room in a house. For this reason, many types of crimes were banished from **Lacedaemon** <note>The ancient name for the city-state of Sparta</note>. For who would have wanted to steal, or receive as a bribe, or seize by force those things which were neither easy to hide nor glorious to possess—indeed, if they were broken into pieces, would they bring any utility at all? At the same time, useless crafts were also cast out, since the products of their labor could not be traded. For this iron currency could not be transferred to other Greeks, nor was it held in any value.

Wherefore it was not permitted to buy anything from foreigners or **perfumers** <note>original: *seplasiariis*; sellers of luxury ointments and exotic goods, often viewed as symbols of decadence</note>; nor did any foreign merchandise arrive in the ports. Indeed, neither did a **sophist** <note>original: *sophista*; a professional teacher of rhetoric, often criticized for "selling words" rather than truth</note> or a seller of words, nor any wandering prophet, nor a trickster and **juggler** <note>original: *præstigiator*; an illusionist or sleight-of-hand performer</note> come into **Laconia** <note>The region surrounding Sparta</note>, nor anyone else who was a craftsman of gold or silver ornaments. This was because the coinage was lacking there which could be of use elsewhere. 

And so **luxury** <note>original: *luxuria*</note>, deprived of all fuel and nourishment, wasted away by itself; and there was no greater advantage for those who possessed much, since the abundance of wealth had no way to break out and be spent, but lay entirely buried and idle. ### BOOK IV.

...says **Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher and student of Aristotle who wrote on laws and customs</note>, he rendered wealth both inglorious and useless through the communality of meals and the thriftiness of living. For there was no use for such riches, nor could anyone enjoy them; indeed, there could be no display or boasting of all that apparatus at all, since both the rich man and the poor man approached the same dinner. Thus, what is expressed in the common proverb was witnessed in Sparta alone: that **Plutus** <note>The Greek god of wealth, often depicted as blind</note> was blind, lying there like an inanimate and motionless painting. 

For it was not permitted for those who had already dined at home and were full to come to the communal gatherings; for the others, diligently observing those who did not drink and eat with them, were accustomed to condemn them as lacking self-control, as men who rejected the common life. For this reason, they also called these banquets **phiditia** <note>original: *phiditia*; the Spartan name for the communal mess</note>; either because they were the cause of mutual friendship and kindness—so that *phiditia* <note>original: φιδίτια</note> was said as if it were *philitia* <note>original: φιλίτια; derived from the Greek word for friendship, *philia*</note>, with the "d" taking the place of "l," which you might interpret as "friendly gatherings"—or because they accustomed men to cheapness and thrift, and were thus called *pheiditia* <note>original: φειδίτια; derived from the Greek word for thrift, *pheido*</note>, which could be called "thrifty gatherings."

# ON ABSTINENCE

...they frequented communal gatherings as if they were being led to a school of **temperance** <note>original: *temperantiæ*; the virtue of moderation and self-restraint</note>. For there they would listen to political discussions and witness noble amusements. They themselves became accustomed to joking and trading witticisms without falling into vulgarity, and learned not to be offended if they were stung by some sharp remark. Indeed, it was considered a hallmark of the **Laconian** <note>original: *Laconicum*; the Spartan character, known for being concise and tough</note> character to endure a joke at one’s own expense. However, if anyone found it too much to bear, they were permitted to decline it, and the other person would immediately stop their mockery.

Such was the frugality of the Lacedaemonians' <note>The formal name for the Spartans</note> diet, even though it had been established for the entire populace. For this reason, those who hailed from this republic are recorded in memory as being stronger, more temperate, and more devoted to what is right than those from other states, which were corrupt in both mind and body. It is clear that while **abstinence** was characteristic of this state, luxury of diet was the hallmark of the others.

Now, if we turn our attention to other nations as well, where special care is taken for justice, gentleness, and piety toward the gods, we shall clearly see that it serves the safety and benefit of cities that the rule of abstinence applies to at least some, if not all. These individuals, by offering sacrifices and other rites to the gods on behalf of the state, would atone for the sins of the people. For just as in the **Mysteries** <note>Religious rites, such as those at Eleusis, where initiates sought spiritual purification</note>, the boy who is called "from the Hearth" <note>original: *à Vefta dicitur puer*; a reference to the *pais aph'hestias*, a child chosen by lot to represent the city's youth and perform rituals to appease the gods</note> appeases the divine power by performing all commanded duties exquisitely on behalf of all the initiates; so, among nations and cities, the same...

# BOOK IV.

...[this is what] priests do, while they sacrifice on behalf of all people and, through their religious devotion and piety, implore the **divine power** <note>original: *numen*; a spiritual force or deity</note> to protect the safety of their people. Therefore, whether you observe the customs of the Greeks or those of the **barbarians** <note>In the ancient sense, "barbarians" referred to any non-Greek peoples, such as the Egyptians or Persians, without necessarily implying a lack of civilization</note>, it is commanded that some priests abstain entirely from eating any animal, while others must abstain from certain kinds. This rule varies from one group to another; yet, if one were to consider all the priests everywhere as a single body, it would seem that, collectively, they abstain from every kind of animal.

If, then, those who preside over the safety of the many—to whom all piety and religious duty toward the gods is entrusted—abstain from eating animals, who could be so bold as to fearlessly condemn such abstinence as if it were useless to the state? Furthermore, when writing about the Egyptian priests—whom he says were also considered philosophers among their own people—**Chaereon the Stoic** <note>A 1st-century Stoic philosopher and historian who served as a tutor to Emperor Nero and wrote extensively on Egyptian religion</note> reports that they chose to live within the temples to spend their lives in study and contemplation. 

Indeed, they believed it was naturally suited to their universal desire for the **contemplation of the gods** <note>original: *contemplationis deorum*; the intellectual and spiritual "gazing" upon the divine</note> that they should remain constantly near their sacred images. This lifestyle of divine veneration granted them security, as everyone honored these philosophers as if they were certain sacred beings. It also provided them with peace and quiet, for they only had to interact with the public during major festivals.
