# ON ABSTINENCE <note>original: *De Abstinentia*</note>

...and they had abstained from many other things. Indeed, this is, as it were, a certain common law of the temples of Egypt. Having rejected all trades and human labors, they dedicated their entire lives to the **beholding** <note>original: *inspectioni*; refers to the ritual act of looking upon the sacred images or mysteries</note> and contemplation of the gods. From this beholding, they gained honor, protection, and awe; from contemplation, they gained knowledge; and from both, they acquired a certain hidden discipline of character, worthy of ancient dignity. For to be always engaged in divine knowledge and **inspiration** <note>original: *afflatione*; literally a "breathing upon" by the divine</note> excludes all greed and arrogance, restrains the passions, and awakens the mind to understanding. Likewise, they practiced frugality, a meager diet, self-restraint, and endurance, as well as justice and equity in all things. 

They were also rendered venerable because they associated very little with others. For during the very time of **ritual purity** <note>original: *castimoniæ*; a state of being ceremonially clean, often involving sexual abstinence and dietary restrictions</note> and purification, they hardly associated even with their closest relatives and companions; nor were they seen by anyone else, except when the necessary needs of those practicing abstinence together required it. This was because they lived in the **inner sanctuaries** <note>original: *penetralia*; the most private, sacred parts of a temple</note>, which were inaccessible to the impure and unpurified, and were holy for the purpose of sacrifice. During all other times, they lived more freely with those like themselves; but they had no dealings with others who were devoid of religious duty. They were always near the gods, and either carried something before the statues of the gods, or presided over them, or arranged...

### BOOK III <note>original: *LIBER III*</note>

<sig>Y</sig>

...publicly they were seen with modesty and gravity. This was not a sign of any pride, but an indication of **natural reason** <note>original: *naturalis rationis*; refers to a disposition based on the inherent laws of nature rather than artificial social status</note>. For they displayed a venerable gravity even in their physical bearing. Their walk was moderate and their gaze was steady, so that whenever they wished, they hardly blinked their eyes; laughter was very rare, and of such a kind that it did not progress beyond a certain cheerfulness of expression. Furthermore, their hands were always kept within their robes. There was also a distinctive mark for each individual to signify their rank and status—for there were many levels of priesthood—which each had been assigned within the temples.

They maintained a meager and simple diet. As for wine, some used none at all, while others used very little, because of the damage it causes to the nerves and the **heaviness** <note>original: *repletionem*; literally a "filling up," a medical concept of the time where the head becomes congested, clouding thought</note> it creates in the head, which would hinder **contemplation** <note>original: *contemplationi*</note>; and also because it incited sexual desires. Consequently, they were very cautious in consuming other things as well. For instance, they never tasted bread during periods of **ritual purity** <note>original: *castimoniis*</note>. When it was not a time of abstinence, they were accustomed to eat bread crushed with **hyssop** <note>original: *hyssopo*; an aromatic plant often used in ancient purification rituals; here believed to neutralize the "heavy" or bloating effects of bread</note>, for they said that hyssop blunted most of its strength. Almost all of them abstained from oil for the most part, and many abstained from it entirely. If anyone did use it occasionally with vegetables, he used only a very small amount, just enough to soften the taste slightly. Furthermore, those foods which were produced outside of Egypt...

...foods and drinks, it was not lawful for them to touch; and thus they kept luxury largely excluded from their lives. Indeed, they abstained from all fish that were found in Egypt; likewise from all quadrupeds which were **solid-hoofed** <note>original: *solipeda*; animals like horses or donkeys</note>, or **cloven-footed** <note>original: *multifida*; literally "multi-split," referring to animals with paws or divided hooves</note>, or horned. They also abstained from birds that fed on flesh. Many, furthermore, abstained from all animals whatsoever, and during periods of **purification** <note>original: *purificationibus*; ritual cycles of cleansing</note> everyone abstained; for they then believed that not even eggs should be eaten by them.

Furthermore, they maintained many other observations among themselves. Regarding cattle, they rejected the females for food; as for the males, they also rejected them if they were twins, or spotted, or of varying color, or of diverse shape, or if they had already been tamed—since these were considered already consecrated to labor—and others similar to those that were honored. They were also rejected if they represented any other image by their appearance, or if they had only one eye, or if they tended toward a **human likeness** <note>original: *humanam similitudinem*; a reference to birth defects or features in animals that ancient observers interpreted as mimicking human faces, often viewed as portents</note>.

They carry out these duties even more meticulously regarding birds. For instance, to mention this as an example, they believe they must not eat the **turtle-dove** <note>original: *turturem*</note>. <header>BOOK IV.</header>

...than fall from them, they decided that they must forbid themselves that entire category [of animals]. Indeed, some of their common rites were the same, while others differed according to the specific class of priests and were adapted to the individual gods they served. However, **chaste practices** <term>original: *castimoniæ*; ritual behaviors involving physical and moral purity</term> and **purifications** <note>original: *purificationes*</note> were observed by all. The timing for these was set whenever they were about to perform some sacred service; the number of days was fixed beforehand, sometimes forty-two, sometimes more or fewer, yet it never fell below a **sevenfold** <note>original: *septenario*; a cycle of seven days</note> period. 

During these times, they abstained from all living creatures [meat], and from every kind of vegetable and legume. Most especially, they abstained from sexual union with women; indeed, they avoided the company of males at all other times as well. They washed three times a day in cold water: when they rose from bed, before they ate, and when they went to sleep. If it happened by chance that they were polluted by emissions during dreams <note>original: *feminis fusione insomniis*; a reference to nocturnal emissions</note>, they immediately purified their bodies with a bath. Indeed, they used cold baths during the rest of their lives as well, though not quite so frequently. 

Their bed was woven from the shoots of palm trees, which they call **bais** <term>a Greek transcription of the Egyptian word for palm branches</term>. A wooden **half-cylinder** <note>original: *semicylindrus*; a curved headrest, typical of ancient Egyptian furniture, used instead of a soft pillow</note>, smoothed to fit, was placed under the head as a support. They practiced thirst, hunger, and a **frugal diet** <term>original: *parcum uictum*</term> throughout their entire lives. Such was their **self-restraint** <term>original: *continentiae*</term> that although they made use of no walks or exercises <note>original: *gestationibus*; referring to light physical activities or being carried for health</note>, they not only lived their lives free from disease, but remained so vigorous that even moderate...

# ON ABSTINENCE

<note>The text begins mid-sentence, concluding a thought from the previous page about why their lifestyle was kept simple.</note> ...so that their strength might suffice for their labors; for indeed they bore many burdens in the performance of sacred rites, and undertook many duties that seemed greater than what common strength could manage. They divided the night for the **observation of the heavens** <note>original: *caelestium observationem*; this refers to both astronomical tracking for the calendar and astrological interpretation</note>, and sometimes for the purpose of sanctification. They distributed the day to the worship of the gods: during which they followed a cycle of hymns three or four times—in the morning and evening, when the sun rose, when it crossed the **middle of the sky** <note>original: *medium caelum*; high noon</note>, and when it was carried toward its setting. 

During the rest of the time, devoting themselves to **arithmetic and geometric** contemplations, they were always working on or thinking out something; and generally, they were engaged in **practical research** <note>original: *experientia*; here meaning empirical study of the natural world</note>. They were accustomed to use the same exercise even on winter nights, staying awake for the study of literature, since they had no care for any financial gain and were free from the slavery of that troublesome mistress, **Luxury**. 

Truly, their tireless and constant labor manifested their endurance, and their lack of all desires showed the self-control of these men. For since they avoided foreign luxuries and customs, they considered it most impious to leave Egypt. Indeed, it seemed permitted only to those who were compelled to handle royal business to travel; yet even those people had such care for their **ancestral institutions** <note>original: *patriorum institutorum*; the traditional laws and religious customs of Egypt</note> that if they were caught violating them even slightly, they were cast out. And indeed, the true method of **philosophizing** <note>Porphyry uses "philosophy" here to describe the entire priestly way of life, including science and ritual</note> remained among the **prophets** <term>original: *prophetas*; the highest rank of Egyptian priests, responsible for temple management and theology</term>, and the **sacrificers** <term>original: *sacrificos*; priests who oversaw the physical offerings</term>, and the **scribes** <term>original: *scribas*; the hierogrammats or sacred writers</term>, and also...

# BOOK VIII <note>While this text is from Porphyry's *On Abstinence*, this specific heading likely refers to its placement within a larger collection of works, such as Eusebius’s *Preparation for the Gospel*, where these passages were frequently cited.</note>

...was preserved for the **time-keepers** <note>original: *horologos*; those responsible for tracking the hours and celestial movements</note>. The rest of the multitude of priests, temple keepers, and ministers also live purely and abstinently, though not with such exact diligence as the higher ranks. And these are the things concerning the Egyptians that were handed down to memory by a man studious of truth and accurate, who philosophized among the **Stoics** <note>The Stoics were a school of Hellenistic philosophy that emphasized virtue, reason, and living in accordance with nature</note> not in vain, but with great solidity. 

From this spiritual exercise and conjunction with God, they came to know that divinity did not pass through man alone, nor did the soul inhabit a tabernacle on earth in man alone; rather, they believed that the same soul passes through almost all living creatures. For this reason, they included every animal alongside man in the **fashioning of the gods**; in these images, they mixed the bodies of beasts and humans, and likewise the bodies of birds and humans. 

For among them, individual statues of the gods show a human form up to the neck, but have the face of a bird, a lion, or some other animal placed upon it; or, conversely, they display the body of another animal joined beneath a human head. By these signs, they clearly signify that, in the judgment of the gods, these creatures share a fellowship with one another; and that animals—both the tame and the wild—are kindred to us and exist not without a certain divine will. 

Because of this, the lion is worshiped as a god; and certain parts of Egypt, which they call a **district** <note>original: *pascuum*; literally "pasture," but here referring to the administrative regions or 'nomes' of Egypt</note>, are named **Leontopolites** <note>City of the Lion</note> from the lion, another **Busirites** <note>House of Osiris, associated with the bull/ox</note> from the ox, and another **Lycopolites** <note>City of the Wolf</note> from the wolf. For the power...

## ON ABSTINENCE

...for the power of God, which is diffused into all things, they venerated through the **fellow creatures** <note>original: *compascua animalia*; literally "animals of the same pasture," implying a shared existence between humans and animals</note> which each god had exhibited. 

Moreover, they worshipped **water and fire** <note>These elements were central to Egyptian ritual; water represented the Nile's life-giving properties, and fire represented the sun and purification</note> most of all elements, as these are the primary causes of our safety; they displayed these in their temples and sacred rites. Even today, this is seen in the opening of the temple of the holy **Serapis** <note>Serapis was a Graeco-Egyptian deity whose cult combined aspects of Osiris and Apis with Greek features</note>, where the worship is conducted through fire and water. For the one who sings the hymns pours water drop by drop and displays the fire when, standing upon the threshold, he awakens the god in the **native language of the Egyptians** <note>original: *patria Ægyptiorum uoce*; this refers to the use of the ancient Egyptian tongue in liturgy, which was believed to have inherent magical power</note>. These things, therefore, they venerate most of all, as they are the greatest participants in the sacred rites. 

After these, they worship all animals; indeed, they even worship man himself in the village of **Anabe** <note>likely referring to Anabeia or a similar district in Middle Egypt where a human was historically treated as a living embodiment of divinity</note>, where sacrifices are offered to him and victims are burned upon altars. Consequently, they would not, a short time later, eat those things which are held in the same honor as a human being. For just as one must abstain from eating man, so must one abstain from the others. 

Add to this that, from their excellent wisdom and the familiarity they have with the gods, they discovered which animals are more pleasing to the gods than even men are. For example, they dedicate the **hawk** to the **Sun** <note>The hawk or falcon was the primary symbol of Ra and Horus, the solar deities</note>; they say its entire nature consists of blood and spirit. They believe the hawk takes pity on man and weeps over his corpse if it should happen to find one, scattering earth upon the eyes, in which they believe the **solar light** dwells. They have also observed that the hawk lives for many years...

...they turned: but when it has departed from life, they assert that it obtains the power of divination, and that once it has been released from the body, it becomes highly rational and a foreteller of future events. For they claim that [these souls] both animate the statues of the gods and move the temples. 

Someone unlearned and ignorant of divine matters might despise and detest the **scarab** <note>original: *Scarabæus*; the dung beetle (Scarabaeus sacer), which symbolized the sun and rebirth in ancient Egypt</note>; but the Egyptians venerate it as a living image of the **Sun** <note>original: *Sol*; often associated with the god Ra, who was believed to roll the sun across the sky much like the beetle rolls its ball</note>. For since the scarab is said to be only of the male sex, it emits its seed into dung, and when it has shaped it into a sphere, it rolls it backward with its hind legs—just as the Sun moves across the heavens—and it observes a cycle of **twenty-eight days** <note>the lunar month; ancient observers linked the beetle's life cycle to the phases of the moon</note>, which is the lunar period. 

In a similar manner, they philosophize about the **ram, the crocodile, the vulture, and the ibis** <note>these animals were sacred to the gods Amun, Sobek, Mut, and Thoth respectively, representing different divine attributes</note>, and indeed about every single animal; thus, out of prudence and divine wisdom, they proceeded even to the veneration and worship of other animals. 

But the unlearned man does not even notice how these [priests] did not follow the common drift of men who know nothing, nor did they themselves wander into the path of folly. Instead, having overcome the ignorance of the common people into which everyone first falls, they judged those things which most held in no esteem to be worthy of divine worship. 

Furthermore, this also led them to the worship of animals no less than the other reasons mentioned above: the fact that the soul of each animal, when it has been released from the body, and ra- <note>the text breaks here at the catchword *tiona-*, likely continuing as *rationabilis* (rational) on the following page</note>

-># ON ABSTINENCE<-

...they had perceived that it is **rational**, and possessing foreknowledge of the future, and prophetic, and the accomplisher of all things which a human being, once freed from the body, might be able to perform. Wherefore, they rightly held those animals in honor and, as far as was possible, they abstained from eating them. 

While there are many other reasons why the Egyptians worshipped the gods through animals, a full review of them all would result in a discourse longer than would seem to suit the present subject; therefore, let these points suffice for now. 

However, one matter must not be overlooked: when they embalm the corpses of the nobility, they remove the **belly** <note>original: *ventrem*; referring to the internal organs or viscera</note> separately and place it in a chest. Then, along with other things that act on behalf of the deceased's **intelligible part** <note>the higher, rational soul as opposed to the physical appetites</note>, the chest is brought out toward the **Sun** <note>original: *Sol*; the god Ra, the ultimate judge and source of life</note>. A prayer is then offered by one of the embalmers on behalf of the person being buried, acting as a witness. This prayer was translated from the native tongue by **Euphantus** <note>an author of Egyptian history, often cited by Porphyry regarding these rituals</note> in this manner:

->“O Lord Sun, and all you gods who have granted life to men, receive me, and lead me into the eternal dwelling-place of the gods. For I have always pursued with piety those gods whom I received from my parents to be worshipped, for as long as I lived in that age; and I have always honored those who procreated my body. Of other men, I have neither killed anyone, nor defrauded anyone of what was entrusted to me, nor afflicted anyone with any other destructive evil. If indeed I have sinned in my life, either by eating or drinking things that were not permitted, I have not sinned through myself, but **through these things** <note>original: *per haec*; referring to the internal organs held in the chest</note>.”<-

At the same time, he points to the chest in which the belly was placed...

...was shown; and when he had said these things, he would cast the chest into the river. Then they would embalm the rest of the body as though it were pure. And so they believed they needed an **excuse** before the gods for those things which they had eaten or drunk, and for that reason they judged that the **belly** <note>original: *ventrem*; the internal organs, seen as the seat of appetite</note> should be treated with this manifest disgrace <note>By separating and discarding the organs of digestion, the Egyptians symbolically purged the deceased of the sins of gluttony and physical desire.</note>.

But truly the **Jews**—before their institutions were first undermined by **Antiochus** <note>Antiochus IV Epiphanes (r. 175–164 BCE), who attempted to suppress Jewish customs, leading to the Maccabean Revolt.</note>, and later almost destroyed by the **Romans** when the temple in **Jerusalem** <note>original: *Hierosolyma*</note> was captured and trampled by everyone to whom it had been inaccessible <note>A reference to the Holy of Holies, which was restricted to the High Priest but violated during the Roman conquest in 70 CE.</note>, and the city was utterly overturned—abstained from eating many other animals, but especially from **swine’s flesh**, a practice they observe even to this day. 

And there were three sects of those practicing **philosophy** <note>The author classifies these Jewish religious groups as "philosophical schools" to better explain them to a Greco-Roman audience.</note> among them: one of the **Pharisees**, another of the **Sadducees**, and the third of the **Essenes**, which seemed to be the most venerable of all. These third ones led their life in this manner, as **Josephus** <note>Flavius Josephus, the famous 1st-century historian whose works are the primary source for this period.</note> testifies in various books. For he mentions them in the second book of the *Jewish History* <note>original: *Iudaica historia*; today titled *The Jewish War*</note>, which he completed in seven books; and in the eighteenth book of the *Antiquities*, which he pursued through twenty books; and in the second book *Against the Greeks* <note>original: *adversus Graecos*; today titled *Against Apion*</note>, of which there are two books. 

The **Essenes** are therefore Jews by race, who love one another more than others do. They turn away from **pleasures** as if they were a vice, but they consider **self-control** <note>original: *continentia*</note> and not succumbing to the passions to be a **virtue** <note>original: *uirtus*</note>. # ON ABSTINENCE

...marriage <note>original: *[matrimo]gium*; completing the word from the previous page's catchword</note> they do not indeed admit for themselves, but taking in the tender young children of others while they are still receptive to training <note>original: *disciplinas*; refers to the sect's rigorous way of life and education</note>, they regard them as their own kin and mold them according to their own customs. And they do this, not to abolish marriage or the succession of their kind in the future, but to avoid the wantonness <note>original: *petulantias*; implying both physical desire and perceived fickleness</note> of women. They are despisers of riches, and there is among them a wonderful sharing of goods, nor is it possible to see anyone among them who excels in the possession of wealth. For there is a law among them that whoever enters into this community <term>college <gloss>original: *collegium*; a formal society or brotherhood</gloss></term> must surrender his private property to the public fund. And so it happens that among all of them, neither the humiliation of poverty nor any preeminence of wealth is seen; but from the mixed possessions of individuals, one single fund is available to all, just as if they were brothers. 

They considered oil to be a stain upon the body; even if someone’s body were anointed against their will, they would wipe it off. For to have a dry, un-oiled skin <note>original: *squalere*; in the Greco-Roman world, oiled skin was a sign of health and status; the Essenes rejected this as a luxury or defilement</note> was considered beautiful among them, and it was held as an honorable thing to always wear white garments. Stewards of their common property are elected by everyone without distinction, according to what necessity requires. They do not have one single city of their own, but many of them live together in every city. To these houses, the door is open to all members of the same sect who arrive from elsewhere. Those whom they have seen for the first time they recognize as if they were close family. Wherefore, whenever they travel, they carry nothing... <note>original: *nihil sum[ptuum]*; they carry no expenses or supplies, relying on the hospitality of their community elsewhere</note>

...rds <note>original: *sumptuum*; completing the word from the previous page's "stewa[rds]" and the OCR's "ptuum"</note> for the sake of expenses, as they are accustomed to carry nothing with them. They never change their shoes or their garments unless they are either entirely torn or worn out by age. They neither buy nor sell anything; rather, each person gives what he has to anyone in need and receives in return whatever may be useful to him. Indeed, they are permitted to take whatever they need from whomever they wish, even without any such repayment.

Regarding the Divine, they certainly practice <term>piety <gloss>original: *pietatem*; a sense of duty and religious devotion</gloss></term> in a peculiar manner. For before the sun rises, they speak of nothing profane; instead, they pour out certain ancestral prayers to the sun, as if they were entreating it to rise. When this is finished, they are dismissed by their overseers to exercise the crafts which each of them knows. Having worked vigorously there until the fifth hour <note>roughly 11:00 AM</note>, they all gather again in one place. There, girded with linen coverings, they wash their bodies in cold water. 

After this <term>purification <gloss>original: *purificationem*; ritual washing to maintain spiritual cleanliness</gloss></term>, they meet in their own private dwelling, where no one of a different sect is permitted to enter. Being thus pure, they proceed into the dining room as if into some sacred shrine. When all have sat down quietly in order, the <term>baker <gloss>original: *pistor*</gloss></term> places loaves of bread, and the cook sets a single vessel of one course <note>original: *opsonii*; a general term for a side dish, often cooked meat or vegetables eaten with bread</note> before each man. However, before the food—which is chaste and pure—is taken, a <term>priest <gloss>original: *sacerdos*</gloss></term> offers a prayer; it is not lawful for anyone to taste the food before this prayer. After the meal, prayers are likewise [offered] by the same...

In addition to these things, he swears that he will pass on their teachings to others in no other way than how he himself received them; that he will abstain from <term>robbery <gloss>original: *latrocinio*</gloss></term>; and that he will carefully guard the books of his <term>sect <gloss>original: *sectae*</gloss></term> and the <term>names of the angels <gloss>original: *angelorum nomina*</gloss></term>. <note>The mention of "names of angels" suggests a mystical tradition within the Essenes involving the study or invocation of celestial beings, which was kept secret from the public.</note> Such is the nature of their oath. 

Those who violate these vows and are expelled from the community often meet a wretched end. Because they remain bound by their oaths and customs, they are forbidden from accepting food from those outside the order. Consequently, they are forced to eat grass until they eventually perish, consumed by hunger. <note>Because the Essenes viewed all food not prepared according to their specific purity laws as defiling, an expelled member who kept his oath would rather starve on wild herbs than eat "unclean" bread.</note> For this reason, the Essenes, moved by mercy, have received many back into the fold when they were in their final extremities, judging that the suffering they endured while at the brink of death was punishment enough for their sins.

For this opinion is firmly held among them: that just as bodies are subject to corruption and consist of unstable and decaying matter, so souls are immortal and endure forever. # BOOK IV.

<sig>Z iii</sig>

...liberated from servitude, they then rejoice and are carried on high. Furthermore, from this kind of living and this exercise in truth and piety, it rightly happens that many are found among them who even foreknow the future, as they have been versed from their earliest youth in the sacred books, various <term>purifications <gloss>original: *castimoniis*</gloss></term>, and the sayings of the prophets; they rarely err in their predictions. And such indeed is the <term>sect <gloss>original: *secta*</gloss></term> of the **Essenes** among the **Jews**. 

Truly, it was forbidden to all <note>The author shifts here from the specific practices of the Essenes to the general laws of the Jews as established by Moses.</note> to eat from the flesh of swine, or fish lacking scales (which we call <term>cartilaginous <gloss>original: *carthilagineos*</gloss></term>), or from those quadrupeds which have a solid hoof. It was also forbidden to them to kill—much less to eat—those animals which as it were pleaded for mercy and fled to their houses for refuge. 

Likewise, the **lawgiver** <note>Referring to Moses and the laws found in the Torah, specifically Deuteronomy 22:6.</note> did not permit the killing of parents along with their young. He had also commanded that even in a hostile region, one must spare the animals that work with us, and beware of perpetrating their slaughter. Nor did he fear that the animal species, growing into an excessive abundance because they were not slaughtered, would bring hunger upon men. For he knew best, first, that the nature of those that produce many offspring is such that they last only a short time; secondly, that there are many animals which, if deprived of human care, easily perish; and furthermore, that there are others which keep an increasing multitude in check. 

The proof of this is that although we abstain from many creatures—for instance, lizards, worms, snakes, dogs...

<meta>This page continues the argument that refraining from eating animals does not lead to human starvation due to overpopulation of species. It transitions from general laws to the specific history of the Syrians and a Phoenician account of how meat-eating first began through an accidental taste during a sacrifice.</meta>

-># ON ABSTINENCE<-

...nonetheless, there is no reason to fear that we might perish from hunger because of such abstinence. Add to this that to eat those things which grow to excess is not the same as to kill them; for although we might kill most of these <note>Referring to culling animals that multiply too quickly and threaten crops.</note>, we believe that we should taste nothing from them. Indeed, it is recorded in history that the **Syrians** also abstained from animals in ancient times, and therefore were not accustomed to sacrifice them even to the gods. Later, however, although they may have sacrificed them to avert certain evils <note>Meaning rituals performed to turn away bad luck or divine wrath.</note>, they were never brought to the point of allowing the eating of meat.

In the course of time, however, **Neanthes of Cyzicus** <note>A Greek historian who wrote about the myths and rituals of various cities.</note> and **Asclepiades of Cyprus** say that in the time of **Pygmalion**—who was a Phoenician by race and held command over the Cyprians—the eating of meat was introduced as a result of a certain <term>expiation <gloss>original: *piaculo*</gloss></term> <note>A religious act or sacrifice meant to make amends for a sin or crime.</note>. For **Asclepiades**, in his book *On Cyprus and Phoenicia*, speaks in this manner: "In the beginning, certainly no living thing was customarily sacrificed to the gods; indeed, there was no law concerning this, because it was prohibited by natural law."

At a certain time, however, they say that when a "soul was demanded for a soul" <note>original: *anima pro anima* — an ancient concept of substitutionary sacrifice where an animal's life is offered to save a human's.</note>, the first victim was sacrificed and entirely consumed by flames. When that sacrifice was being performed later and the victim was being burned, a small piece of meat fell to the ground; the priest who stood by immediately picked it up; and since he had burned his fingers on the charred meat, he happened to bring them to his mouth as a remedy for the burn...

And he who assumes the lion-like persona takes various animal forms upon himself.

# BOOK IV.

AA ii

<meta>catchword: cults</meta>

...they are all of one race, all descended from the lineage of a single father and a single mother. The **Samanaeans** <note>Also known as *Shramanas*, these were non-Vedic ascetics including early Buddhists and Jains.</note>, however, are not like this; rather, as we have already said, they are gathered from every tribe of the Indians. Therefore, the **Brahmans** do not live under the rule of a king, nor do they perform any civic duties alongside others. Instead, among them, those who are philosophers live some in the mountains and others by the **Ganges** river. Those in the mountains live on fruits and cow’s milk curdled with herbs. Those by the river also sustain themselves on fruits, which grow in abundance near the river; for the earth produces fresh fruit almost constantly. It even produces a great deal of wild <term>rice <gloss>original: *oryzæ*</gloss></term> on its own, which they are accustomed to use for food whenever the fruit fails. For them, to taste any other kind of food, or to even touch any nourishment derived from living creatures, is considered an act of extreme impurity and is viewed as no different from impiety. 

They offer worship to God with great religious devotion and piety. Indeed, they dedicate the greater part of the day and night to hymns and prayers to the gods. Each man, however, stays in his own small hut and remains alone as much as possible. For the **Brahmans** do not easily tolerate living together or speaking much with one another; and if it should happen that they do meet, they withdraw afterward and do not speak for many days. They also fast frequently. 

The **Samanaeans**, as we have said, are those who are chosen <note>Unlike the Brahmans, who were a caste born into their role, the Samanaeans were a voluntary monastic order.</note>. When someone is to be admitted into their college, he approaches the leaders of the city or the village where he resides, and there he gives up his goods and possessions...

...[having renounced all their] faculties and possessions, and having shaved off every superfluous part of the body, he receives a robe. And thus, taking no account of wife or children, if they should happen to be present—as if they no longer belong to him at all—he withdraws to the **Samanaeans** <note>These were non-Vedic Indian ascetics, primarily Buddhist monks or Jain monastics, often contrasted with the hereditary Brahmans.</note>. The life of the **Samanaeans** is as follows. They live outside the city and spend the entire day in divine discourses, in places where they have houses and temples built by the king. In these places, <term>stewards <gloss>original: œconomi</gloss></term> are appointed, who receive a certain stipend from the king for the nourishment of those who gather there. Their diet is prepared from <term>rice <gloss>original: oryza</gloss></term>, bread, fruit, and vegetables. For when they have entered the house at the signal of a bell, the **Samanaeans** pour out prayers; once they have done this, and the signal is given again, servants offer a dish to each man—for two do not eat from the same vessel—and feed them rice. If anyone requires any variety, a vegetable or some fruit is added, which they eat quite quickly. 

Both they and the **Brahmans** are held in such high veneration by others that even the king sometimes visits them and asks them to pray to God for the welfare of the region, or consults them on what ought to be done. They themselves are so disposed toward death that they unwillingly endure their time of living as if it were a certain necessary service to nature, and therefore they hasten to release their souls from their bodies. And often, when they feel they are in good <note>The text cuts off here at "habere" (well/to have), continuing the thought that these ascetics often sought ritual suicide when healthy to reach the next life.</note>

AA iii
<meta>catchword: things (res)</meta>

...perceive they [are doing well], they depart from life though no evil compels them, having first announced their intention to others. Nor is there anyone who would prevent them; rather, all proclaim them blessed, and each of their household members entrusts them with certain messages <gloss>original: mandata</gloss> to be delivered to their deceased kinsmen. So stable and true do they and many others believe the life shared between souls to be after death.

Once they have received the messages entrusted to them, they cast their bodies into the fire so that they may separate the soul in its purest state from the body; thus, celebrated by all, they meet their death. Indeed, their friends and relatives dismiss them to death with a more tranquil mind than other men feel when sending their own citizens on journeys <note>original: peregrinationes</note> that are not even particularly long. They weep for themselves as those who remain behind in life, but they call the dead blessed, seeing as they have now received an immortal lot.

Nor has any <term>sophist <gloss>original: sophista</gloss></term> <note>In this context, Porphyry uses "sophist" dismissively to refer to professional orators or pseudo-philosophers who argue for the sake of winning rather than seeking truth.</note> or declaimer stepped forward among these people—or among the others I mentioned previously—of the sort that are now so numerous among the Greeks, to cast doubt and say: "If everyone were to imitate you, what would become of us?" Nor have human affairs been thrown into confusion because of these men. For not everyone has imitated them, and those who have followed their example have been a cause of order and tranquility rather than confusion for the people. 

Furthermore, the law did not impose any necessity upon them; instead, while it permitted others to eat meat, it left these men to be their own masters <note>original: sui iuris; a legal term meaning "of one's own right."</note> and venerated them as being, in a sense, superior to the law. For the law did not regard these men as perpetrators of injustice...

...things, he has subjected [them] to the punishment he inflicts, but others. <note>The text here concludes a point about the different burdens or consequences placed upon different classes of people.</note> To those, however, who ask what would happen if everyone were to imitate this kind of life, one must give that answer of **Pythagoras**. For if everyone were kings, he said, it would result in life being led with difficulty; yet, for that reason, kingship is not to be shunned. So also, if everyone were upright, no way would be found to manage and arrange the state <note>original: respublica</note> in which the dignity belonging to uprightness <gloss>original: probitas</gloss> should be preserved. But no one in their right mind would be so insane as not to think it beneficial for everyone to strive to become upright.

Indeed, he <note>Referring to the lawgiver or the ideal of Reason.</note> grants many other things to the common and confused crowd which he would not permit to a philosopher, nor even to one who rightly administers a city. For although he does not admit every craft <gloss>original: ars</gloss> into the administration of the state, he does not forbid them from being practiced. Nevertheless, he bars the workers of base <note>original: sordidarum</note> crafts <note>In the ancient world, "base crafts" usually referred to manual labor or trades that were thought to dull the mind or involve "dirty" work.</note> from magistrates and from any presiding over those matters in which justice or the other virtues are required. 

Neither does he forbid most people from having relations with prostitutes; even while demanding a tax from the prostitutes, he nonetheless deems their company to be infamous and shameful for modest men. Likewise, he does not prohibit spending one's life in taverns <note>original: Cauponis</note>; yet nothing prevents him from holding this in the greatest disgrace for freeborn <note>original: ingenuis</note> men. 

The same occurs in diet as well. For not those who commonly per-

Moreover, those sacred laws have existed among individual nations and cities which command purity <gloss>original: castimoniam</gloss> for holy men and forbid the eating of living things <note>original: animatorum esum; this refers specifically to the consumption of animal flesh, as animals were "beings with souls."</note>: indeed, they even prohibit the multitude from certain things, either for the sake of piety or because of the harm that results from food.

For if some people abstain from certain things for the sake of piety, he who would be pious in all things will undoubtedly judge that he must abstain from all [flesh].

> I do not follow the nocturnal rites of **Bacchus** <note>Dionysus, the god of wine and ecstasy, whose "nocturnal" rites often involved raw meat.</note>, nor raw feasts,
> Nor do I carry the fiery torches for the **Mountain Mother** <note>Cybele, a mother goddess associated with wild nature and ecstatic worship.</note>.
> But, adorned in white garments,
> I reject the generation of mortals <note>This refers to abstaining from sexual reproduction and the cycle of birth.</note>.
> I avoid touching the tombs of the dead,
> And I take care that I never eat anything that has had a soul <note>original: "animatis"; referring to animals, which were believed to possess a "sensitive" soul unlike plants.</note>.

For they established that the purity of a holy man consists in non-mixing, just as, on the contrary, impurity and defilement consist in mixing. Therefore, because nourishment from fruits is not taken from the dead, nor from living beings <note>original: "animatis"</note>, they believed that when they offered these things to nature, that which is provided by nature was by no means defiled. Indeed, they considered that the slaughter of animals endowed with sensation, and the withdrawal of their souls, brought defilement upon the living; and this was much more the case when a body which once possessed senses—now deprived of sense and dead—was mixed with those who are living and possess sense <note>This is Porphyry's core argument against meat-eating: it is the "mixing" of a dead body into a living one, which pollutes the soul.</note>. 

And for that reason, chastity <gloss>original: castimonia</gloss> and purity in all things consist in the setting aside and abstinence from many contrary things, but in the solitude and adoption of those things which are kindred and fitting. From this principle, even sexual acts <note>original: "Venerea"; literally "things of Venus."</note> defile, since they are the union of female and male. For whether seed is conceived from the conjunction with the body, or it is not conceived, it brings defilement to the soul from the "death" of that which is applied <note>The author views the loss of generative seed as a form of "mortification" or death that stains the soul.</note>. Moreover, the congress of males with males contaminates in a twofold way: both because it results in what is dead, and because it is against...

### BOOK IV.

...exists through nature. And indeed, sexual matters and female emissions pollute for this reason: because the soul is mixed with the body and is dragged downward toward pleasure. The passions of the soul also defile it when it is confusedly entwined with the irrational part, and the masculine is effeminized <note>In Neoplatonic thought, the "masculine" represents the active, rational soul, while the "feminine" represents the passive, irrational, or material aspect. To be "effeminized" meant the soul was losing its rational autonomy to physical desires.</note>. For the names of "defilement" and "pollution" signify, in a certain way, a mixture between things of a different kind—especially when a certain infection follows of the sort that can only be wiped away with great difficulty. 

Hence even in the craft of dyeing, which exists through mixture, when one species is confounded with another, they call it "staining" <note>original: "inquinare"</note>. No differently than the woman, says **Homer**, who stains ivory with Tyrian purple <note>original: "tyrio... ostro"; a reference to Iliad 4.141, describing a Carian or Maeonian woman dyeing ivory to make a cheek-piece for a horse.</note>. Furthermore, painters call their mixtures "corruptions." 

Likewise, common custom calls that which is unmixed and pure "uncorrupted," "sincere," and "unpolluted." For water mixed with earth is corrupted, and is no longer sincere <note>In this context, "sincere" (sincerus) retains its literal Latin meaning of "pure" or "without foreign mixture."</note>. But that water which flows, carried by a swift current, rejects the earth it encounters; which, as **Hesiod** says, flows clear, streaming from an eternal spring. For because it is uncorrupted and unmixed, it provides a healthy drink. 

In the same way, a woman who has admitted no exhalation or mixture of seed into herself is called "uncorrupted." Therefore, corruption and defilement consist in the mixture of opposites: that is, of the dead into animals, and of those things which once lived with sensation into the living, and of dead flesh...

<sig>BB</sig>
<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>Porphyry argues that impurity is fundamentally a "mixing" of different or opposing substances, using examples from sexual acts, the dyeing of fabric, and the siltation of water. He concludes that eating meat is a form of corruption because it mixes the dead with the living.</summary>
<keywords>purity, pollution, mixture, soul and body, Homer, Hesiod, asceticism, abstinence, Tyrian purple</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that "purity" is the state of a thing being unmixed with foreign substances. The previous page established that mixing different species—whether in dyeing fabrics or in the soul’s interaction with the body—results in "staining" or "corruption." Here, he applies this logic to the defilement caused by death and the consumption of meat.</meta>

### ON ABSTINENCE

...the introduction [of meat] into living beings deservedly brings about the pollution and defilement of our nature. Just as the soul is also defiled when it is joined to the body. For this reason, both the one who is born—through the mixing of the soul with the body—and the one who dies, contract pollution from the event; for death leaves behind a dead body that is alien to and different from the living person <note>In many ancient Mediterranean cultures, contact with a corpse or the process of childbirth required ritual purification because they were seen as moments where the boundary between the sacred soul and the profane material world was blurred.</note>. Likewise, the soul is contaminated by anger, and by desires, and by a multitude of disturbances, for which diet also tends to be a cause in some way. 

For just as water that flows over rocks is more uncorrupted than that which runs through marshes—seeing as it does not drag along much silt—so the soul that manages its affairs through a **dry body**, rather than one overflowing with the juices of foreign flesh, is better, more uncorrupted, and more ready for understanding <note>original: "intelligentia". This reflects the ancient philosophical idea, often attributed to Heraclitus, that "a dry soul is wisest." Moisture was associated with the heavy, irrational, and material world.</note>. Indeed, they even say that thyme—which is very dry and endowed with the greatest pungency—produces the best honey for bees. 

Therefore, thought itself is defiled—or rather, the thinking being is defiled—when it is mixed with the faculties and operations of either imagination or opinion <note>In the hierarchy of the mind, "opinion" (doxa) and "imagination" were considered lower faculties because they rely on sensory input, whereas pure "thought" or "intellect" was seen as divine and independent of the body.</note>. Purification and holiness, however, consist of a separation from all these things, and a state of solitude; it is the nourishment that preserves each thing in its own essence. For in this way, you would rightly call that which causes a stone to persist in its form its "nourishment"; and for a plant, that which preserves it in its growing and fruiting; and for the animal body, that which [protects] its constitution...

<sig>aa</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...tects (tur.)</meta>

<summary>Porphyry argues that the soul is defiled by its mixture with the body and material passions, comparing a pure soul to clear water running over rocks. He defines purification as the separation of the soul from physical "juices" and foreign influences, advocating for a dry, meat-free diet to maintain intellectual clarity.</summary>
<keywords>soul, pollution, purification, diet, Neoplatonism, meat-eating, intellect, Heraclitus, dry soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the end of the previous page regarding the "staining" of the soul. It moves from discussing the physical impurities of meat to the philosophical distinction between the nourishment required for the body versus that required for the mind.</meta>

# BOOK IV

### BB ii

...mixed. For it is one thing to nourish and provide necessities, and quite another to supply luxuries. Therefore, according to the diversity of those things which are nourished, the nourishments are also diverse. And all things indeed must be nourished; yet effort must be given so that those things which are foremost in us are nourished and "grow fat." Therefore, the nourishment of the **rational soul** <term>rationalis animæ: the part of the human soul capable of logic and reason, distinct from the animal soul</term> is that which preserves its rationality, and that is the **mind** <note>original: "mens". In this Platonic context, "mind" or "intellect" refers to the highest, divine part of the human being.</note>. Thus the soul must be nourished by the mind; and one must strive more to fatten the mind itself than to fatten the flesh with food. 

For the mind preserves eternal life for us. But a fattened body causes the soul to suffer from a hunger for the blessed life; it increases our mortal nature, which, being insane, is an impediment to immortal life. Likewise, by "embodying" the soul <note>The process of "incorporation" or "embodying" (incorporans) was viewed by Neoplatonists as the soul becoming too deeply entangled with physical matter, losing its spiritual lightness.</note> and dragging it downward into a nature foreign to it, the body defiles and contaminates it. 

Indeed, a **magnet stone** <note>original: "magnes lapis". The author uses the physical phenomenon of magnetism to illustrate how the soul can be "lifted" by divine influence.</note> transmits its power to iron placed near it; and the iron, which is very heavy, is made light and rises up, lifted by the "spirit" <note>original: "spiritu". Here meaning the invisible force or "breath" of the magnet.</note> of the stone. Truly, will anyone who is suspended toward the incorporeal and intellectual God think much about food—by which the body might grow fat, which is an impediment to the mind? And will he not rather, himself nourished by that which is quite narrow and easily obtained for the necessity of the flesh, cling more to God than iron does to the magnet? For if it were possible that we could also abstain from food even from fruits without trouble, and if our corruptible nature did not require it—for if, as [Homer] says...

<meta>catchword: Homer</meta>

<summary>Porphyry argues that the rational soul should be nourished by the intellect rather than the body by meat, using the metaphor of a magnet lifting heavy iron to describe the soul's ascent toward God.</summary>

## ON ABSTINENCE

**Homer** suggests that we would need neither food nor drink if we were truly immortal. <note>original: "Homerus, neque cibo, neque potu indigeremus..." This refers to the *Iliad* (5.341), where Homer observes that the gods are bloodless and immortal because they do not eat bread or drink wine.</note> It was rightly said by the poet to signify that nourishment brings with it an occasion not so much for living, as for dying. Therefore, if we had no need for such nourishment, we would be as much more blessed as we were more immortal. But now, since we exist in a mortal state, we imprudently render ourselves even more mortal—if one may put it that way—by clinging to it. For the soul, as **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus was a Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle; he was a frequent source for later writers on the ethics of diet.</note> says, does not merely pay a high "rent" for its habitation in the body, but even joins its whole self to it besides. <note>original: "mercedem habitationis". A metaphor comparing the soul's residence in the body to a tenant paying a costly rent to a landlord.</note> 

But would that it were possible to lead the life spoken of in fables, where one lives entirely without hunger or thirst! In such a life, each person, having restrained the **flux of the body** <note>original: "fluxu corporis". In Neoplatonic thought, the physical body was seen as being in a state of constant "flow" or decay, which distracts the soul.</note>, might dwell after the shortest interval among the best things—those things in which God, being present, is truly God. 

Yet why must we deplore these things among men who are so enveloped in darkness that they even cherish their own misfortune? They pursue with hatred first themselves and Him who truly begot them, and then they hate those who remind them [of the truth] and who urge and incite them to sober up from their **drunkenness**. <note>The "drunkenness" or "intoxication" of the soul was a common philosophical metaphor for being overwhelmed by sensory pleasures and forgetting one's divine origin.</note> Therefore, it will perhaps be better to leave these men aside and move on to examine the remaining points. 

For those who oppose the customs of the nations we have described by citing the **Nomads**, or the **Troglodytes**, or the **Fish-eaters**, <term>Ichthyophagos: literally "Fish-eaters," a name given by ancient geographers to various coastal tribes who subsisted entirely on fish.</term> <term>Troglodytas: "Cave-dwellers," typically referring to tribes in Ethiopia or near the Red Sea.</term> they seem to be ignorant that such peoples behave this way because of the infertility of their region, which is so sterile...

<summary>The author argues that physical food is a necessity of mortality and a burden to the soul, citing Homer and Theophrastus to support the idea that the soul pays a heavy price for inhabiting the body. He dismisses those who use meat-eating tribes like the "Fish-eaters" as counter-arguments, noting their diet is born of geographic necessity rather than choice.</summary>

<keywords>Homer, Theophrastus, immortality, soul and body, Fish-eaters, Troglodytes, abstinence, mortal nature</keywords>

<header>BOOK IV.</header>

...beset by cliffs and sands, such that it does not even produce any herbs, [they] were led to this necessity of nourishment <note>The author is likely referring to the *Ichthyophagi* or "Fish-eaters," a people described by ancient geographers like Herodotus and Arrian who lived on the barren coasts of the Erythraean Sea.</note>. The proof of this is that they cannot even use fire due to a lack of fuel and wood; instead, they roast fish upon rocks burning in the sun and upon the sand. Indeed, these people live in such a way because they are compelled by necessity. 

But if some nations are found that are monstrous and savage by nature, it is not fitting for a fair judge of matters to bring a **slander** <note>original: "calumniam"</note> against the entire human race because of them. For in that way, not only the eating of other animals, but even the **eating of humans** <note>original: "hominum quoque esus"; a reference to cannibalism (anthropophagy).</note> and every other kind of ferocity would come into dispute. 

For history records that the **Massagetae** and the **Derbices** <note>Ancient Iranian nomadic groups. The Derbices were said to inhabit the borders of Hyrcania.</note> consider those among their kinsmen to be the most miserable who die a natural death; therefore, they forestall this by slaughtering and eating their own elders. The **Tibareni** <note>A people of ancient Pontus in Asia Minor.</note> cast their nearest relatives from cliffs while they are still living elders. The **Hyrcanians** and **Caspians** expose some people while living, and others when dead, to be devoured by birds and dogs. The **Scythians** bury the living together [with the dead], and above the funeral pyre, they slit the throats of those whom the deceased especially loved. The **Bactrians** throw those who have grown old to the dogs while they are still alive. Indeed, when **Stasanor**, a governor under **Alexander**, attempted to abolish this custom, he nearly lost his command. 

But just as we do not believe that we should strip away our **humanity** <note>original: "humanitatem"</note> toward other men based on the example of these people, so we should not follow...

<meta>This page continues the ethnographic survey of "savage" customs used to argue that human nature should not be judged by its most extreme or desperate examples. It transitions from discussing nations that eat their own kin out of necessity or tradition to the legislative history of Athens, specifically the ancient laws of Triptolemus, to argue for a more "civilized" and pious way of living that includes animal welfare.</meta>

# ON ABSTINENCE

...[the Hyrcanians] <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "The Hyrcanians [abandon their dead to be torn by birds and dogs]..."</note> we ought to disregard those nations that eat meat out of necessity. Instead, we should imitate those who conduct themselves piously and lead a life more devoted to the gods. For **Democrates** <note>likely referring to the Pythagorean philosopher Democrates, rather than the more famous Democritus</note> used to assert that to live badly—and not prudently, temperately, and holily—was not merely "living badly," but rather **dying for a long time**.

It remains for us to bring forward a few testimonies regarding the abstinence of individual men. For this was another point that was being raised as an objection. We have learned that **Triptolemus** <note>A legendary figure in Greek mythology who was taught the art of agriculture by the goddess Demeter and was said to be one of the first legislators of Athens.</note> was the most ancient of the Athenian legislators. Regarding him, **Hermippus** <note>A 3rd-century BCE biographer and historian.</note> writes in the second book of *On the Legislators* in this manner: "They say also that Triptolemus enacted laws for the Athenians." The philosopher **Xenocrates** <note>Head of the Platonic Academy from 339 to 314 BCE.</note> asserts that three of his decrees still exist at **Eleusis** <note>A town near Athens, home to the Eleusinian Mysteries.</note>:

1. **Honor your parents.**
2. **Worship the gods with the fruits of the earth.** <note>original: "fructibus," implying grain and vegetable offerings rather than blood sacrifices.</note>
3. **Do not harm animals.**

Xenocrates says that the first two were indeed rightly established. For we ought to return thanks to our parents in turn, according to our strength, since they have deserved the best from us; and we should offer **first-fruits** to the gods from those things which they have bestowed upon us as useful for life. 

Regarding the third law, he thinks it must be investigated what Triptolemus intended when he commanded abstinence from animals. Was it because he thought it altogether unworthy that humans should kill **kindred beings** <note>original: "cognata"; reflecting the philosophical idea that animals and humans share a common vital spark or "kinship" of the soul.</note>? Or was it because he observed that it happened that the most useful animals were being taken for food by men? Since he wished, therefore, to make the life of men mild, he took care that those which with...

<summary>This page contrasts "savage" meat-eating necessitated by harsh environments with the ancient Athenian laws of Triptolemus, which mandated honoring parents, offering plant-based sacrifices to the gods, and sparing animals.</summary>
<keywords>Triptolemus, Xenocrates, Eleusis, Athenian laws, animal welfare, first-fruits, piety, ethics of eating, Democrates</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page concludes the fourth book of Porphyry’s treatise on abstinence. It finishes the explanation of the third law of Triptolemus—regarding the non-injury of animals—and reinforces the argument for vegetarianism by citing the ancient Athenian laws of Draco, which emphasized offerings of fruit and grain rather than blood sacrifice.</meta>

### BOOK IV. 100

[...and that] animals might live among men, and especially that those which are tame might be preserved <note>original: "hominibus degerent animalia, maxime'q; essent mansueta, conseruarentur." This continues from the previous page's discussion of the third law of Triptolemus: "Do not harm animals."</note>. Unless perhaps he thought that this worship would be more firmly established—since he had commanded that the gods be honored with the fruits of the earth—if sacrifices of animals were not made to the gods at all.

While many other reasons for this decree are offered by **Xenocrates** <note>The head of the Platonic Academy from 339 to 314 BCE.</note>, though they are not particularly refined, it is enough for us that this institution is clearly seen to have originated from **Triptolemus** <note>A legendary figure who taught agriculture to the Athenians.</note>. From there, those who later behaved unjustly, when they turned to the eating of animals out of great necessity and through involuntary sins (as we have shown), fell into a custom of this kind.

For a law of **Draco** <note>The first recorded legislator of Athens (c. 621 BCE), known for the severity of his legal code.</note> is also recorded in memory in this way: "An eternal law for those inhabiting **Attica** <note>The region of Greece surrounding the city of Athens.</note>, which shall be held as valid for all time: The gods and native heroes are to be venerated in public by those following the laws of the fathers, and in private according to one's means, with auspicious words, and with the **first fruits** <term>first fruits: the initial harvest of the season offered as a religious tribute</term>, and annual cakes." Here, the law commands that the divine power be worshipped with the first fruits of those crops upon which man feeds, and with cakes.

The author argues that animals share a biological or spiritual kinship with humans, making their slaughter a form of fratricide.</note>?
